**Summary.** This work introduces a novel, efficient algorithm within the Stochastic Series Expansion framework to compute imaginary-time correlation functions for both diagonal and off-diagonal operators. By implementing discrete imaginary-time slicing, the method overcomes previous computational bottlenecks associated with measuring non-local correlations in spin models like the TFIM and XXZ chain. This advancement significantly broadens the scope of quantum simulations for strongly correlated matter.

**Why it may be interesting.** The ability to efficiently measure off-diagonal correlation functions in strongly correlated spin systems is crucial for understanding quantum phase transitions and excitations (like spinons) that are often masked by diagonal measurements.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to develop an efficient, general algorithm for calculating imaginary-time correlation functions in Quantum Monte Carlo simulations.

**Main result.** The authors present a method using discrete imaginary-time slicing of the SSE operator string that allows for the calculation of both diagonal and off-diagonal correlators with minimal overhead.

**Method.** The technique involves splitting the inverse temperature $eta$ into $m$ time slices ($\Delta	au = eta/m$) within the Stochastic Series Expansion (SSE) framework. This slicing enables the direct estimation of correlation functions at discrete imaginary times $	au_k$.

**Model / system.** The method is benchmarked on spin systems, specifically the one-dimensional transverse-field Ising model (TFIM) and the XXZ Heisenberg chain. These models are used to test the algorithm's applicability across different types of operators.

**Key observables.** Imaginary-time correlation functions ($G(	au)$), including both diagonal and off-diagonal spectral functions, which can be analytically continued to real frequencies.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Inverse temperature ($eta$), number of time slices ($m$), system size ($L$), and the specific coupling constants defining the Hamiltonian (e.g., $\Delta$ for XXZ).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes that discretizing imaginary time into $m$ slices provides a representation accurate enough to avoid significant discretization errors, while maintaining the ergodicity of existing cluster update schemes.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the implementation details: one shows the directed-loop update for XXZ chains, and another demonstrates the TFIM cluster update mechanism used within the SSE framework.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the challenge of calculating off-diagonal correlators in QMC. It then introduces the core technique—time-slicing the SSE operator string—and derives estimators for both diagonal and off-diagonal operators. Finally, it validates this general algorithm on established models like TFIM and XXZ chains.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Combined with numerical analytic continuation techniques, quantum Monte Carlo (QMC) methods enable the extraction of real-frequency dynamical properties from imaginary-time correlation functions. However, the efficient computation of imaginary-time correlation functions by QMC simulations can (depending on the particular model used) be challenging, particularly for operators that are off-diagonal in the computational basis. In this work, we present an efficient and general algorithm within the stochastic series expansion (SSE) framework for evaluating imaginary-time correlation functions of both diagonal and off-diagonal operators. The algorithm builds on a discrete imaginary-time slicing of the SSE operator string, which provides correlation functions on a grid of well-defined imaginary-time points with no discretization error. For off-diagonal operators, we derive estimators that integrate directly into the existing SSE directed-loop or cluster updating schemes, introducing only minimal computational overhead. We benchmark the method on the one-dimensional transverse-field Ising model (sampling with cluster updates) and XXZ spin chain (using directed-loop sampling), demonstrating excellent agreement (with only statistical errors) with exact diagonalization of small systems. We also study larger systems to demonstrate efficiency.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes the two-atom Dicke model to study how internal atomic interactions modify superradiant phase transitions. By incorporating dissipation and analyzing the Liouvillian spectrum, the authors demonstrate that these interactions can fundamentally alter critical thresholds and change the universality class of the transition. The results highlight the tunability of collective quantum phenomena through engineered coupling terms.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a detailed theoretical framework for controlling collective light-matter interactions in cavity QED systems by engineering atom-atom coupling, which is crucial for designing quantum memories or nonlinear optical devices.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how atom-atom interactions modify superradiant criticality in a minimal cavity QED model, particularly concerning phase boundaries and the universality class of the transition.

**Main result.** Interaction terms can suppress the superradiance threshold entirely, causing the critical coupling strength to vanish at specific interaction ratios (e.g., $J=2\delta$), and changing the critical scaling exponent from $\gamma=1$ to $\gamma=2$.

**Method.** The analysis employs both analytical techniques like perturbation theory for phase boundaries and numerical methods involving solving the Liouvillian spectrum and analyzing relaxation dynamics via the Master equation.

**Model / system.** The system is a Dicke model consisting of two interacting two-level atoms coupled to a single cavity mode. The physics is governed by a Hamiltonian analyzed through a Master equation that includes terms for photon loss ($\kappa$) and local spin relaxation ($\gamma_{\downarrow}$).

**Key observables.** Critical coupling strength ($	ilde{g}_c$), steady-state photon number ($n_{ss}$), two-spin concurrence ($C$), Liouvillian gap ($\Delta_L$), and the scaling exponent ($\gamma$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Atom-atom interaction strength ($J$), cavity frequency ($\omega$), coupling constant ($g$), dissipation rates ($\kappa, \gamma_{\downarrow}$), and the ratio $J/\delta$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis often involves projecting onto reduced subspaces (e.g., ignoring the singlet state) or utilizing approximations like the classical oscillator limit for analytical tractability.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the bare spin spectrum, phase boundaries in the $(	ilde{g}/J/\delta)$ plane showing Normal/Superradiant phases, and the evolution of photon number and populations under cavity loss, alongside plots of Liouvillian eigenvalues.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the minimal model Hamiltonian to analyzing the closed-system phase transitions using perturbation theory. It then extends the analysis to include dissipation via the Master equation, examining steady states, relaxation dynamics (Liouvillian spectrum), and finally deriving stability criteria for different interaction regimes.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Interactions among emitters provide a powerful means of controlling collective light--matter phenomena, yet their role in superradiant criticality has not been thoroughly investigated. Here we construct a minimal model that can yield analytical insights --- a Dicke model with two interacting atoms coupled to a single mode cavity --- to study such interaction effects. We show that interaction changes the phase boundary, and may even completely suppress the atom-photon coupling threshold for superradiance and change the universality class of the phase transition. We further study the dissipative phase transition and quantum dynamics in the presence of dissipation channels such as photon loss and spin relaxation. Our results demonstrate important roles played by the atom-atom interaction and how it can be engineered to control atom-photon coupling.

</details>

**Summary.** The research presents an experimental demonstration of integrating squeezed-light generation and detection onto one photonic chip using heterogeneous integration. By overcoming material conflicts between low-loss transmission and high-absorption detection, the authors achieved 3 dB squeezing with a high total quantum efficiency. This establishes a scalable platform for advanced quantum photonic applications.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses hardware bottlenecks in quantum optics by demonstrating a fully integrated platform for continuous-variable quantum state generation and measurement, which is crucial for scalable quantum networking and computation.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Integrating squeezed-light generation and photodetection on a single photonic chip is challenging due to conflicting material requirements: low loss for quantum correlations versus efficient absorption for detection.

**Main result.** The authors successfully demonstrated a scalable architecture by integrating all three stages (generation, routing, and detection) on one chip, achieving measurable 3 dB squeezing with low overall circuit loss.

**Method.** They used heterogeneous integration to combine components like Kerr microcavities for squeezing, tunable filters for routing, and modified uni-traveling carrier photodiodes for balanced homodyne detection.

**Model / system.** The system is a single photonic chip built on SiN, incorporating an Optical Parametric Oscillator (OPO) pumped by a continuous wave laser to generate two-mode squeezed light. Detection relies on heterogeneously integrated MUTC photodiodes.

**Key observables.** Squeezing level ($\approx 3 	ext{ dB}$), total quantum efficiency ($72\%$), and quadrature noise variances relative to shot noise.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Total optical loss of $1.1 	ext{ dB}$ post-squeezer, pump power near OPO threshold, and the measured MMI splitting ratio ($46/54$ vs designed).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary limitation noted is that squeezing level is limited by component non-idealities like squeezer escape efficiency and residual loss.

**Figures summary.** Figures show architectural diagrams, sideviews of photodiodes, characterization spectra (transmission/mode), noise spectrum validation confirming sub-shot-noise operation, and total quantum efficiency measurements.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the architecture, presents component characterizations (loss, QE), demonstrates the measurement of quadrature noise variances to quantify squeezing below the shot noise limit, and discusses the scalability of the integrated platform.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Squeezed light underpins quantum-enhanced sensing and continuous-variable quantum information processing, and integrated photonics offers a route to producing it at scale. Universal to these applications are squeezed-light generation and measurement. Importantly, quantum measurements serve not only as readout but also as active operations in quantum-state evolution. However, integrating squeezed-light generation and photodetection on the same photonic chip has remained challenging because they impose fundamentally conflicting material requirements: low optical loss to preserve quantum correlations, but efficient photon absorption for photodetection. Here, we demonstrate squeezed-light generation, routing, and balanced homodyne detection integrated on a single photonic chip through heterogeneous integration. A two-mode squeezed quantum microcomb comprising 34 quantum modes is measured with approximately 3 dB squeezing. Our work establishes a scalable architecture for fully integrated squeezed-light quantum photonic systems, unifying quantum-state generation, processing, and detection on a single chip.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper presents CAMERA, a novel method to encode Circuit-SAT into an MWIS problem on Rydberg atom arrays using compact logic gadgets. By avoiding the massive overhead of CNF representations, it drastically reduces the required number of atoms while demonstrating end-to-end simulation capability for complex arithmetic circuits.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete, resource-aware blueprint for mapping abstract computational problems onto the physical constraints of Rydberg arrays, directly addressing scalability issues inherent in previous encoding schemes.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to encode the NP-complete Boolean Circuit Satisfiability problem (Circuit-SAT) onto a physical quantum hardware platform.

**Main result.** A novel, atom-efficient encoding method called CAMERA was introduced, which reduces the required number of atoms for Circuit-SAT by an average factor of 22.4 compared to conventional CNF encodings.

**Method.** The authors developed a gadget-based approach that maps logic gates directly into weighted gadgets on the king subgraph geometry of Rydberg arrays, bypassing the overhead associated with Conjunctive Normal Form (CNF) representations.

**Model / system.** The physical platform is Rydberg atom arrays, which naturally encode the Maximum-Weight Independent Set (MWIS) problem via the blockade mechanism. The system's Hamiltonian governs interactions that restrict excitations to independent sets on a graph structure like the king subgraph.

**Key observables.** Maximum Weight ($W^\star$) of the MWIS, which corresponds to circuit functionality; port occupations read out from the ground state solution.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The atom cost reduction factor ($\approx 22.4$); the blockade radius ($R_b$); and the weights assigned to gadgets (e.g., $\{1, 2\}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The work establishes a proof of principle for the complete encoding and simulation workflow; verification relies on exact classical ground state calculations (dynamic programming) for small instances.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the workflow from circuit check to gadget assembly and final mapping onto the array. Specific tables summarize the MWIS weights achieved for verifying arithmetic blocks like the full adder ($W^\star=73$) and two-bit multiplier ($W^\star=142$).

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the problem (Circuit-SAT $ightarrow$ MWIS) and the limitations of CNF encoding. It then details CAMERA, presenting the gadget construction and VLSI-inspired compilation. The core is validation through compiling and solving increasingly complex circuits: three-gate circuit $ightarrow$ full adder $ightarrow$ two-bit multiplier, culminating in an end-to-end simulation.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Rydberg atom arrays natively encode the maximum-weight independent set (MWIS) problem through the blockade mechanism, so the Boolean circuit satisfiability problem (Circuit-SAT) can be brought onto the platform once it is reduced to MWIS. The conventional encoding of Circuit-SAT in the Rydberg atom array proceeds through conjunctive normal form (CNF) and incurs a substantial atom overhead. We introduce CAMERA (Circuit-SAT Atom-efficient MWIS Encoding for Rydberg Arrays), a method that provides MWIS encodings of Circuit-SAT instances on the king subgraph geometry of the array. CAMERA represents each logic gate as a compact weighted gadget and assembles the gadgets with a placement and routing compiler inspired by very large scale integration (VLSI) design. On random multi-gate benchmarks, the direct encoding route lowers the atom cost relative to the CNF route by an average factor of $22.4 \pm 1.8$. To demonstrate that the encoding extends from individual weighted gadgets to multi-gate arithmetic blocks, we compile a full adder and a multiplier, verifying each against its complete truth table by exact classical ground state calculations. We further showcase solving a representative Circuit-SAT instance end-to-end, from gate level compilation through a closed-system tensor-network simulation of a hardware-compatible annealing protocol on the encoded 30-atom instance to readout of a satisfying assignment. These results establish a complete encoding and simulation workflow as a proof of principle, and a concrete route toward solving a broader family of combinatorial problems on Rydberg atom arrays.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper investigates how topology behaves in dissipative superconducting chains by analyzing the Kitaev model under Lindblad dynamics. It reveals that while local measurements can be 'sector-blind' due to cross-talk between Majorana sectors, nonlocal diagnostics like the entanglement spectrum zero events successfully reveal the underlying, hidden open-boundary topological structure.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work bridges non-Hermitian physics (dissipation) with topological invariants and entanglement measures, providing advanced diagnostics for open quantum systems where standard bulk-boundary correspondence fails. This is highly relevant to AMO/open system research.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how non-Bloch topology manifests and breaks down in dissipative topological superconductors, particularly when multiple Majorana sectors are present.

**Main result.** The system exhibits a sector-resolved non-Bloch bulk-boundary correspondence at zero chemical potential, which is masked by local observables due to cross-sector covariance. This hidden edge content can be recovered dynamically via finite-time zero events in the entanglement spectrum.

**Method.** The analysis employs third quantization on a bond-dissipative Kitaev chain, analyzing the Liouvillian spectrum and utilizing the spatial entanglement spectrum (ES) dynamics under quenches.

**Model / system.** The model is a bond-dissipative dimerized Kitaev chain governed by a Lindblad master equation. The Hamiltonian includes hopping terms ($t_{1a}, t_{2b}$) and onsite potentials, alongside dissipation rates ($\gamma_\ell, \gamma_g$).

**Key observables.** Non-Bloch winding numbers ($
u$), local density relaxation (cross-sector covariance), and finite-time zero events in the entanglement spectrum ($\xi_\ell = 1/2$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Zero chemical potential ($\mu=0$) is crucial for sector separation; finite $\mu$ causes hybridization. The ratio of hopping parameters ($\Delta/t$) controls the topological regime.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The exact sector decomposition into two independent non-Hermitian sectors is only valid at $\mu=0$. For $\mu 
eq 0$, the analysis must use the full rapidity matrix due to hybridization.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the schematic of the chain structure and sector split ($\mu=0$), map out non-Bloch winding numbers vs. coupling ratios, show observable selection rules (e.g., $	ext{G}_{14}$ decay), and demonstrate ES zero events signaling hidden OBC topology.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the general framework of dissipative non-Hermitian topology. It then analyzes the specific case at $\mu=0$ to reveal sector-resolved diagnostics. Finally, it extends the analysis to finite chemical potential ($\mu 
eq 0$), showing how hybridization complicates the simple sector separation but that total ES dynamics remain a robust diagnostic tool.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

A core characteristic of dissipative non-Hermitian topology is that the relaxation dynamics tracks the non-Bloch bulk-boundary correspondence, rendering an algebraic decay in the gapless regime and an exponential falloff in the gapped phase, so that local observables directly diagnose the topology. We show that this correspondence breaks down in a dissipative topological superconductor, where the local observables turn blind to the very topology they are expected to decipher. Via a bond-dissipative dimerized Kitaev chain in a third-quantized rapidity-matrix formulation, we find that at zero chemical potential the Majorana rapidity matrix decomposes into two independent non-Hermitian sectors, each with its own generalized Brillouin zone and non-Bloch winding number, thereby revealing a sector-resolved non-Bloch bulk-boundary correspondence. The local density is a cross-sector covariance and relaxes at the sum of the two sector rates, so it remains sector-blind even when one sector is gapless and topological. For balanced gain and loss, the finite-time zero events of the entanglement spectrum under purely periodic-boundary Lindblad evolution recover this hidden edge content sector by sector, serving as a dynamical invariant that returns the open-boundary edge rapidities without physically opening the chain.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors introduce an activated-mode principle to universally describe the critical threshold where non-Hermitian spectra transition from real to complex values. By identifying a minimal 'activated' subspace pair, they derive a closed-form condition that governs this onset across diverse physical models. This framework successfully predicts crossover regimes and channel switching without relying on specific symmetries or perturbative assumptions.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, generalized analytical tool for understanding spectral singularities (like EPs) that govern system stability or amplification onset in open quantum systems, applicable far beyond simple perturbation theory.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper develops a general, non-perturbative activated-mode principle to govern the critical threshold for real-to-complex spectral transitions in non-Hermitian systems.

**Main result.** It establishes a unified, closed-form condition ($\lambda_c = \Delta_{	ext{act}} / G_{	ext{act}}$) that determines the transition onset by finding the minimum threshold across all possible active Hilbert subspaces.

**Method.** The theory involves pair-resolved analysis of the exceptional point (EP) condition on a small, 'activated' subspace, avoiding perturbative expansions in system parameters.

**Model / system.** The framework is applied to broad classes of non-Hermitian systems, including coupled Hatano-Nelson chains, topological SSH models with gain/loss, and impurity-modified lattices.

**Key observables.** Critical coupling strength ($\lambda_c$), spectral detuning ($\Delta E_{ij}$), and projected activation strength ($G_{	ext{act}}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** System size (L), impurity strength ($\mu$), and the ratio of hopping parameters in SSH models.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The theory assumes that the transition is governed by a small, variationally determined active subspace pair, and $H_0$ is diagonalizable in the spectral window.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the activated mode mechanism (EP competition), show the agreement between derived closed-form thresholds and numerical results for cNHSE models, and track how the 'winning' active channel switches as a function of external parameters ($\mu$).

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from establishing the general non-perturbative principle to applying it sequentially to specific physical realizations: first, generic NHSE systems; second, impurity-modified OBC/PBC setups; and finally, analyzing complex switching behavior in SSH chains.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Real-to-complex spectral transitions mark the onset of amplification in non-Hermitian systems, but their thresholds are often treated as model-specific quantities. Here we develop a general, non-perturbative activated-mode principle that governs the real-to-complex threshold across broad classes of non-Hermitian systems. A central insight is that only a small Hilbert subspace is ``activated" at the transition onset, which can be variationally determined through the competition between spectral detuning and mode-level projected non-Hermitian couplings. The result is a closed-form exceptional-activation condition for arbitrarily large ``disturbances", rather than a perturbative estimate. We apply our framework to three contrasting illustrative problems, establishing (i) a closed-form threshold for critical non-Hermitian skin amplification at \emph{all} system sizes; (ii) a new link between impurity tunneling threshold and exceptional point switching; and (iii) activation channel switching without underlying topological phase transition. Overall, our findings recast real-to-complex transitions as generic mode-selection problems independent of any specific symmetry.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper reports the first experimental demonstration of simulating nonlinear time evolution using a superconducting quantum processor. By developing a variational framework that embeds nonlinearity directly into optimization circuits, they successfully simulated the Burgers equation across multiple timesteps. This work significantly expands the scope of quantum simulation beyond purely linear systems toward complex continuum dynamics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly pushes the boundaries of what is computationally feasible on near-term quantum hardware by tackling non-Hamiltonian dynamics, which is a major frontier in quantum computation theory.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core challenge is extending quantum simulation from linear, unitary dynamics to nonlinear and non-Hermitian evolution required for simulating continuum physics like fluid flow.

**Main result.** The authors achieved the first experimental realization of time propagation for nonlinear dynamics (Burgers equation) on a quantum processor, successfully demonstrating stable evolution across multiple timesteps despite hardware noise.

**Method.** They employ a hybrid variational framework that encodes nonlinearity directly into a quantum circuit optimization procedure, bypassing traditional linearization techniques like Carlemanization.

**Model / system.** The system modeled is nonlinear fluid dynamics, specifically the viscous and inviscid Burgers equations. The computation is performed on superconducting quantum processors using parametrized quantum circuits.

**Key observables.** Mean-Squared Error (MSE) comparing reconstructed dynamics to ideal evolution; state-overlap fidelity; time-dependent field reconstruction ($f(x, t)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Reynolds numbers of order $10^2$; circuit depth up to 60 controlled-Z layers; viscous parameter $
u=0.01$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The approach assumes the validity of specific noise models (e.g., global depolarizing channel) for error mitigation techniques like Hadamard Verification.

**Figures summary.** Figures summarize the reconstructed dynamics showing shock formation and gradient sharpening in both viscous and inviscid regimes, alongside comparisons of optimization methods (SGEO vs COBYLA) and error mitigation protocols.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem gap (nonlinearity incompatibility), details the variational framework for time evolution, presents experimental results on superconducting hardware using advanced error mitigation techniques, and concludes by establishing a pathway for quantum simulation beyond linear settings.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

From fluid flow and transport to collective dynamics, numerical simulation of nonlinear partial differential equations underpins modern scientific computing. Extending this capability to quantum computers remains a longstanding challenge because nonlinear and non-Hermitian evolution is fundamentally incompatible with conventional Hamiltonian-based quantum simulation. Here we experimentally realize the time evolution of nonlinear fluid dynamics on a quantum processor using a hybrid variational framework for the viscous and inviscid Burgers equations. Our approach directly encodes the nonlinear dynamics into a variational optimization procedure, avoiding the enlarged linear embeddings and truncation overhead associated with Carleman linearization-based quantum algorithms. We further demonstrate convection-dominated dynamics corresponding to Reynolds numbers of order $10^2$. We encode the governing evolution into parametrized quantum circuits and iteratively reconstruct the time-dependent field through quantum-classical optimization. By introducing a zero-noise extrapolation method without additional circuit-folding overhead, we accurately execute deep error-circuits with entangling-gate counts beyond those typical of Hadamard test circuits. We accurately reconstruct the time evolution across multiple timesteps despite hardware noise and finite device coherence. Our results constitute, to our knowledge, the first experimental realization of nonlinear time propagation on a quantum processor, extending quantum simulation beyond predominantly linear settings and establishing a route toward quantum computation for nonlinear continuum dynamics.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents an advanced, unified quantum theory to model and predict the temporal correlations generated by DLCZ Raman photon sources used in quantum networks. By combining Heisenberg-Langevin dynamics with Maxwell-Schrödinger propagation, the authors experimentally validate that correlation engineering can be achieved through controlled retrieval tuning. This establishes a robust quantitative framework for generating high-quality entangled photons on demand.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it tackles open-system dynamics in a platform critical for quantum networking—the DLCZ source. The combination of full propagation effects with time-resolved correlation measurement provides advanced tools for controlling photonic resources.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is developing a unified, predictive theory for time-resolved correlation engineering in DLCZ Raman photon sources that accounts for realistic propagation and noise.

**Main result.** The study establishes a quantitative framework showing that classically controlled retrieval allows temporal gating of the anti-Stokes wavepacket, confirming robust correlations achievable through careful tuning.

**Method.** The authors developed a propagation-inclusive open-system quantum theory combining Heisenberg-Langevin dynamics with Maxwell-Schrödinger propagation equations.

**Model / system.** The system is based on the Duan–Lukin–Cirac–Zoller (DLCZ) protocol using spontaneous Raman scattering in atomic ensembles to generate entangled photon pairs for memory-assisted quantum networks.

**Key observables.** Time-dependent Stokes generation, spin-wave evolution, retrieved anti-Stokes wavepackets, and time-resolved cross-correlations (two-photon correlations).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Short write pulses enhance correlations; the theory is validated across different tuning ranges.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The theory improves upon previous work by retaining write-driven population redistribution and combining propagation effects into the open-system framework.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically illustrates the three stages of operation: WRITE (driving pulse $\Omega_d$, Stokes emission creating spin-wave), STORAGE, and READ (coupling pulse $\Omega_c$ mapping spin-wave to anti-Stokes field).

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the physical problem, develops the combined quantum theory using HLEs and MSEs, analyzes the dynamics in specific regimes (like large detuning), presents experimental validation of key predictions, and concludes by establishing the correlation engineering framework.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Memory-assisted quantum networks require photon sources with controllable temporal and correlation properties. The Duan-Lukin-Cirac-Zoller (DLCZ) protocol provides a platform based on spontaneous Raman scattering in atomic ensembles, but a unified predictive theory connecting control parameters to correlations under realistic propagation and noise conditions remains lacking. Here we present a propagation-inclusive open-system quantum theory that retains write-induced population redistribution while combining Heisenberg-Langevin dynamics with Maxwell-Schrödinger propagation. We experimentally validate its key predictions. The theory predicts time-dependent Stokes generation, spin-wave evolution, retrieved anti-Stokes wavepackets, and time-resolved cross-correlations. Experiments confirm robust correlations under retrieval tuning and enhanced correlations for shorter write pulses, consistent with the different scaling of correlated coincidences and accidental backgrounds with the mean spin-wave excitation number. Classically controlled retrieval enables temporal gating and slicing of the anti-Stokes wavepacket, establishing a quantitative framework for correlation engineering in memory-compatible DLCZ photon sources.

</details>

**Summary.** This work introduces an In-situ Adjoint Optimization Protocol (iNAP) to enable real-time gradient-based optimization in complex nonlinear wave systems. By physically performing both forward and adjoint propagations on hardware, the method allows researchers to adaptively control devices like wireless communications components despite inherent multipath complexity and nonlinearity. This bypasses major computational bottlenecks associated with traditional system modeling.

**Why it may be interesting.** The development of an *in-situ* optimization protocol that bypasses the need for full system modeling or digital twins is highly relevant to quantum optics and open quantum systems, as it provides a pathway for adaptive control in complex, noisy physical implementations.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research aims to overcome limitations in wave control by harnessing multipath complexity and nonlinearity, which are typically seen as impediments.

**Main result.** They successfully demonstrate an in-situ adjoint optimization protocol (iNAP) that allows for real-time gradient calculation in complex nonlinear physical systems without needing digital twins or conventional backpropagation.

**Method.** The core method is the In-situ Adjoint Optimization Protocol (iNAP), which involves sequential forward and adjoint measurements performed directly on the physical hardware to calculate parameter sensitivities.

**Model / system.** The system utilizes a minimal platform composed of incommensurate coaxial cables forming a wave-chaotic multipath network, incorporating a single localized nonlinear defect. The analysis also details deriving a self-consistency equation for the resonator intensity by integrating out linear graph degrees of freedom.

**Key observables.** Objective function gradient sensitivities ($\partial g / \partial p$), field measurements ($\Psi$, $\zeta$), and the resonant intensity ($y$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The nonlinearity strength, input power levels (e.g., -50 to 10 dBm), and system parameters $p$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** For iNAP, a crucial assumption is enforcing field invariance under phase conjugation at the nonlinear defect to ensure adjoint dynamics match hardware emulation. For modeling, invertibility of the linear graph matrix ($\mathbf{A}^{-1}_g$) is assumed.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the four-step physical measurement process for iNAP (Forward $ightarrow$ Adjoint $ightarrow$ Evaluation $ightarrow$ Gradient). Figure S1 shows frequency-resolved scattering response $|S_{ij}|^2$ versus input power for a measured nonlinear vertex.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the theoretical need to harness nonlinearity/multipath, detailing the iNAP protocol using adjoint fields in hardware (Chunk 1 & 2), deriving a compact scalar self-consistency equation by integrating out linear graph modes (Chunk 3), and finally characterizing a physical nonlinear vertex using this framework (Chunk 4).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Complex multipath environments are usually avoided in wave-based information processing because repeated scattering creates many interfering propagation paths, obscuring controllability and generating extreme sensitivity to perturbations. The addition of nonlinear mechanisms fundamentally alters the wave-control landscape by breaking the superposition principle that underpins most wave-management strategies. Here, we show that these two apparent impediments -- multipath complexity and nonlinearity -- can instead be harnessed as key resources for physical optimization. We demonstrate an in-situ adjoint optimization protocol in a wave-chaotic platform incorporating a single localized nonlinear defect, in which the system itself performs both the forward and the adjoint propagations required for gradient evaluation. Recurrent multipath returns repeatedly expose the wave to the defect, producing from a minimal hardware a rich nonlinear input-output map with many pathway-mediated degrees of freedom. At the same time, a suitable adjoint excitation enables direct extraction of the sensitivities from measurements alone, without a digital twin or conventional numerical backpropagation. We experimentally validate the protocol on a minimal nonlinear multipath platform composed of incommensurate coaxial cables connected via T-junctions, one of which hosts a diode-loaded cavity. Our approach opens a route to adaptive wireless communications, imaging and analog intelligence in complex, partially unknown environments where conventional modeling is impractical.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper proposes a novel, high-density quantum computing architecture combining nanomechanical resonators and solid-state spin defects. By using spins as nonlinear couplers, the system can encode information in bosonic cat states, enabling universal quantum gates. This approach promises scalability by achieving small qubit footprints while maintaining long coherence times necessary for error correction.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete blueprint for integrating solid-state quantum memories (spins) with scalable bosonic qubits (resonators), offering an alternative pathway to fault-tolerant computation that addresses density limitations seen in superconducting circuits.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Developing a scalable quantum computing platform using bosonic encoding that overcomes the resource intensity and low spatial density of current methods.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrated high-fidelity single-qubit gates (X, Z) and two-qubit entangling operations with fidelities up to 0.98 in a solid-state spin-resonator architecture.

**Method.** The work combines theoretical modeling of the Hamiltonian with numerical simulations (Lindblad/QMC) to demonstrate gate sequences and error correction protocols.

**Model / system.** The platform uses nanomechanical resonators for bosonic encoding (cat states) coupled to strain-sensitive solid-state spin defects. The interaction is modeled via dispersive coupling, allowing for controlled quantum operations.

**Key observables.** Cat state fidelity (>0.98), single-shot optical parity readout fidelity (>0.95), and gate fidelities ($X_L, Z_L$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The system leverages the small footprint ($\sim 10 \mu	ext{m}^2$) compared to other codes, and long coherence times from diamond spins are key.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The architecture relies on piezoelectric driving for population, and gate fidelity is limited by photon loss or quasi-static noise in certain protocols.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the device structure, unit cell coupling, cat state preparation/readout schemes (using spin ancillae), and detailed sequences for single-qubit gates ($X_L$ via unitary evolution) and parity readout.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the physical system and Hamiltonian to demonstrating cat state initialization, followed by protocols for single-qubit gates, two-qubit entanglement generation, and finally addressing noise mitigation through dynamical decoupling techniques.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Nanomechanical structures have been investigated as a method of achieving long-lived quantum excitations at radio frequencies. Their high quality factors are especially intriguing as a medium for bosonic encoding of quantum information. However, to leading order, mechanical modes typically lack the nonlinearities necessary to achieve interaction between bosonic channels and thus are limited in their ability to scale to the many-qubit regime necessary for practical quantum computing. In this work, we propose and describe an approach for bosonic quantum information processing that uses strain-sensitive solid-state spins as nonlinear elements to produce the relevant nonclassical mechanical states. We outline the architecture required to achieve nearest-neighbor connectivity between mechanical cat-state qubits on-chip, as well as the control and readout architecture required for universal quantum computation. In addition, we show that this architecture can allow for a high spatial density of logical qubits by leveraging both the efficiency of bosonic error correction schemes and the small sizes of the constituent nanomechanical resonators and spin qubits. Finally, we identify the necessary performance metrics that will enable error-correction thresholds at high qubit densities, illuminating a path towards scalable quantum information processing.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper presents an experimental realization of a continuously operating microwave single-photon detector using a superconducting artificial molecule. By engineering the system dynamics to transfer absorbed photons into a continuously monitored state, they overcome the efficiency vs. duty cycle trade-off inherent in prior detectors. This breakthrough enables continuous quantum measurement crucial for quantum sensing and thermodynamics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant to AMO physics as it uses superconducting circuits—a platform for engineered quantum matter—to solve a fundamental problem in quantum optics: efficient, continuous single-photon detection.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge addressed is developing a microwave single-photon detector that achieves high efficiency, low dark counts, and continuous operation simultaneously, overcoming limitations of existing cyclic detectors.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrated a continuously operated detector with a measured efficiency of 0.47, successfully establishing a method for continuous microwave photon detection in quantum technologies.

**Method.** The experiment involves characterizing the system's response using both pulsed (cyclic) and continuous readout protocols, analyzing quantum jumps and signal-to-noise ratios.

**Model / system.** The physical platform is a superconducting artificial molecule built from two coupled transmon qubits. The dynamics are modeled as a four-level system involving symmetric ($	ext{S}$) and anti-symmetric ($	ext{A}$) modes, driven by photon absorption into the $	ext{S}$ mode and monitored via continuous readout of the $	ext{A}$ mode.

**Key observables.** Continuous detection efficiency ($\eta_{	ext{cont}} = 0.47$), dark count rate ($1.3 	ext{ kHz}$), temporal resolution ($1\ \mu	ext{s}$), and dead time ($15\ \mu	ext{s}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Operating temperature of $8 	ext{ mK}$; coupling rate $\Gamma_s/2\pi = 7.26 	ext{ MHz}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The continuous efficiency is modeled as a product of the intrinsic absorption efficiency ($\eta_{	ext{abs}}$) and the readout fidelity factor ($F_{	ext{cont}}$), which are both dependent on measurement strength.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the operating principle, spectroscopy of the symmetric mode, dispersive readout of the anti-symmetric mode, and the photon transfer mechanism from $	ext{S}$ to $	ext{A}$.

**Paper structure.** The work progresses by first characterizing the system modes (spectroscopy), then demonstrating pulsed detection efficiency ($\eta_{	ext{cyc}}$), and finally establishing continuous operation by modeling the trade-off between absorption and readout fidelity.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Efficient detection of single microwave photons is a key capability for emerging quantum technologies. Yet, it remains far less developed than its optical domain counterpart. Realizing detectors that simultaneously achieve high efficiency, low dark counts, and continuous operation has proved challenging. Existing detectors operate cyclically, forcing a trade-off between efficiency and duty cycle. Here, we demonstrate a continuously operated microwave single-photon detector based on a superconducting artificial molecule. In our scheme, an incoming photon is captured by a bright state of the molecule and then transferred to a long-lived dark state via a driven-dissipative process. Photon ``clicks'' are revealed as quantum jumps in the continuously monitored dark state. We observe a cyclic detection efficiency of $0.73$, and a continuous detection efficiency of $0.47$ over a $5\,\mathrm{MHz}$ instantaneous bandwidth, with a $1\,μ\mathrm{s}$ temporal resolution and a $15\,μ\mathrm{s}$ dead time. By overcoming the trade-off between efficiency and duty cycle, this approach establishes continuous microwave photon detection for quantum sensing, quantum thermodynamics, and fundamental physics.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper provides a rigorous comparison of theoretical methods—specifically the Floquet-Redfield equation versus the master equation—for modeling heat transport in quantum systems subjected to periodic driving. By analyzing a driven spin-boson model, the authors demonstrate that advanced techniques are necessary to capture non-trivial features like multi-photon resonances in the heat current spectrum, which simpler approximations miss.

**Why it may be interesting.** This is highly relevant as it tackles non-equilibrium quantum thermodynamics in periodically driven systems, a key area where standard approximations often fail to capture resonant or multi-photon effects crucial for understanding energy harvesting and thermal machines.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study compares different theoretical frameworks, specifically the Floquet-Redfield equation versus the full master equation approach, for accurately calculating heat transport in periodically driven quantum systems.

**Main result.** The non-secular Floquet-Redfield approach correctly predicts multi-photon resonances in the heat current spectrum, outperforming simpler secular approximations, especially near resonance points.

**Method.** The authors employ projection operator techniques to derive generalized master equations, which are then analyzed by projecting onto the basis of Floquet states for periodic driving.

**Model / system.** The work analyzes a general open quantum system, exemplified by a driven spin-boson model (a qubit coupled to a bosonic heat bath). The dynamics are governed by Hamiltonians involving time-periodic terms and coupling to an environment characterized by spectral densities.

**Key observables.** Heat current ($P_b(t)$), steady-state populations, coherences ($	ilde{ho}_{nm}(t)$), and the power spectrum of bath fluctuations $S(\omega)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Drive frequency ($\omega_d$), coupling strength (weak coupling limit), spectral density function parameters (e.g., ohmic spectral density).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis develops from exact weak-coupling results, comparing them against approximations like the Markovian or full secular approximations; discrepancies are noted when using the instantaneous eigenbasis approach outside the adiabatic regime.

**Figures summary.** Figures likely compare calculated heat current spectra (e.g., showing multi-photon peaks) derived from different theoretical limits (Floquet-Redfield vs. Secular Master Equation).

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the general open quantum system dynamics using a master equation, projects this onto the Floquet basis for periodic driving, and then systematically compares results obtained via the full Floquet-Redfield formalism against simplified approximations like the secular or Markovian limits.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We provide a comprehensive study of heat transport in periodically driven quantum systems using a combination of master equation and Floquet approach. We give exact results (within the weak coupling Redfield theory, with no Markovian or secular approximations) and compare them with alternative approaches involving further approximations. Numerical and analytical results obtained in their appropriate driving regimes are provided for the driven spin boson-model. The adiabatic regime, which is relevant to thermal machines, is discussed.

</details>

</div>

**Summary.** This work unifies the theory of neural network training by deriving the Neural Quadratic Form (NQF) based on permutation symmetry. It demonstrates that diverse architectures map onto this single model, whose dynamics are governed by solvable equations like Lotka–Volterra systems. This provides a powerful theoretical tool to predict both sudden learning plateaus and smooth scaling laws across different deep learning models.

**Why it may be interesting.** The mathematical machinery developed—reducing complex high-dimensional learning dynamics to low-dimensional, solvable ODEs governed by symmetry principles—is highly relevant for understanding emergent collective behavior in many-body quantum systems or disordered physical models.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper unifies two seemingly disparate observations in neural network training: abrupt 'sudden learning' steps and smooth power-law scaling laws.

**Main result.** It shows that the dynamics of various architectures (MLPs, Attention) can be unified under a single mathematical framework called the Neural Quadratic Form (NQF), whose evolution reduces to solvable differential equations like Lotka–Volterra systems.

**Method.** The analysis uses symmetry arguments (permutation symmetry) combined with Taylor expansion around zero weights to derive the NQF structure, followed by solving the resulting low-dimensional dynamical system for the order parameter $M=WW^T$.

**Model / system.** The model is a general neural network trained via gradient descent on a smooth cost function. The core mathematical object is the Neural Quadratic Form (NQF), which approximates the network's behavior near initialization.

**Key observables.** Order parameter $M=WW^T$, effective slope $k_{	ext{eff}}$, and the time evolution of mode amplitudes $ho_a(t)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Initial weight scale ($\epsilon$), learning rate, and architectural parameters defining the structure matrix $A(x)$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes permutation symmetry ($\mathcal{S}_d$) for the network layer and that the system can be accurately described by a quadratic expansion (NQF) near initialization.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate how different architectures reduce to the NQF structure via $A(x)$, show tracking accuracy between full models and NQFs, and compare predicted scaling laws against finite-initialization effects.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes Theorem 1 (NQF) using symmetry arguments for various modules. It then analyzes the dynamics by reducing the evolution to moments $(M, \mu)$, showing reduction to Lotka–Volterra equations. Finally, it confirms these predictions by analyzing scaling laws and mode evolution.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Neural networks trained by gradient descent on a smooth cost function can nevertheless learn in steps: the cost holds on long plateaus and then drops abruptly. Meanwhile, training losses instead follow smooth power laws. Variants of both behaviors occur in architectures with very different microscopic structures, which is the signature of a few relevant collective variables. We show that a symmetry fixes what those variables are: a network layer is a sum over interchangeable units, so relabeling the units leaves it unchanged; given smoothness and the condition that a unit's gradient vanish at the origin, symmetry then enforces a universal leading form for the expansion about the near-zero weights present at the start of training, the quadratic $\Tr[WW^{\top}A(x)]$, in which every architectural detail is confined to a single ``structure matrix" $A(x)$ that we compute for each architecture. Perceptrons, attention layers, mixtures of experts, and convolutions become one model at different $A$. Its training dynamics then close on the ``order parameter" $M=WW^{\top}$ and, whenever the data matrices share an eigenbasis, reduce to a Lotka--Volterra equation whose modes switch on one after another. The smaller the initial weights, the further apart the switch-on times, and the plateaus appear as a singular limit of a smooth flow; when many modes are unresolved the same events merge into a power law in training time whose exponent the theory predicts. We confirm both numerically across training methods and architectures.

</details>

### [Beyond persuasion: Mobile electoral interfaces in polarized societies](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13184v1)

**Authors:** Nuno Crokidakis, Celia Anteneodo  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** statistical mechanics · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.13184v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** 🔥 `non-equilibrium universality` **4/5** · `driven-dissipative phase transition` **3/5** · `methods for driven-dissipative` **3/5** · `Frenkel-Kontorova` **2/5** · `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **1/5** · `correlated / nonlocal dissipation` **1/5** · `scars & prethermalization` **1/5**

<div class="figure-strip-hint">↔ Scroll figures horizontally</div>

Each polit- ical bloc consists of a rigid partisan core (L0 and R0) and a fluctuating aligned population (l(t) and r(t)). The nonaligned compartment is similarly composed of a rigid nonaligned nu- cleus of size M0 and a mobile electoral interface of size m(t). Alignment and detachment processes determine the size of the mobile interface, with steady state m∗, while a rejection- based probabilistic rule allocates the remaining mobile voters between the competing blocs.</figcaption>

The susceptibil- ity reaches its maximum at ∆ρ = 0, corresponding to equally rejected political blocs, and decreases as the rejection asym- metry increases.</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** This paper models polarized elections by focusing on the dynamics of a mobile voter fraction rather than direct opinion change. It shows that the system's sensitivity to political shocks (electoral susceptibility) is directly controlled by the size and fluctuation dynamics of this mobile interface. This shifts the focus from ideological conversion to interfacial fluctuations as the key driver of electoral outcomes.

**Why it may be interesting.** The mathematical framework of modeling collective behavior using a 'mobile interface' whose fluctuations control macroscopic response mirrors concepts in non-equilibrium statistical mechanics, particularly those involving phase boundaries or critical fluctuations.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To model electoral competition in contemporary polarized societies where direct opinion change between stable partisan blocs is suppressed.

**Main result.** Electoral susceptibility to political shocks is found to be proportional to the stationary size of the mobile electoral interface, suggesting fluctuations at this interface govern macroscopic response rather than large-scale ideological conversion.

**Method.** The authors developed a minimal dynamical model using coupled differential equations to track the allocation and dynamics of a mobile voter fraction between two partisan blocs.

**Model / system.** The system partitions the electorate into two stable partisan cores ($L_0, R_0$), fluctuating aligned populations ($l(t), r(t)$), and a key mobile electoral interface ($m(t)$). The dynamics are governed by capture/detachment rates and a Fermi-like probabilistic rule.

**Key observables.** Electoral susceptibility ($\chi_e$) and the stationary size of the mobile electoral interface ($m^*$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Rejection asymmetries ($ho_L, ho_R$), capture rate ($a$), detachment rates ($d_L, d_R$), and the mobility regime (low vs. high $m^*/m_{\max}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Direct transitions between opposing blocs are strongly suppressed; analysis is restricted to homogeneous dynamics for tractability.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically shows the components of the electorate and the two-step process: mobility dynamics determining $m^*$, followed by rejection-based allocation. Figure 2 illustrates $\chi_e$'s dependence on asymmetry ($\Deltaho$).

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the model partitioning, derives equations for mobile interface evolution and voter allocation probabilities (Fermi rule), calculates susceptibility ($\chi_e$) based on these dynamics, and analyzes how different mobility regimes dictate the system's response to shocks.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Many traditional models of electoral dynamics emphasize opinion change, social influence and persuasion mechanisms. In contrast, contemporary polarized societies often exhibit relatively stable partisan blocs coexisting with an electorally relevant mobile population. We propose a minimal dynamical model in which electoral competition is governed not by direct persuasion between opposing blocs, but by the dynamics and allocation of a mobile electoral interface. The electorate is partitioned into two stable partisan blocs and a mobile fraction, while direct transitions between the polarized blocs are strongly suppressed. Mobile voters are allocated between the competing blocs through a Fermi-like probabilistic rule governed by rejection asymmetries. Analytical calculations show that the electoral susceptibility to political shocks is proportional to the stationary size of the mobile electoral interface, identifying electoral mobility as the key quantity controlling the macroscopic response of polarized systems to external perturbations. The model naturally predicts a continuum of mobility regimes, ranging from frozen polarization to highly responsive electoral states characterized by a broad mobile interface. These results suggest that, in strongly polarized elections, aggregate electoral changes may be governed primarily by fluctuations at the mobile electoral interface rather than by large-scale ideological conversion.

</details>

**Authors:** Bastian Schindler, Martin Eckstein  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** strongly correlated electrons · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.13146v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** 🔥 `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **4/5** · `methods for driven-dissipative` **3/5** · `non-equilibrium universality` **3/5** · `driven-dissipative phase transition` **2/5** · `correlated / nonlocal dissipation` **1/5** · `scars & prethermalization` **1/5**

**Summary.** This paper introduces an advanced computational technique combining Tensor Cross Interpolation and Quantics Tensor-Trains to efficiently calculate high-dimensional integrals arising in the strong-coupling expansion of quantum impurity models. By implementing this method directly into the real-time Keldysh formalism, the authors achieve a controlled and scalable solver for non-equilibrium steady states in correlated electron systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, systematically improvable numerical framework for calculating non-equilibrium quantum dynamics in strongly correlated systems, which is crucial for understanding open quantum systems far from equilibrium.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is evaluating high-dimensional, time-ordered integrals arising from the strong-coupling expansion for quantum impurity models in non-equilibrium steady states (NESS).

**Main result.** The combination of Tensor Cross Interpolation and Quantics Tensor-Train representations substantially reduces the computational cost of evaluating higher-order diagrams, enabling systematic calculations up to the third order.

**Method.** The authors employ a strong-coupling expansion within the Keldysh formalism, utilizing Tensor Cross Interpolation (TCI) to decompose high-dimensional tensors into a low-rank Quantics Tensor-Train (QTT) format for efficient computation of retarded convolutions.

**Model / system.** The work focuses on quantum impurity models, specifically addressing non-equilibrium steady states using the Anderson model and extended Dynamical Mean Field Theory (DMFT) with retarded density-density interactions. The formalism is built around solving Dyson equations for pseudo-particle propagators.

**Key observables.** Pseudo-particle propagator $\hat{G}(t, t')$, self-energy $\hat{\Sigma}(t, t')$, and physical correlation functions $C_{AB}(t, t')$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The order of the strong-coupling expansion (up to third order), bond dimensions ($\chi$), and artificial broadening parameter ($\eta$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes that transient dynamics decay sufficiently fast to allow for a NESS description, necessitating an artificial damping factor $\eta$ in calculations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the Keldysh contour structure, examples of self-energy diagrams (OCA), and show convergence tests for spectral functions (NCA, OCA, TOA) as a function of broadening ($\eta$) or bond dimension ($\chi$).

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the formalism by first establishing the need to evaluate high-dimensional integrals in NESS. It then introduces TCI/QTT for tensor decomposition and applies this machinery to solve the Dyson equation iteratively, culminating in calculations of higher-order self-energies.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Including higher order diagrammatic corrections to the strong-coupling expansion is mainly limited by the evaluation of high-dimensional, time-ordered integrals. In this work we present and compare four different parametrizations of the integrands in order to obtain a low-rank (quantics) tensor-train representation using tensor cross interpolation. Particular emphasis is placed on a quantics time-difference formulation in which the required retarded convolutions are performed directly in quantics tensor-train form. Using controlled Gaussian benchmarks, we analyze the accuracy, bond dimensions, and computational scaling of the different approaches. We then validate the most promising formulations in self-consistent equilibrium and nonequilibrium DMFT calculations and demonstrate calculations up to the third order in the strong-coupling expansion. Finally, we extend the solver to impurity models with retarded density-density interactions and apply it within nonequilibrium extended DMFT. Our results show that tensor cross interpolation substantially reduces the cost of evaluating higher-order diagrams and provides a controlled, systematically improvable framework for nonequilibrium quantum impurity calculations.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper demonstrates that modulating the rate at which a quantum particle is measured can be used as a control knob to induce directed current flow in an otherwise symmetric system. By analyzing both periodic and stochastic switching protocols, the authors show that breaking the symmetry of measurement rates ($\delta R 
eq 0$) is necessary for non-zero transport. The work provides deep insights into the interplay between coherent dynamics, dissipation, and measurement backaction.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides concrete theoretical tools to engineer non-equilibrium quantum transport in open systems, directly relevant to controlling quantum dynamics using measurement backaction, a key area in quantum optics and AMO physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research addresses how controlling open-system dynamics and stationary quantum transport can be achieved by temporally modulating the measurement rate (measurement backaction).

**Main result.** Directed particle transport is only possible when the measurement rate is modulated ($\delta R 
eq 0$), as this breaks the dynamical symmetry of the system.

**Method.** The authors derive exact analytical expressions for the long-time current using methods applicable to both periodic and stochastic switching protocols, including solving coupled linear differential equations.

**Model / system.** The model is a minimal exactly solvable quantum particle on a 1D lattice that undergoes continuous monitoring. The dynamics are governed by a master equation incorporating coherent tunneling, incoherent hopping, and measurement-induced dephasing terms whose rates switch between two values ($R^+$ and $R^-$).

**Key observables.** Long-time averaged particle velocity ($v_\infty$), which serves as the primary diagnostic for directed transport.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The difference in measurement rates ($\delta R = (R^+ - R^-)/2$) is crucial; the analysis also explores limits like slow-switching and fast-driving regimes.

**Assumptions / limitations.** For stochastic switching, the system is assumed to be ergodic. For periodic switching, convergence to an asymptotic state is assumed.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the lattice structure for different switching states; Figure 2 plots $v_\infty$ versus driving parameters (period or rate); Figures 3 and 4 map out transport regimes separating Zeno-dominated from anti-Zeno-dominated transport regions.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the general dynamics using a master equation for a switching lattice. It then derives analytical solutions for the long-time current under periodic driving, followed by an analysis using the Birkhoff ergodic theorem for stochastic switching. Finally, it compares results across different time/driving limits.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Temporal modulation of the measurement rate provides a powerful mechanism for controlling open-system dynamics through measurement backaction. We demonstrate this mechanism in a minimal exactly solvable model of a continuously monitored quantum particle on a switching lattice. We derive exact analytical expressions for the long-time current under both periodic and stochastic switching, revealing a common slow-switching limit and identifying a measurement-induced crossover between Zeno- and anti-Zeno-dominated transport regimes that controls the direction of the current.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper reports on achieving mid-circuit ground-state cooling in the omg trapped-ion architecture. By using dissipative operations on one qubit species to cool the shared motional mode, they enable non-destructive readout of a second metastable qubit. This capability is crucial for implementing error correction and complex quantum gates in future ion trap processors.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses crucial hardware requirements—cooling and readout fidelity—for scaling trapped-ion quantum computers, providing vital experimental benchmarks for open quantum system control.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core challenge is demonstrating mid-circuit ground-state cooling and non-destructive ancilla readout within the optical-metastable-ground (omg) trapped-ion architecture.

**Main result.** The authors successfully demonstrated cooling the global motional mode to near the quantum ground state ($ar{n} \approx 0$) while preserving the coherence of the metastable qubit, enabling non-destructive readout for quantum logic spectroscopy.

**Method.** The technique combines measurement-based cooling using dissipative operations on the ground qubit with fluorescence detection on an ancilla ion to read out the metastable qubit state.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a trapped-ion crystal utilizing two species: a metastable qubit ($	ext{m}$) and a ground state ($	ext{g}$) ancilla. The process involves complex laser sequences, including Doppler cooling, EIT cooling, and specialized sideband driving for cooling.

**Key observables.** Motional state occupation number ($ar{n}$), FC success rate ($99.81\%$), and qubit coherence times ($T_2$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Cooling fidelity achieved to $ar{n} = 0.02(1)$; the metastable qubit lifetime is assumed largely undisturbed during short pulses.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that the metastable manifold remains spectrally isolated from the fast cycling transitions used for detection and cooling.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the laser beam geometry for two ions, and show the sequence of Doppler and EIT cooling steps alongside heating effects from fluorescence checks or RF displacement.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the experimental setup, presents measurements on motional excitation sources (FCs, RF pulses), validates the non-destructive readout mechanism via QLS principles, and concludes by confirming ground-state cooling capability for fault-tolerant primitives.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The trapped-ion optical-metastable-ground ($\textit{omg}$) architecture for quantum processors promises the full functionality of two-species experiments, including sympathetic cooling and non-destructive ancilla readout, without the corresponding hardware overhead. We confirm that we can cool a global motional mode of a mixed metastable-ground state Coulomb crystal to the motional ground state via dissipative operations on the ground ($\textit{g}$) qubit without disturbing coherence of the metastable ($\textit{m}$) qubit. This enables quantum logic spectroscopy to non-destructively readout the state of the $\textit{m}$ qubit using fluorescence detection of the $\textit{g}$ qubit. Extensions of these demonstrations to larger system sizes should enable the mitigation of motional heating after ion shuttling and syndrome extraction for quantum error correction, both crucial primitives for future fault-tolerant quantum computers based on trapped ions.

</details>

**Summary.** This work reports the first FIR spectroscopy of interacting moiré bands in MATBG using a novel millikelvin platform. By combining experiment with the Topological Heavy-Fermion model, the authors revealed characteristic resonances arising from strong light-matter coupling between itinerant and localized electrons. This provides fundamental insight into correlated electronic states in twisted 2D materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** The strong coupling between optical fields and emergent many-body modes in flat bands provides a clean platform for studying light-matter interaction in strongly correlated electronic systems, relevant to quantum optics and open quantum systems dynamics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Optical spectroscopy of interacting moiré bands in the far-infrared (FIR) regime has been largely unexplored due to extreme experimental challenges.

**Main result.** The study observed characteristic FIR resonances in MATBG, showing that light-matter interaction is governed by itinerant topological electrons acting as an 'antenna' coupled to localized heavy fermions.

**Method.** The research combines new millikelvin FIR spectroscopy measurements with theoretical modeling using the Topological Heavy-Fermion (THF) model.

**Model / system.** The system is Magic Angle Twisted Bilayer Graphene (MATBG), whose physics is modeled by the THF framework, which incorporates Hubbard interactions for localized heavy fermions and topological character from itinerant light fermions.

**Key observables.** FIR resonances, photovoltage ($	ext{V}_{	ext{ph}}$) spectra, transport characteristics ($	ext{R}_{	ext{xx}}$), and spectral weight development at low energies.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Filling factor ($v$), magnetic field ($B$), temperature ($T$), on-site Coulomb energy, and various coupling constants within the THF model (e.g., $W_1, W_3, U_1$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that itinerant topological electrons couple strongly to the optical field, and the THF model provides a practical route to compute the optical response.

**Figures summary.** Figures show FIR spectra mapping photovoltage vs. filling factor/bias current; theoretical calculations illustrate resonance dependence on magnetic field and strain ($\sigma(\omega)$).

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the experimental gap, presents measurements using a new millikelvin platform to observe resonances in MATBG, develops the THF model to explain these findings, and analyzes parameter sensitivity across different doping regimes.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Moiré engineering in twisted two-dimensional (2D) materials radically alters low-energy bands, interactions and topological quantum states. Despite extensive studies, optical spectroscopy of interacting moiré bands in the characteristic far-infrared (FIR) regime has remained largely unexplored due to extreme experimental challenges. Using a newly developed millikelvin FIR platform, we report the observation of the long-sought-after characteristic FIR resonances of flat-band electrons in magic-angle twisted bilayer graphene (MATBG). We observe highly tunable spectroscopic signatures of interacting light and heavy fermions that constitute the flat bands in MATBG. Using the topological heavy-fermion model (THF), we show that itinerant topological electrons act as an "antenna" that couples strongly to the optical field, with resonant frequencies renormalized by the hybridization with localized heavy electrons. We establish optical selection rules of MATBG which uncovers the key symmetry governing light-heavy fermion hybridization. At charge neutrality, we observe pronounced resonances at energies below the on-site Coulomb energy, implying the emergence of new many-body modes. Our experiments and modeling provide a fundamental understanding of light-matter interactions in MATBG and enable resonant optical spectroscopy of moiré bands down to millikelvin temperatures.

</details>

### [Aperiodicity is sufficient for macroscopic thermalization](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13462v1)

**Authors:** Amit Vikram  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** statistical mechanics · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.13462v1>
