**Summary.** This paper proposes that macroscopic quantum systems can thermalize even when starting from non-equilibrium states, provided the dynamics exhibit sufficient aperiodicity. The key breakthrough is establishing that this equilibration can be predicted using only information about the decay of initial state return probabilities over finite time intervals. This provides a strong, operational framework for understanding thermalization in complex quantum many-body systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** It offers a powerful, operational extension to traditional thermalization theory by providing concrete, computable criteria applicable over experimentally relevant finite time windows, moving beyond the limitations of infinite-time averages.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper seeks a general mechanism to explain how macroscopic quantum observables thermalize over finite time scales in isolated, non-equilibrium systems.

**Main result.** It shows that mere aperiodicity and the small return probability of initial states over a finite time interval are sufficient conditions for predicting macroscopic equilibration at almost all times.

**Method.** The approach connects concepts from dynamical ergodicity (aperiodicity) to operational quantum statistical mechanics, providing criteria based on observable decay rather than relying solely on infinite-time limits.

**Model / system.** The focus is on isolated many-body quantum or classical systems. The analysis centers on macroscopic observables like coarse-grained charge densities, treating the system as evolving from a non-equilibrium state towards equilibrium.

**Key observables.** Coarse-grained observables (e.g., charge densities), return probability of initial states, and memorylessness decay.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Finite observation times, the thermodynamic limit ($N 	o \infty$), and the degree of aperiodicity.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that the mechanism can be characterized by observable-independent information, specifically the small return probability over finite time ranges.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theory by first identifying aperiodicity as the key measure of partial ergodicity. It then contrasts this finite-time approach with standard infinite-time ergodic theorems, culminating in a framework that unifies memorylessness decay, state alignment, and predictions for various thermalization regimes.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We identify a general mechanism for the finite-time thermalization of macroscopic observables, such as coarse-grained charge densities, in terms of elementary forms of the quantum dynamics of initial states: (1) aperiodicity, which provides a computable measure of (2) a dynamical partially ergodic exploration of the Hilbert space. Specifically, this mechanism predicts the equilibration of all (concentrated) macroscopic observables, in almost all states in an initial ensemble and almost all times within finite and longer intervals, given only the observable-independent information that the return probability of the ensemble of initial states is small over a finite time range. As a special case, it also accesses standard results on equilibration over infinitely long times in terms of (stronger versions of) the effective dimension of initial state delocalization in the energy eigenbasis. Our results incorporate macroscopic thermalization into the domain of operational quantum statistical mechanics, recently developed to provide finitely computable criteria for microscopic thermalization. We discuss an overall characterization of this approach as establishing connections between (1) the decay of a (theoretically or experimentally) computable probe indicating memorylessness, (2) a fundamental invariant mechanism in terms of the alignment of observables or states in the Hilbert space, and (3) predicting different natural forms of (classical and) quantum thermalization, most of which rigorously recover conventional eigenstate-based descriptions of infinite-time thermalization as a special case but provide stronger accessible predictions over finite observation times in the thermodynamic limit.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces and validates the use of a Quantum Pulse Gate (QPG) for coherently filtering multimode parametric down-conversion sources. This technique successfully projects heralded signal photons into spectrally pure, well-defined temporal modes with high fidelity. The results conclusively show that this coherent method vastly outperforms conventional intensity filtering, which degrades photon purity by introducing mixed states.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses a key hardware bottleneck in quantum networking—the preparation of high-quality single photons—by providing a robust, coherent filtering mechanism applicable to existing PDC sources.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The challenge is generating spectrally pure and indistinguishable single photons from multimode sources, which is crucial for quantum network applications.

**Main result.** Coherent temporal filtering using a Quantum Pulse Gate (QPG) successfully generates heralded photons with high purity (>0.90), whereas conventional intensity filtering results in mixed states.

**Method.** The authors compare QPG-based coherent filtering against standard spectral intensity filtering by analyzing the resulting photon state's spectral density matrix and chronocyclic Q-function.

**Model / system.** The system utilizes a time-frequency entangled pair source via parametric down-conversion (PDC). The core comparison is between projecting the idler photon onto a specific temporal mode using a QPG versus simple spectral amplitude filtering.

**Key observables.** Photon purity ($\mathcal{F}$), Spectral Density Matrix ($\Gamma$), and Chronocyclic $Q$-function ($\mathcal{F}(\Omega, 	au)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Filter bandwidth, coherence properties (single TM vs. multimode mixture), and the resulting spectral purity.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Conventional intensity filtering cannot control relative phase or select a well-defined temporal mode; minor deviations in QPG performance are attributed to finite measurement resolution or pump shaping limitations.

**Figures summary.** Figures conceptually distinguish coherent (single TM transmission) vs. incoherent (spectral intensity transmission, phase erasure) filtering setups and show reconstructed spectral purity ($\mathcal{F}$) versus filter bandwidth for both methods.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the need for pure single photons; it then details the theoretical comparison between QPG-mediated coherent projection and conventional incoherent filtering using mathematical formalisms (JSA, $\Gamma$ matrix); finally, it presents experimental results demonstrating superior purity and programmability with the QPG.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Spectrally pure and indistinguishable single photons are essential for quantum network platforms, where high-visibility interference underpins many quantum information protocols. However, most practical single-photon sources emit spectrally multimode states with reduced purity. Conventional spectral intensity filtering can partially improve purity but cannot select a well-defined temporal mode (TM). Here, we demonstrate coherent temporal filtering of a multimode parametric down-conversion (PDC) source using a quantum pulse gate (QPG) and benchmark its performance against conventional intensity filtering. The generated PDC photons exhibit strong spectral correlations, rendering extraction of pure heralded photons from the pair a challenge. We demonstrate that QPG filtering consistently generates heralded photons with purities above 0.90 regardless of the filter shape. Contrariwise, using spectral intensity filters yields mixed photons. Photon purities are probed with chronocyclic Q-function tomography. Furthermore, we demonstrate the versatility of QPG filtering by extracting structured TMs, including superposition of picosecond time bins. These results establish the QPG as a practical coherent filtering tool for quantum network applications.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper demonstrates that magnetic fluxes threading cycles in chiral quantum walks act as a controllable resource to engineer local measurement back-action. By analyzing the two-time statistics, the authors show flux can drive the system to saturate fundamental bounds like the Lüders bound. This establishes gauge-invariant flux control over temporal quantum correlations.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete mechanism—flux engineering—to manipulate non-classical temporal correlations (LGI violation) in quantum transport models, which is highly relevant for understanding measurement limitations in open quantum systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates how gauge-invariant magnetic fluxes control measurement back-action and temporal quantum correlations (Leggett-Garg inequalities) in chiral continuous-time quantum walks.

**Main result.** Flux can be engineered to saturate the Lüders bound ($L_3 = 3/2$) by reducing dynamics to a specific Krylov subspace, establishing flux as a resource for controlling local measurement back-action.

**Method.** The analysis involves deriving exact two-time statistics using rooted return amplitudes and performing short-time expansions of correlation functions related to the Kolmogorov inconsistency.

**Model / system.** The system is chiral continuous-time quantum walks (CTQWs) on a graph, governed by a Hamiltonian incorporating oriented Peierls phases ($	heta_{jk}$). The study focuses on measurements performed at a single vertex using a dichotomic observable.

**Key observables.** Measurement back-action ($K_
u$), Leggett-Garg functional ($L_3$), and the rooted return amplitude $A_
u(t; \Phi)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The external fluxes ($\Phi$) threading cycles in the graph, time ($t$), and measurement times ($\alpha t$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes a localized initial state $|
uangle$ and relies on short-time expansions for correlation functions.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate flux dependence of back-action contrast over time and show phase diagrams mapping Leggett-Garg violation against applied fluxes.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by defining the CTQW model with gauge-invariant fluxes, deriving exact two-time statistics from return amplitudes, analyzing short-time expansions to find flux dependence, and finally demonstrating how this control can saturate fundamental bounds like the Lüders bound.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Gauge-invariant fluxes control interference in chiral continuous-time quantum walks. We investigate how they affect sequential measurements at a single vertex, using a dichotomic observable that distinguishes return to that vertex from occupation of its complement. For a walker initially localized at the measured vertex, the complete two-time statistics, including measurement back-action and Leggett--Garg correlators, are determined exactly by the return amplitude, connecting temporal correlations to the local spectral measure and gauge-invariant closed-walk interference. At short times, the leading disturbance is independent of the Peierls phases, whereas flux sensitivity enters at higher orders through interference among closed walks. We further identify a graph-independent sufficient mechanism for saturating the Lüders bound: flux can reduce the rooted dynamics to a balanced two-dimensional Krylov subspace with equal spectral weights, yielding a constructive flux-engineering criterion for attaining the Lüders bound of $3/2$ at finite times. The mechanism is realized exactly on a two-flux diamond graph, where destructive interference renders additional rooted modes dark and the local back-action depends on relative combinations of the two independent fluxes. For flux-threaded cycles, an exact winding-number expansion reveals a parity-dependent onset: the leading flux contrast occurs generically at order $t^N$ for even cycles and $t^{2N}$ for odd cycles. Across the cycles examined, flux can either enhance the maximal Leggett--Garg violation or shift strong violations to earlier measurement times, with half flux driving the four-site cycle to the Lüders bound. These results establish gauge-invariant flux as a resource for engineering local measurement back-action and temporal quantum correlations.

</details>

Magnon enhanced cooperativity depending on the driving magnon power: We plot the cooperativity between the magnon and center of mass motion of levitated YIG for zero detuning. When Cmb exceeds unity, the two modes are strongly coupled and quantum effects can be become dominant.</figcaption>
</figure>

**Summary.** The authors successfully demonstrated the stable levitation of a YIG sphere using a magnetic Paul trap at room temperature. They analyzed the coupled dynamics between the mechanical motion (phonons) and the spin wave excitations (magnons). This work establishes a platform for investigating strongly coupled quantum magno-mechanics, with potential applications in quantum information processing.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work bridges macroscopic mechanical trapping (Paul traps) with microscopic quantum excitations (magnons), providing an experimentally realized platform to study cavity QED concepts in a solid-state magnetic system, relevant for quantum sensing and information processing.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The work demonstrates stable levitation of a YIG sphere using a magnetic Paul trap, aiming to realize strongly coupled quantum magno-mechanical systems.

**Main result.** Stable trapping was achieved at room temperature with measured Q-factors ($\sim 25$) and secular frequencies (15.8 Hz, 17.2 Hz), and theoretical estimates suggest achievable magnon-phonon cooperativity above unity.

**Method.** The study combines classical stability analysis using Floquet theory on Mathieu equations with experimental measurements of mechanical motion via backscattered light detection.

**Model / system.** The system involves a YIG sphere (soft magnet) trapped by a magnetic Paul trap. The physics couples the center-of-mass mechanical motion (phonons) to the quantized spin waves (magnons), described by coupled Hamiltonians.

**Key observables.** Q-factors ($\sim 25$), secular frequencies (15.8 Hz, 17.2 Hz), and magnon-phonon cooperativity ($C_{mb}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Room temperature operation, magnetic field gradients, and coupling strengths between mechanical and magnonic modes.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the classical limit for soft magnets (YIG) and employs small-motion approximations to derive the equations of motion.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the stability diagram in parameter space (Fig. 1), the experimental setup using neodymium magnets (Fig. 2), and the measured power spectral density showing trapped oscillations (Fig. 3).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from deriving the full equations of motion for the coupled system, simplifying them to a vector Mathieu equation for stability analysis, presenting the theoretical framework for magnon-phonon coupling, and concluding with experimental measurements confirming stable levitation and quantifying quantum coupling potential.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Magnetic levitation offer passive levitation of massive objects for use in advanced inertia sensors, for the generation of non-classical macroscopic motional states, and towards the table-top testing of low energy gravity with quantum mechanics. Magnons, a quanta of spin wave, couple to many physical quantities and strongly to electromagnetic fields, even at room temperature. In this work we demonstrate the stable trapping of a small YIG sphere using a magnetic Paul trap. We present a classical stability analysis of a magnetic Paul trap and show the stability diagram for all mechanical degrees of freedom. We show that coupling between librational and translational modes changes the stability region. We experimentally levitate the soft magnet yttrium iron garnet at room temperature obtaining Q-factors of $\sim25$ and secular frequencies $15.8$ Hz and $17.2$ Hz. We provide numerical estimates of the achievable enhanced coupling between the center-of-mass motion and excited magnon modes, with a cooperativity above unity despite strong mechanical damping, indicating potential applications in quantum information processing, quantum interconnects and quantum memories.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper reports an experimental demonstration of Quantum Key Distribution using the principle of Indefinite Causal Order, realized via a photonic quantum SWITCH. This approach allows for eavesdropper detection by monitoring coherence in a control qubit without forcing Alice and Bob to publicly reveal any part of their raw key material. It proves that ICO can enhance QKD security protocols.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses fundamental concepts in quantum information theory—causality and resource quantification—by proposing a novel physical implementation (the quantum SWITCH) to enhance cryptographic security protocols beyond current standards.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To demonstrate an experimental protocol for Quantum Key Distribution (QKD) that leverages the concept of Indefinite Causal Order (ICO) to detect eavesdropping without sacrificing any key bits.

**Main result.** The experiment provides a proof-of-principle that ICO can be exploited to achieve eavesdropper detection by monitoring the control qubit, which is superior to standard QKD methods requiring public key comparison and bit discarding.

**Method.** The protocol adapts BB84-like QKD using a photonic quantum SWITCH to embed Alice's and Bob's operations. Eavesdropping is detected by measuring coherence loss in the control qubit rather than comparing raw key material.

**Model / system.** The system utilizes a photonic quantum SWITCH, where the path degree of freedom controls the indefinite causal order between measurement/preparation operations (Alice and Bob). The polarization degree of freedom carries the key information.

**Key observables.** Average eavesdropper detection probability ($0.15 \pm 0.02$ per shared qubit) measured via the control qubit state, specifically monitoring population in $|-angle_c$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The protocol's success hinges on a new measurement technique allowing polarization readout inside the SWITCH without destroying path coherence.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Currently, the implementation is limited by post-selection required for measurements within the quantum SWITCH, meaning it is not yet a fully secure QKD protocol.

**Figures summary.** The notes do not provide specific figure summaries beyond general references to figures illustrating joint probabilities and mutual information calculations in related theoretical analyses.

**Paper structure.** The paper moves from establishing the theoretical framework of ICO using the quantum SWITCH, adapting it to BB84-like QKD, detailing the measurement techniques required for local readout, presenting the experimental results demonstrating eavesdropper detection, and concluding with a discussion of limitations.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

In quantum physics the order in which different operations occur can be placed in superposition. The resulting processes have an indefinite causal order and are both of fundamental interest and can be viewed as a novel quantum resource that enables a variety of new protocols. Here we report an experimental implementation of one such protocol, where we perform BB84-like quantum cryptography by placing Alice and Bob's measurement-and-preparation operations in a photonic quantum SWITCH. By embedding Alice and Bob within the quantum SWITCH, the protocol achieves an average eavesdropper detection probability of $0.15 \pm 0.02$ per shared qubit, with eavesdropper detection performed through measurements of the control qubit rather than by comparing the key. Unlike the standard BB84 and related schemes, which detect eavesdropping by publicly revealing and discarding a fraction of the raw key, our approach requires no disclosure of key material: every retained qubit can, in principle, be tested for eavesdropping while remaining available for key generation. The experiment relies on a new measurement technique that allows the polarization of a photon to be measured inside the quantum SWITCH without destroying path coherence. Although the present implementation does not yet constitute a secure quantum key distribution protocol, owing to the post-selection required for measurements within the quantum SWITCH, it provides a proof of principle that indefinite causal order can be exploited to detect eavesdropping without sacrificing key bits.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents an advanced ion trap platform built on borosilicate glass featuring an integrated waveguide written by femtosecond lasers. This architecture solves the challenge of routing optical signals in large-scale trapped-ion quantum processors. By demonstrating trapping and coherent operations using this on-chip light delivery, it offers a robust and scalable route for future quantum computing devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses a major engineering bottleneck in trapped-ion quantum computing—the integration of photonics. For theorists studying open quantum systems or quantum optics, this provides a concrete platform for modeling light-matter interactions on chip at scale.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge addressed is developing a scalable platform for trapped-ion quantum computing that requires robust, on-chip delivery of optical light to address individual qubits.

**Main result.** The authors successfully demonstrated an end-to-end system integrating an ion trap on borosilicate glass with an integrated, low-loss waveguide capable of delivering coherent light for qubit operations.

**Method.** They fabricated the platform using femtosecond laser writing (FLW) to create waveguides bonded atop a surface-electrode ion trap and validated functionality in a cryogenic trapped-ion system.

**Model / system.** The physical system is an ion trap built on borosilicate glass, featuring aluminum electrodes and an integrated waveguide. The light delivery mechanism physically separates the optical layer from the electrode substrate, enabling compatibility with silicon integration.

**Key observables.** Coherent Rabi oscillations driven by 729 nm light delivered through the waveguide; measurement of stray electric fields showing slow drift over hours.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Single-mode low-loss guidance at 729 nm and 405 nm; curved waveguides maintained down to a radius of curvature of 6 mm.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The architecture relies on bonding an optically structured block onto the trap surface, and that positive refractive index changes induced by femtosecond laser exposure are sufficient for guiding weakly guided modes.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the concept showing the integrated waveguide positioned in-plane with the ion addressing zone; other figures detail waveguide writing setups and mode profile tuning based on focus offset.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the need for scalable optical access to presenting the physical platform, detailing the fabrication of low-loss waveguides, and finally validating the entire system through trapping, shuttling, and coherent quantum operations.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present an ion-trap platform on borosilicate glass with an integrated femtosecond-laser-written waveguide for on-chip light delivery. The optical layer is physically separated from the electrode substrate and bonded atop the trap, remaining compatible with silicon-based integration. We engineer single-mode low-loss guidance at 729 nm with tunable mode-field diameter and achieve low-loss curved waveguides down to a radius of curvature of 6 mm. We also extend single-mode operation to a wavelength of 405 nm. The fabrication process is compatible with the industrial fabrication of a single-metal-layer surface-electrode trap, including active fiber alignment and bonding. We validate the platform in a cryogenic trapped-ion system with $^{40}$Ca$^+$, demonstrating trapping, shuttling the ion to a zone in front of the waveguide, and coherent operations driven by 729 nm light delivered through the integrated waveguide. We characterize the effect of the exposed dielectric on the ion and measure stray electric fields that show slow drift at a timescale of hours. The architecture is compatible with hybrid micro-optics (e.g. pick-and-place lenses) to realize single ion addressing and provides a robust, scalable route to integrated light delivery for trapped-ion devices.

</details>

**Summary.** This work introduces the STS metric to rigorously measure how many quantum shots are needed to find approximate solutions to NP-hard problems like MIS using neutral atoms. By comparing results against an excitation-matched random baseline, the authors prove that quantum annealing provides a genuine advantage over classical expectations. The findings delineate specific regimes where quantum resources yield practical benefits versus when classical heuristics suffice.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a rigorous, quantifiable benchmark for assessing true quantum speedup in optimization problems implemented on Rydberg arrays, distinguishing genuine algorithmic advantages from mere hardware noise or local correlations.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is developing an operational metric, STS(r), to quantify whether neutral-atom quantum optimization protocols exhibit genuine concentration toward low-energy solution structures.

**Main result.** Quantum annealing consistently outperforms a random baseline, demonstrating enhanced concentration beyond what can be explained by mere excitation density. The required shot cost scales differently for near-exact versus relaxed target solutions.

**Method.** The authors introduce the Shots-to-Approximate-Solution metric, STS(r), and analyze postprocessed outputs using a degeneracy-weighted shell distribution model ($\pi_j \propto d_{\alpha-j} e^{-eta j}$).

**Model / system.** The study uses programmable Rydberg-atom arrays to implement the Maximum Independent Set (MIS) problem. The analysis relies on modeling output quality via a shell index ($j$) derived from postprocessing the raw quantum bitstrings.

**Key observables.** Shots-to-Approximate-Solution metric ($	ext{STS}(r)$), effective concentration parameter ($eta$), and the residual advantage $\Deltaeta_{	ext{ann}}$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Approximation ratio ($r$), system size ($N$), and the shell index $j$ relative to the optimal MIS size $\alpha$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis focuses on postprocessed outputs rather than raw bitstrings due to blockade violations, necessitating a random baseline control. The shell distribution is modeled using maximum entropy principles.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare quantum annealing shot costs against random baselines and classical reference times for different target ratios ($r=1$ vs. relaxed targets), showing distinct scaling behaviors.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the STS metric, models output quality via shell distributions ($eta$), establishes a random baseline control by matching excitation density, and then analyzes two regimes (near-exact vs. relaxed) to quantify quantum advantage.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Whether neutral-atom quantum optimization protocols exhibit genuine concentration toward low-energy solution structure remains an open question. Here, we introduce a shots-to-approximate-solution metric, STS(r), where r denotes the approximation ratio, and evaluate it using postprocessed outputs modeled by a degeneracy-weighted shell distribution governed by a single effective parameter, $β$, that quantifies concentration toward near-optimal independent sets. To extract the genuine concentration effect in the quantum data, we apply identical postprocessing to both experimental bitstrings and randomly generated bitstrings with matched excitation density, thereby constructing an excitation-matched random baseline. Experiments on programmable Rydberg-atom arrays with system sizes up to 125 sites show that quantum annealing consistently exceeds the random baseline, demonstrating enhanced concentration toward low-energy solution structure beyond what can be attributed solely to excitation density. The results further reveal two distinct target-dependent regimes. For near-exact targets with $r \approx 1$, the required shot count grows exponentially with system size and is reduced at the same exponential level by quantum annealing within the shell-model description. By contrast, for relaxed targets, the shot cost becomes effectively constant, and the corresponding quantum enhancement diminishes, with the classical postprocessing heuristic alone reaching the target in order-unity attempts. Together, these results establish an operational method for quantifying quantum optimization performance and clarify the regimes under which quantum approaches can yield practical benefits.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper presents a novel field-widened interferometer design using a multi-pass Herriott cell to overcome signal degradation in free-space optics. By combining advanced ray tracing with experimental validation, the authors demonstrate that this architecture maintains high interference visibility for large time delays (up to 12 ns) and wide viewing angles. This makes it a highly promising component for robust quantum communication networks.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses hardware limitations for quantum communication protocols by providing a robust, passive optical architecture that maintains coherence over long temporal delays in turbulent or pointing-error prone free-space channels.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge addressed is maintaining high interference visibility for spatially multimode optical beams when large time delays are introduced, which is critical for free-space quantum communication links.

**Main result.** The authors successfully demonstrated a field-widened interferometer using a multi-pass Herriott cell that maintains high interference visibility ($\approx 0.9$ to $0.98$) even with path length differences up to 12 ns and large field-of-view angles.

**Method.** The work combines analytical ray transfer matrix methods for initial design optimization with advanced non-sequential Gaussian Beamlet Decomposition (GBD) ray tracing simulations, validated by pulsed laser time-bin measurements.

**Model / system.** The system is a field-widened, multimode interferometer configured in a Michelson arrangement, utilizing a multi-pass Herriott cell to achieve long optical path separations within a compact physical footprint. It is designed for applications like quantum key distribution (QKD) over free space.

**Key observables.** Interference Visibility ($V$), Time-Bin Separation ($\sim 12$ ns), and Field-of-View ($0.4^\circ$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Path length difference ($\geq 10 	ext{ ns}$), large time delays, and angular deviations (pointing errors).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Initial ray tracing methods neglect wavefront distortions, polarization, and phase effects; the final results rely on GBD modeling to account for these complex wave phenomena.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the conceptual Herriott cell design, analytical optimization of mirror parameters ($\ell$, $d_{fm}$), and experimental measurements showing high visibility maintenance across single-mode and multi-mode inputs at large time delays.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the need for passive alternatives to adaptive optics in free space. It details the theoretical design using ray tracing, validates this with GBD simulations accounting for wavefronts, and concludes with experimental demonstrations using both CW lasers and pulsed sources to measure time-bin visibility.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Interference of optical signals in free-space channels requires optical receivers to support many spatial modes due to atmospheric turbulence, typically necessitating adaptive optics systems. Field-widened interferometers offer a passive alternative, making them particularly attractive for time-bin encoded signals with delays on the order of one nanosecond. Here, we demonstrate a field-widened, multimode interferometer design that achieves a high interference visibility for spatially multimode beams with large time bin separations. The interference of the multimode beams is enabled using a multi-pass Herriott cell that enables a very long path separation with a small form-factor. The design is tested using both numerical ray-tracing simulations and proof-of-principle demonstrations. We create a prototype interferometer with a path length difference of 12ns and determine that it maintains a high interference visibility with a large field-of-view of $0.4^{\circ}$.

</details>

**Authors:** Tomer Alkalay, Jinhong Park, Minseong Oh, Changki Hong, Jun-Young M. Lee, Priya Tiwari, Tapas Senapati, Vladimir Umansky, Moty Heiblum, H. -S. Sim

**Summary.** The paper reports evidence for non-Abelian anyons by measuring time-domain braiding signatures in the $\nu=5/2$ fractional quantum Hall state. Using current fluctuation measurements on edge modes, they independently probe both charged and neutral quasiparticles. The agreement between these two distinct measurements strongly supports the predicted topological order (PH-Pf) over simpler models.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete experimental realization using mesoscopic transport measurements to probe topological order parameters (non-Abelian statistics), which is highly relevant for understanding emergent quantum phenomena in condensed matter systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is providing experimental evidence for non-Abelian braiding statistics of anyons, which has been difficult to observe.

**Main result.** The authors report independent measurements of both charged (downstream) and neutral (upstream) current fluctuations that provide strong evidence supporting the presence of non-Abelian anyons in the $\nu=5/2$ fractional quantum Hall state.

**Method.** The study uses weak partitioning of highly dilute one-dimensional edge modes via two Quantum Point Contacts to measure noise signatures corresponding to time-domain braiding effects.

**Model / system.** The physical system is the Fractional Quantum Hall (FQH) state at filling factor $\nu=5/2$, analyzed using the 'particle-hole Pfaffian' topological order. The measurement probes quasiparticles exhibiting non-Abelian statistics, contrasting them with Abelian anyons.

**Key observables.** Charge Fano factor ($\mathcal{F}_{	ext{charge}}$) and Neutral Fano factor ($\mathcal{F}_{	ext{neutral}}$), derived from measured shot noise ($S_{D2}$ and $S_{D1}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Filling factor $\nu=5/2$, fractional charge $e^* = e/4$, and the comparison between theoretical predictions for PH-Pf and A-Pf topological orders.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that observed excess noise cannot be explained by an incoherent Joule heating model, ruling out this simpler physical mechanism.

**Figures summary.** Figure 2 compares measured shot noise ($S_{D2}$) against theoretical predictions for PH-Pf and A-Pf edges. Figure 3 shows the neutral noise measurement ($S_{D1}$), while supplementary figures compare excess noise to heating model predictions, showing a significant discrepancy.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the experimental setup using two QPCs to measure charge and neutral current fluctuations. It then analyzes the charged sector, finding evidence for non-Abelian statistics via $\mathcal{F}_{	ext{charge}}$. Subsequently, it analyzes the neutral sector ($\mathcal{F}_{	ext{neutral}}$) and concludes by ruling out an incoherent heating model as the source of excess noise.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Unlike elementary particles, which obey either bosonic or fermionic exchange statistics, certain quasiparticles, known as anyons, are predicted to exhibit Abelian or non-Abelian braiding statistics. While braiding Abelian anyons modifies the wavefunction by a 'statistical phase', braiding non-Abelian anyons implements a unitary transformation of the state within a degenerate subspace of states. Experimental evidence of non-Abelian braiding has thus far remained elusive. Here, we report a 'time-domain braiding' signature of non-Abelian anyons in the $ν= 5/2$ fractional quantum Hall state, by extending our previously demonstrated approach with Abelian anyons at $ν= 1/3$. Our approach is based on measurements of the current fluctuations arising from weak partitioning of a highly dilute one-dimensional edge mode. We independently probe the partition noise of the downstream charged mode and also that of the upstream neutral mode. These independent measurements agree with our theoretical predictions for 'time-domain braiding' of the downstream Abelian and the upstream non-Abelian anyons, respectively, in the 'particle-hole Pfaffian' topological order. Together, these results provide evidence for the presence of non-Abelian anyons.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a theory for transferring phase information from coupled spins to a mechanical oscillator using post-selection as a selective measurement backaction. By comparing various variance estimators against the Quantum Fisher Information, the authors establish diagnostic tools like coherence susceptibility. The work highlights that while post-selection can enhance sensing, one must carefully account for the success probability when interpreting sensitivity gains.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a detailed theoretical blueprint for utilizing non-unitary quantum processes (measurement backaction) to enhance sensing capabilities in complex hybrid systems, which is highly relevant to open quantum system dynamics and quantum metrology.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how phase information encoded in a spin system can be selectively transferred to a mechanical oscillator using post-selection as a measurement-induced backaction mechanism for quantum magnetometry.

**Main result.** Post-selection successfully transfers phase information from the spins to the MO, but the apparent improvement in sensitivity calculated only on successful events can overestimate true performance due to low success probabilities and discarded data.

**Method.** The authors compare multiple diagnostics—Semiclassical variance estimator, Pegg-Barnett quantum estimator, and Quantum Fisher Information (QFI)—to quantify phase transfer efficiency under various environmental conditions.

**Model / system.** The system is a hybrid quantum platform consisting of one or more spins coupled to a mechanical oscillator (MO). The dynamics are governed by Hamiltonians incorporating spin-oscillator coupling ($\lambda_0$) and dissipation modeled via a Lindblad master equation.

**Key observables.** Phase variance ($\Delta\hat{\varphi}^2$), Quantum Fisher Information ($F_{ps}(\varphi)$), Coherence Susceptibility ($|dC/d\varphi|$), and the success probability $p(\varphi)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Oscillator quantum coherence, coupling strength ($\lambda_0$), number of spins ($N$), dephasing rate ($\gamma_\phi$), and dissipation rate ($\Gamma_s$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis compares different estimators (SC vs. PB) to determine the best measure of phase transfer; a key limitation noted is that relying solely on successful events overestimates performance.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare variance estimates for different initial states (coherent, cat, thermal), show how dephasing degrades accuracy relative to dissipation, and illustrate the decomposition of QFI into components related to post-selection probability and MO state.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops a theoretical framework for probabilistic phase estimation in hybrid systems. It introduces coherence susceptibility as a diagnostic tool, decomposes the total QFI after post-selection, and analyzes how different environmental effects (dephasing vs. dissipation) impact the achievable measurement precision.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum information transfer between light-matter-type systems is poised to enable important applications, while also serving as a testbed for theoretical investigation. Such systems can be realized with spins coupled to a mechanical oscillator, a platform that has been extensively studied both theoretically and experimentally. Early demonstrations of quantum information transfer have relied mostly on coherent control. However, measurement-induced backaction has emerged as a strong alternative for quantum control. In this work we use post-selection on the spin system as a selective backaction to refocus spin's phase information onto the mechanical oscillator. We identify physical resources and operating regimes that govern conditional phase transfer, including oscillator quantum coherence, the number of spins, the mechanical initial state, coupling strength, oscillator amplitude, and relaxation. We benchmark different scenarios using the variance as the figure of merit, estimated via two complementary approaches: a semiclassical variance estimator and a Pegg-Barnett quantum estimator. The Cramér-Rao bound is also computed for comparison. The analysis provides a framework for understanding phase transfer in high-dimensional hybrid quantum systems and for measurement-induced backaction used in quantum magnetometry.

</details>

**Authors:** Yichi Zhang, Zhenqi Bai, Xu Zhao, Hongyan Fan, Ximo Wang, Tiecheng Wang  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.13090v1>

**Summary.** The paper proposes a novel, robust method to implement controlled-Z gates on Rydberg atoms using level-crossing-free adiabatic passage. By designing specific pulse sequences with zero net area, the scheme naturally cancels unwanted phases and maintains high fidelity ($>0.999$) even when subjected to realistic experimental noise and dissipation. This makes it a highly promising platform for scalable quantum computation.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work represents a significant advancement in quantum gate implementation by providing an architecture (level-crossing-free echoing RAP) that intrinsically suppresses common sources of error like laser intensity noise and dynamical phase accumulation, making it highly practical for large arrays.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to design a highly robust controlled-Z (CZ) gate for Rydberg atoms that minimizes decoherence and remains functional despite experimental imperfections.

**Main result.** The proposed level-crossing-free echoing rapid adiabatic passage scheme achieves very high fidelity ($0.9999$ for CZ) while being remarkably resilient to laser intensity fluctuations ($\pm 2\%$) and detuning offsets ($\pm 1\%$).

**Method.** The authors employ a double-pulse sequence utilizing antisymmetric Rabi frequency pulses and symmetric detuning pulses, which naturally cancels the dynamical phase via time-reversal symmetry.

**Model / system.** The system involves Rydberg atoms in neutral atom arrays, modeled using the Lindblad master equation to account for dissipation. The gate relies on the strong Rydberg blockade effect restricting dynamics to low-excitation subspaces.

**Key observables.** Gate fidelity ($F$), nonadiabatic transition probability ($P_{	ext{nonad}}$), and population evolution during the gate time ($	au_{	ext{gate}}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Rabi frequency fluctuation ($\pm 2\%$), detuning offset ($\pm 1\%$), blockade interaction strength ($V_0$), and gate duration ($	au_{	ext{gate}}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the system can be effectively described by restricting evolution to low-dimensional subspaces due to the Rydberg blockade, and that the pulse structure ensures zero-area conditions.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate schematic level structures (FIG. 1), pulse profiles for CZ/CCZ gates (FIG. 2(a)), instantaneous eigenenergies showing avoided crossings (FIG. 2(b)), and Bloch sphere trajectories demonstrating controlled phase accumulation.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theory by comparing adiabatic passage schemes, establishing that level-crossing-free zero-area pulses are superior for robustness. It then applies this framework to simulate CZ/CCZ gates under dissipation using the Lindblad master equation, quantifying fidelity and noise resilience.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We propose a controlled-Z gate scheme for Rydberg atoms based on level-crossing-free echoing rapid adiabatic population transfer. We design antisymmetric Rabi frequency pulses and symmetric detuning pulses, enabling the system to completely avoid level-crossing points throughout the evolution, and the dynamical phase is naturally eliminated by the time-reversal symmetry of the double-pulse sequence. We incorporate dissipative effects through the Lindblad master equation. The numerical simulation yields a two-qubit CZ gate fidelity of 0.9999. When the Rabi-frequency fluctuation is within $\pm 2\%$, and the detuning offset is within $\pm 1\%$, the fidelity can still remain above 0.999. Under the same dissipative model, the three-qubit CCZ gate achieves a fidelity of 0.999. When a single-parameter fluctuation does not exceed $\pm 3\%$, the fidelity is always higher than 0.997. Our scheme requires no laser phase jumps or fast switching operations. The zero-area pulse structure suppresses first-order intensity noise, and the symmetric double-pulse sequence avoids spatially resolved laser switching, making it suitable for parallel gate operations in large-scale neutral-atom arrays.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper reports on a quantum repeater node using a multimode quantum memory ($	ext{Yb}^{3+}:	ext{Y}_2	ext{SiO}_5$) to overcome rate limitations in quantum communication. They successfully demonstrated the storage and distribution of entanglement across thousands of temporal modes over both laboratory fiber spools and a real metropolitan network link. This advances the feasibility of long-distance, high-capacity quantum networking.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses scalability issues in quantum networking by demonstrating high-mode count entanglement distribution, which is crucial for realizing large-scale quantum repeaters beyond simple single-photon links.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is achieving practical, high-rate entanglement distribution over metropolitan distances by overcoming rate limitations inherent in single-mode quantum memories.

**Main result.** The authors successfully demonstrated the distribution of entanglement across a 5.66 km metropolitan fiber link while storing and verifying correlations across 8235 temporal modes.

**Method.** They utilized an Atomic Frequency Comb (AFC) quantum memory in a quantum repeater node setup, combining it with Spontaneous Parametric Down-Conversion (SPDC) sources and performing Bell tests to confirm entanglement preservation.

**Model / system.** The platform uses a multimode quantum memory based on the $	ext{}^{171}	ext{Yb}^{3+}:	ext{Y}_2	ext{SiO}_5$ crystal. Entanglement is established between a telecom photon in the fiber and a stored signal photon, with mode capacity quantified via Schmidt decomposition.

**Key observables.** CHSH Bell parameter ($S > 2$), effective temporal mode capacity (up to 16340 modes), coincidence detection rates, and interference fringe visibility ($V$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Memory bandwidth of $250	ext{ MHz}$, storage lifetime ($	ext{AFC}$) of $\sim 76.6\ \mu	ext{s}$, and metropolitan distribution distance up to $5.66	ext{ km}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the fair-sampling assumption for Bell nonlocality revelation, and there is a noted trade-off between coincidence rate and storage time due to memory efficiency decay.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the experimental setup involving SPDC sources, AFC memories, and detection systems. Specific panels show correlation peaks in joint detection histograms and the measurement of entanglement via Bell tests over varying fiber lengths.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the physical system (AFC memory + SPDC source), introduces quantitative measures like Schmidt decomposition for mode capacity, demonstrates broadband storage capabilities using fiber spools, and culminates in a field deployment test over a metropolitan network while verifying nonlocality via CHSH tests.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Entanglement generation between telecommunication photons and matter is central to fibre-based quantum repeaters. Achieving practical communication rates requires multiplexing, which multimode quantum memories can provide. Rare-earth-ion ensembles offer large temporal multimode storage by exploiting the numerous spectral channels within their absorption spectrum. Here, we report on a quantum repeater node comprised of a $^{171}$Yb$^{3+}$:Y$_2$SiO$_5$ multimode quantum memory, featuring a 250 MHz bandwidth and a $76.6~μ\mathrm{s}$ lifetime, and a bandwidth-matched entangled photon-pair source. We introduce and validate a quantitative measure of the effective temporal mode capacity using a Schmidt decomposition. With this platform, we demonstrate entanglement between a telecom photon propagating through a 25.3 km fiber spool and a 979 nm photon stored for $125~μ\mathrm{s}$ across 16340 temporal modes. Finally, we report a field deployment distributing entanglement over 5.66 km through the Geneva metropolitan fibre network while storing 8235 modes for $63~μ\mathrm{s}$.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces pulse engineering as a powerful technique to construct and study effective Hamiltonians for interacting spin chains. By smoothly interpolating between two distinct models (XXX and ZX), the authors demonstrate controlled access to exotic eigenstates exhibiting specific entanglement patterns. This establishes pulse engineering as a general tool for tuning quantum resources in many-body physics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses how external driving fields can be used as a tool to sculpt complex many-body Hamiltonians, offering precise control over emergent quantum features like entanglement structure, which is highly relevant for designing quantum simulators.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central challenge is coherently controlling the properties of interacting quantum many-body systems by systematically engineering their Hamiltonians.

**Main result.** Pulse engineering provides a versatile framework to generate structured Hamiltonians, allowing for controlled transitions between different types of atypical eigenstates (like VBS states and rainbow scar states) while tracking changes in entanglement and nonstabilizerness.

**Method.** The authors use pulse engineering schemes combined with average Hamiltonian theory to derive an effective time-independent Hamiltonian that interpolates between two known models ($\mathcal{H}_{XXX}$ and $\mathcal{H}_{ZX}$).

**Model / system.** The system is a one-dimensional spin-1/2 chain with nearest-neighbor interactions and a local impurity. The analysis compares the chaotic Heisenberg (XXX) model to a target ZX Hamiltonian.

**Key observables.** Entanglement entropy, stabilizer Rényi entropy, nonstabilizerness ('magic'), and spectral statistics ($\langle r angle$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The interpolation parameter $eta$ controls the mixing between the two Hamiltonians; regimes include exchange-dominated ($J/\epsilon > 1$) and impurity-dominated ($J/\epsilon < 1$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the leading-order Magnus approximation for deriving the effective Hamiltonian.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the pulse-driven spin chain, the resulting effective Hamiltonian structure, and show how entanglement/nonstabilizerness evolve across different engineered limits.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem of control in many-body systems, derives the effective Hamiltonian via pulse engineering theory, analyzes the system's spectral properties (chaos vs. non-chaos) across the parameter space, and finally characterizes specific atypical eigenstates and their associated quantum resources.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Understanding and coherently controlling the properties of interacting quantum many-body systems is a central challenge in non-equilibrium quantum physics. While in the past decades, a wide range of many-body Hamiltonians have been introduced to study quantum chaos, atypical eigenstates, and quantum resources, systematically engineering and continuously tuning these properties within a single microscopic model remains largely unexplored. Here, we employ a pulse engineering scheme to construct an effective Hamiltonian that continuously interpolates between a chaotic Heisenberg (XXX) chain with a local impurity and the ZX Hamiltonian. Along this interpolation, we identify several families of analytically tractable atypical eigenstates embedded in the excited-state spectrum with distinct entanglement and nonstabilizerness properties. In the XXX limit, these states exhibit exact plateaus in both entanglement and stabilizer Rényi entropy and correspond to coherent superpositions of long-range valence-bond solid (VBS) states. As the pulse strength increases, the effective Hamiltonians exhibit a hierarchy of new set of approximate entanglement plateaus in the low-energy spectrum. Interestingly, in the fully pulse-engineered ZX limit, we uncover a distinct pair of long-range entangled stabilizer eigenstates, corresponding to rainbow scar states. We further show that pulse engineering preserves the distinct chaotic and non-chaotic regimes of the original model, while that is largely absent in the dynamical generation of entanglement and nonstabilizerness. The pulse-engineered models generate nearly identical quantum resources in both regimes, revealing a partial decoupling between quantum chaos and quantum-resource generation. Our results establish pulse engineering as a versatile framework for generating many-body Hamiltonians with structured eigenstates and tunable quantum resources.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper theoretically investigates stripe supersolidity in a Bose-Hubbard model governed by engineered non-axisymmetric dipolar interactions. It demonstrates that the system's response to doping is highly asymmetric: particle doping stabilizes a true supersolid phase with finite superfluidity, whereas hole doping leads to locked, less coherent states. This behavior hinges critically on the presence of soft-core doublon fluctuations enabled by onsite interaction $U$.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a detailed theoretical framework for realizing and controlling exotic quantum phases like stripe supersolids in controllable lattice systems, directly relevant to cold atom simulations using dipolar interactions.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates whether doping defects (vacancies/interstitials) can become phase coherent without melting the ordered background in a supersolid state, and how this coherence depends on particle vs. hole doping.

**Main result.** The system exhibits an asymmetry: particle doping leads to a stripe supersolid with finite superfluid stiffness ($ho_s>0$) and compressibility ($\kappa>0$), while hole doping results in locked commensurate solids with vanishing $ho_s$.

**Method.** Path-integral Quantum Monte Carlo method utilizing the worm algorithm for simulations.

**Model / system.** A soft-core Bose-Hubbard model on a square lattice featuring an engineered non-axisymmetric dipolar interaction ($V_{ij} \propto (x^2-y^2)/r^5 + 1/r^6$).

**Key observables.** Compressibility ($\kappa$), Superfluid Stiffness ($ho_s$), Double Occupancy ($D$), and the structure factor $S(q)$ to confirm $(q,0)$ stripe order.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The ratio $U/t$ (controlling onsite softness) and the doping level $\delta n = n - 1/2$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the off-site dipolar kernel selects a fixed $(q,0)$ stripe channel, while the finite onsite interaction ($U$) controls defect mobility.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the asymmetric phase diagram in doping space ($\delta n$), showing plateaus on the hole side versus continuous supersolid formation on the particle side. Tables summarize how this DSS state vanishes when the soft-core doublon channel is suppressed by increasing $U/t$ towards the hard-core limit.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the Bose-Hubbard model, first establishing the role of the engineered dipolar interaction in selecting stripe order. It then compares the physical response to particle vs. hole doping, using $ho_s$, $\kappa$, and $D$ as diagnostics to prove the one-sided nature of the supersolid phase.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

A supersolid combines density order with phase coherence, and doped lattice solids ask whether added defects can become coherent without melting the ordered background. We study a soft-core Bose-Hubbard model with isotropic hopping and an engineered non-axisymmetric dipolar interaction, \(V_{ij}=V_2(x_{ij}^2-y_{ij}^2)/r_{ij}^5+W_6/r_{ij}^6\), where the sign-changing \(d_{x^2-y^2}\) component selects a fixed \((q,0)\) stripe channel and the \(W_6/r^6\) core stabilizes the short-distance attractive branch. Using sign-problem-free quantum Monte Carlo method with worm algorithm, we find that the half-filled stripe parent responds asymmetrically to doping: the hole side forms locked commensurate stripe solids with vanishing superfluid stiffness, whereas the particle side forms a stripe supersolid with finite compressibility \(κ>0\), finite superfluid stiffness \(ρ_s>0\), and enhanced double occupancy \(D\). Keeping the same off-site kernel while increasing \(U/t\) toward the hard-core limit shows that the particle-side supersolid disappears once doublon-like defects are projected out. Thus the engineered dipolar kernel selects the fixed \((q,0)\) stripe channel, while onsite softness selects the phase-coherent defect sector.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors introduce a novel, operational witness ($W_Q$) to quantify nonclassicality in qubits by examining violations of Kolmogorov consistency across sequential measurements. They show that while projective measurements fail to detect nonclassicality for diagonal states, generalizing to POVMs allows detection via measurement unsharpness. This provides a robust tool connecting quantum resource theory to experimental measurement protocols.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides an experimentally accessible diagnostic tool that extends beyond standard state-based measures by linking nonclassicality detection directly to the nature (unsharpness) of the quantum measurements themselves.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to develop an operational measure, a nonclassicality witness ($W_Q$), for qubit systems based on detecting violations of Kolmogorov consistency conditions in sequential measurements.

**Main result.** The witness reveals that non-projective Positive Operator-Valued Measures (POVMs) can detect nonclassicality even when the initial state is diagonal, achieving a maximum value of $1/4$.

**Method.** The methodology involves deriving the witness from joint probability distributions calculated using measurement update rules for both projective measurements and general POVMs.

**Model / system.** The system analyzed is a qubit undergoing evolution coupled to an environment (bath), modeled with pure dephasing dynamics. The analysis uses concepts linking Kolmogorov consistency, Leggett-Garg inequalities, and POVM theory.

**Key observables.** Nonclassicality witness ($W_Q$), measurement unsharpness parameter ($a_z$), joint probabilities $P(x_2, t_2; x_1, t_1)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Initial state populations ($\alpha_0, \alpha_1$), dephasing factor ($\chi(\Delta t)$), and the POVM unsharpness parameter ($a_z$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes a Markovian process and models the dynamics using pure dephasing. It compares projective measurements to general POVMs.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the dependence of $W_Q$ on initial populations, dephasing factors, and the measurement unsharpness parameter ($a_z$), showing that $W_Q$ is zero for ideal projective measurements but positive for generalized POVMs.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the witness based on Kolmogorov violation, derives it for two-measurement scenarios, compares results for projective vs. general POVMs, and establishes the maximum value of the witness by connecting it to measurement unsharpness.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We introduce an operational measure of nonclassicality for qubit systems based on the violation of Kolmogorov consistency conditions in sequential measurements. In contrast to previous work by Milz et al.~\cite{Milz-2020}, who characterized classicality via NCGD maps, and Sakuldee et al.~\cite{Sakuldee-2022a, Sakuldee-2022b}, who studied quantum correlations under measurement disturbance, our witness explicitly quantifies nonclassicality in terms of the POVM unsharpness parameter $a_z$. For projective measurements on an initially diagonal state, the witness vanishes identically, showing that such measurements cannot reveal nonclassicality. However, by generalizing to positive operator-valued measures (POVMs), we find that non-projective measurements can reveal nonclassicality even for diagonal initial states. We derive explicit expressions for the witness for general initial states, including coherences, and show that its maximum value is $1/4$, which is a new result achieved for unbiased POVMs, maximal dephasing, and equal initial populations. Our results connect Kolmogorov consistency, Leggett-Garg inequalities, and POVMs, providing an experimentally accessible tool for detecting nonclassicality in qubit systems with potential applications in quantum technology certification.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes quasiparticle poisoning in a periodically driven fluxonium superconducting circuit. It demonstrates that while periodic driving can induce dynamical freezing of the Josephson nonlinearity, this process does not eliminate dissipation from quasiparticles. The authors provide a framework to identify optimal operating regimes by balancing the desired coherent state against detrimental resonance-enhanced quasiparticle loss.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant for AMO/open quantum systems as it models dissipation (quasiparticle loss) in a driven superconducting circuit, providing concrete guidelines for engineering coherence by managing external driving fields.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates quasiparticle poisoning effects in a superconducting fluxonium circuit subjected to periodic driving, determining if dynamic freezing suppresses dissipation.

**Main result.** Dynamic freezing does not generically suppress quasiparticle-induced dissipation; optimal operation requires balancing the drive's tendency toward dynamical freezing against unavoidable quasiparticle loss mechanisms.

**Method.** The authors calculate quasiparticle transition rates using Floquet Fermi's Golden Rule, comparing full Floquet treatments with approximations derived from the Magnus expansion.

**Model / system.** The system is a fluxonium superconducting circuit driven by a time-dependent external magnetic flux. The analysis uses the driven Hamiltonian and examines pair generation and pre-existing quasiparticle tunneling channels.

**Key observables.** Quasiparticle generation rate ($\Gamma_{	ext{pair}}^0$), quasiparticle tunneling rate ($\Gamma_{	ext{tunnel}}^0$), lifetime ($T_{1,01}$), and fidelity ($ar{F}_{01}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Drive frequency ($f_d$), drive amplitude ($\phi_{ac}$), charging energy ($E_C$), Josephson energy ($E_J$), and inductive energy ($E_L$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis often compares results to the Magnus Hamiltonian reference, and later sections assume quasiparticles are promptly removed from the junction or use Boltzmann occupation benchmarks.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the landscape of pair generation rates vs. drive parameters, resonance structures in tunneling rates organized by the Floquet-Magnus spectrum, and a trade-off plot showing lifetime versus fidelity as a function of drive frequency.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the model using the driven Hamiltonian, then calculates both pair-breaking and tunneling rates using Floquet theory. It analyzes how these rates depend on drive parameters, contrasting results with simpler approximations to map out safe operating regimes.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Periodic driving can suppress the Josephson nonlinearity of a fluxonium superconducting circuit, producing a nearly harmonic Floquet spectrum at isolated freezing points [K. Lewellen et al., Newton 2, 100434 (2026)]. Here we show that this dynamically frozen behavior does not generically suppress quasiparticle-induced dissipation in the resulting frozonium circuit. We formulate quasiparticle processes in the frozonium using a Floquet framework and analyze both drive-assisted Cooper-pair breaking and tunneling of pre-existing quasiparticles. Pair generation is controlled by gap-breaking thresholds at high drive frequencies, while multiphoton resonances produce pronounced rate enhancements at lower frequencies. Quasiparticle tunneling exhibits connected resonance structures organized by the harmonic Floquet-Magnus spectrum near the freezing point, with resonant hybridization generating characteristic avoided crossings. Our results show that suitable operating regimes must balance dynamical freezing against quasiparticle loss and provide a framework for identifying experimental drive parameters away from harmful resonances.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper tackles the challenge of simulating non-Markovian dynamics using quantum computation by developing algorithms for Volterra integro-differential equations (VIDEs). It achieves exponential speedup when memory effects are weak ($M < 1$) or when the system's kernel has a specific, structured form. This expands the scope of quantum simulation beyond traditional Markovian models into complex physical systems like those found in open quantum dynamics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses simulating complex open quantum systems where memory effects are crucial, pushing the boundary of what quantum computers can simulate efficiently beyond simple Markovian master equations.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Developing quantum algorithms capable of simulating non-Markovian dynamical systems, which retain memory of past system states.

**Main result.** Efficient quantum algorithms are developed for linear Volterra integro-differential equations (VIDEs) under specific conditions ($M < 1$ or structured kernels), achieving exponential speedup over classical methods in certain regimes.

**Method.** The approach involves recasting the VIDE into a solvable linear system, utilizing Quantum Linear System Algorithms (QLSA) when memory effects are weak, or employing 'Markovianization' to convert the problem into an ODE set for structured kernels.

**Model / system.** The focus is on linear Volterra integro-differential equations (VIDEs), which model non-Markovian dynamics where the evolution depends on a convolution memory kernel $K(t-	au)$. Applications include open quantum systems described by the Mori-Zwanzig formalism.

**Key observables.** Quantum state encoding of the system's state over a time interval or at a specific final time T.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The Memory Strength Parameter ($M$), which characterizes the ratio of memory term strength to Markovian dissipation; regimes include $M < 1$ (short-term memory) and $M \geq 1$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** For general kernels, simulation is conjectured to be intractable when $M \geq 1$; efficiency for large $M$ relies on the kernel being decomposable as a sum of exponentials (SOE).

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the limitation of existing Markovian simulation algorithms, then develops efficient quantum circuits for VIDEs when $M < 1$, and finally introduces 'Markovianization' to extend this capability to structured kernels even when $M \geq 1$.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Existing quantum algorithms for simulating dynamical systems -- from Hamiltonian simulation to linear and nonlinear differential equations solvers -- simulate Markovian dynamics, in which the system's future evolution depends solely on its current state. We turn our attention to developing quantum algorithms for non-Markovian dynamical systems where the system's future evolution depends on its past history and thus has memory. Specifically, we develop efficient algorithms for linear Volterra integro-differential equations (VIDEs) with a convolution memory kernel that output a quantum state encoding the state description over a time interval or at a particular time. Given efficient circuits for the problem inputs, our algorithms achieve an exponential speedup in system size over existing classical algorithms.   We develop an algorithm for general kernels assuming that $\textsf{M} < 1$, where $\textsf{M}$ characterizes the strength of the memory term relative to the dissipation of the Markovian part of the dynamics. We complement this with lower bounds for general-kernel VIDEs when $\textsf{M} \geq 1$, showing that the problem becomes intractable for a family of systems. However, by specializing to structured kernels which admit concise decompositions over exponentials, we develop efficient quantum algorithms even when $\textsf M \geq 1$ by converting the VIDE into a larger set of ODEs, a procedure which we call Markovianization. As an application of the overall framework, we discuss the Mori-Zwanzig formalism used in open quantum systems and fluid dynamics. Overall, our results expand the range of dynamical systems that quantum computers can simulate efficiently.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a comprehensive theory for the boundary physics of a Kondo spin-$s$ chain by combining Boundary Conformal Field Theory with exact Bethe Ansatz and TBA methods. It maps out a rich phase diagram featuring multiple impurity-bound states that reorganize the system's excitation spectrum into distinct towers. The results unify descriptions of screening mechanisms, providing quantitative predictions for thermodynamic quantities like entropy across various coupling regimes.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, unified theoretical toolkit (combining CFT, integrability, and advanced statistical mechanics) to analyze complex quantum impurity problems in 1D correlated systems, which is highly relevant for understanding open quantum systems dynamics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the boundary phases and thermodynamic properties of a Kondo impurity coupled to a strongly correlated spin-$s$ chain, particularly focusing on transitions between overscreened Kondo behavior and localized boundary-bound states.

**Main result.** The system exhibits a rich phase diagram with five distinct regimes (AF1-AF3, F1, F2), characterized by the restructuring of excitation towers in the Hilbert space. The analysis provides a unified description using CFT, Bethe Ansatz, and TBA methods.

**Method.** A combination of Boundary Conformal Field Theory (BCFT) for low-energy universality, exact Bethe Ansatz solutions, generalized Thermodynamic Bethe Ansatz (TBA), and tensor-network simulations are employed to analyze the system across different temperature and coupling regimes.

**Model / system.** The model is a spin-$1/2$ impurity coupled to the boundary of a strongly correlated spin-$s$ Takhtajan–Babujian chain, described by an integrable Hamiltonian. The low-energy physics is related to a perturbed SU(2) WZW conformal field theory.

**Key observables.** Impurity entropy (showing nonmonotonic temperature dependence), impurity spectral function (exhibiting sharp threshold features), and the structure of excitation towers.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The coupling ratios ($J/g$) define five distinct phases ($	ext{AF}1-	ext{AF}3, 	ext{F}1, 	ext{F}2$), which dictate whether the system is in an overscreened Kondo regime or a localized boundary-bound state.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on mapping the problem to integrable models solvable by Bethe Ansatz techniques and developing generalized TBA frameworks to handle multi-tower structures.

**Figures summary.** Figure 2 details the phase diagram showing coupling ranges for five phases, while Table I provides quantitative fingerprints (entropy, residual values) associated with these distinct boundary configurations.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically develops the theoretical framework by first establishing the Hamiltonian and using BCFT for the weak-coupling limit. It then employs exact Bethe Ansatz methods to derive energy eigenvalues, leading into a generalized TBA formulation that accounts for multiple excitation towers across all coupling regimes.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study a spin-$\frac12$ impurity coupled to the boundary of a strongly correlated spin-$s$ Takhtajan--Babujian chain, an integrable model whose low-energy physics is described by a perturbed $SU(2)_{2s}$ Wess--Zumino--Witten conformal field theory. While boundary conformal field theory determines the low-energy universality class of the weak-coupling regime, exact Bethe Ansatz methods reveal a sequence of boundary quantum phase transitions in which impurity-bound states emerge and reorganize the Hilbert space into multiple excitation towers built on distinct boundary configurations. This tower restructuring provides the organizing principle for a rich boundary phase diagram extending beyond the conventional Kondo regime. Weak antiferromagnetic coupling realizes the overscreened $2s$-channel Kondo universality class, whereas stronger couplings generate localized boundary modes and qualitatively new screening mechanisms. To describe the resulting thermodynamics, we develop a generalized thermodynamic Bethe Ansatz framework that captures the multi-tower structure across all regimes. The impurity entropy reproduces the boundary conformal field theory prediction in the overscreened Kondo regime but develops pronounced nonmonotonic temperature dependence once boundary-bound states appear, in quantitative agreement with large-scale finite-temperature matrix-product-operator simulations. Complementary dynamical calculations reveal sharp threshold features in the impurity spectral function that directly track the underlying tower structure. Together, boundary conformal field theory, exact Bethe Ansatz, generalized thermodynamic Bethe Ansatz, and tensor-network simulations provide a unified description of impurity screening, boundary-bound-state formation, and excitation-tower reconstruction in a correlated spin-$s$ chain.

</details>
