**Summary.** This paper rigorously analyzes the conservation laws of three-state IRF cellular automata and their quantum deformations using advanced matrix product methods. It establishes a lower bound on the system's maximal sound speed by analyzing conserved charges derived from both classical rules. The findings provide deep insights into the underlying integrability structure, particularly concerning the emergence of quasi-local invariants in the quantum regime.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous mathematical framework used to constrain conserved quantities in integrable or near-integrable lattice models is highly relevant to understanding dynamics in many-body quantum systems, particularly those exhibiting quasi-periodicity or emergent symmetries.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study focuses on determining the local and quasilocal conservation laws governing three-state Interaction-Round-a-Face (IRF) cellular automata, both classically and through their quantum deformations.

**Main result.** The analysis established a rigorous lower bound for the maximal Euler speed ($\sqrt{3/23}$) for the species-flipping rule, strongly supporting the completeness of known sound-active charges. Furthermore, the quantum deformation yields exact low-bond-dimension invariant states (scar candidates).

**Method.** The research employs patch-matrix-product methods (PMPA) combined with hydrodynamic projection techniques to analyze conserved quantities and their associated Euler velocities.

**Model / system.** The model is a three-state IRF cellular automaton defined on a lattice, featuring two specific rules: species-preserving ($\chi_p$) and species-flipping ($\chi_f$). These classical systems are extended into a coherent quantum deformation.

**Key observables.** Quasi-local conservation laws, Euler velocities (e.g., $\pm\sqrt{3/23}$), low-entanglement invariant states, and the Mazur bound for diagonal quasi-local charges.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis involves a one-parameter family of conserved laws and parameters related to the structure of the PMPA ansatz ($\lambda \in I^*$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The authors note that while they provide strong support for known sound-active charges, unconditional completeness of the full conserved space is not claimed.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates one time step of the IRF circuit using three-site gates; tables summarize the rules for $\chi_p$ and $\chi_f$.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the classical conservation laws using PMPA, derives explicit algebraic forms for conserved quantities across multiple orders (Chunks 3 & 4), and then extends these findings to characterize low-energy states and charges in the quantum deformation.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Using patch-matrix-product methods, we study two reversible three-state interaction-round-a-face cellular automata introduced by Klobas and Prosen [J. Phys. A 55, 094003 (2022)] - the species-preserving and species-flipping rules - and a coherent quantum deformation interpolating between them. Within an explicit translationally invariant ansatz, the two classical rules share a one-parameter family of quasi-local conservation laws with an auxiliary-dimension-three realization. Every regular member is also the first centered logarithmic derivative of an analytic auxiliary-dimension-two family passing through the identity observable. On the closed span of this family and the three elementary local charges, the only nonzero Euler velocities are $\pm\sqrt{3/23}$. Consequently, $\sqrt{3/23}$ is a rigorous lower bound on the maximal Euler speed in any larger conserved sector. For the species-flipping rule, this value agrees with the reported extrapolated value $0.361$ of Klobas and Prosen, strongly supporting completeness of the known sound-active charges. The species-preserving rule admits, in addition, a staggered generating family whose logarithmic derivatives form an infinite tower of strictly local charges. In the quantum deformation, the same algebraic structures yield exact low-bond-dimension invariant states (scar candidates), exponentially long-lived quasi-local quasimodes, and diagonal quasi-local charges that produce a nonzero Mazur bound after projection away from the three elementary local charges.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a rigorous, four-component thermodynamic accounting system to quantify the difference between what is merely stored (memory) and what truly retains operational value in a learning device. By defining 'value' as an achievable work gap, it provides exact theorems governing how this value changes under task shifts or updates. It offers a powerful theoretical tool for analyzing information processing beyond standard generalization bounds.

**Why it may be interesting.** While abstract, the framework provides a deep thermodynamic analogy for information processing. Concepts like 'operational capital' and 'work gap' resonate with non-equilibrium statistical mechanics approaches used to study computation or energy dissipation in complex systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper develops a typed accounting framework to rigorously separate the physical quantity recorded by a finite-state learning device (memory) from the actual operational value that will persist or be useful for future tasks.

**Main result.** It establishes precise, non-trivial bounds and exchange rates governing how 'value' is retained or capitalized during task-distribution shifts, showing that simple measures like record correlation do not capture this full operational value.

**Method.** The authors employ a novel four-component accounting system (fit functional, record-correlation stock, update ledger, and operational capital value) derived from information theory and thermodynamics principles.

**Model / system.** The model is an abstract, finite-state learning device operating under defined protocols ($	ext{Protb}$), involving memory states ($M$), training records ($D$), and future task distributions ($T$). It analyzes the work gap between informed and blind protocol classes.

**Key observables.** Operational capital value $V(M; T, b)$, record-correlation stock $I(M; D)$, capitalization efficiency $\eta_{	ext{cap}}$, retention gap $L_{	ext{gen}}$, and the four component currencies ($C_1$ through $C_4$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Budget ($b$), temperature ($kT$), memory size ($n$), and various functional parameters defining stability regimes (e.g., $\mathrm{flat}^{*}$ regime).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The results are strictly limited to finite-state learning devices operating within a fixed, explicit operational framework; the paper explicitly states it is not a theory of statistical generalization.

**Figures summary.** Figures and tables define the four component currencies and illustrate theorems connecting these accounts, such as showing how different protocols yield distinct energy bounds ($\Delta V$).

**Paper structure.** The structure builds from defining the four-component accounting system to proving three main theorems: Separation (I), Capitalization Ledger (II), and Value Retention (III). The analysis uses specific computational witnesses (S, B, A) to derive these rigorous statements.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

What a finite learning device has recorded and what will hold value for it on future tasks are not the same quantity. We develop a typed accounting for finite-state learning devices that separates four components: a training-side fit functional $Φ_{\mathrm{fit}}$, the record-correlation stock $J_{D}=I(M;D)$, an update-side search ledger $σ_{M}$, and an operational capital value $V(M;T,b)$. This value is the work gap between an informed protocol class and a blind class obtained by deleting the memory-read port and re-optimizing from scratch. (I) Separation: for every $n$, there is a device family on which record correlation and world correlation grow by $n\ln 2$ while the capital gain is exactly zero. In the $\mathrm{flat}^{*}$ regime, data-free updates never increase $V$. (II) Capitalization ledger: an exact $\mathrm{flat}^{*}$ extraction identity and a universal ledger identity give, for (F5$'$)-stable $M$-local updates under a no-discarded-record-correlation condition (f), the bound $η_{\mathrm{cap}}\le 1$ for the capitalization efficiency $η_{\mathrm{cap}}=ΔV/(k T\,σ_{M})$, together with necessary and sufficient conditions for equality. (III) Value retention: for the retention gap $L_{\mathrm{gen}}$ and retention ratio $ρ_{\mathrm{gen}}$ (the former carries no sign constraint; the latter is defined for positive training-side value and is not confined to $[0,1]$) we give a two-layer alignment domain: an exact exchange rate between value and the side-information-adjusted record fit $I(M';D\mid Y)$ without any record-side-information independence assumption, and a raw record-stock exchange rate under a joint side-information neutrality condition $(M,D)\perp Y$, whose boundary is marked by an explicit one-time-pad witness. These are statements about finite-device value retention under task-distribution shift, not a theory of statistical generalization.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces Time-Ordered Free Energy (TOFE) as the fundamental measure of available free energy when extracting work from correlated quantum states under causal limitations. It develops an optimal strategy using dynamic programming that scales linearly with sequence length, providing a powerful tool for analyzing non-Markovian quantum processes.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous, information-theoretic framework for quantifying irreversibility and extractable energy in open quantum systems subject to realistic measurement constraints, which is highly relevant to non-equilibrium thermodynamics in AMO physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is determining the maximum work an agent can extract from a sequence of quantum states generated by a hidden, non-Markovian process while adhering to strict causal constraints.

**Main result.** They introduce Time-Ordered Free Energy (TOFE) as the fundamental measure for available free energy in such systems and develop an optimal strategy solvable via dynamic programming in linear time complexity.

**Method.** The methodology employs a resource-theoretic framework, using dynamic programming over belief states derived from Bayesian inference to find the optimal causal policy.

**Model / system.** The system models quantum state sequences generated by a Hidden Markov Model (HMM), where an agent interacts online with the emitted quantum system.

**Key observables.** Time-Ordered Free Energy (TOFE), Causal Dissipation ($\delta$), and various work extraction rates ($	ext{f}_{	ext{TO}}$, $	ext{w}_{	ext{LO}}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Sequence length $L$, temperature parameter $eta$, and the belief state uncertainty quantified by entropy.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The agent operates under strict causality (actions depend only on past observations), and the DP approach assumes belief states are sufficient statistics for optimization.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the perturbed coin process source, causal networks showing agent-system interaction, and comparisons of work deficit versus calculated causal dissipation across different sequence lengths.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from defining the physical problem (causal work extraction) to developing the computational solution (DP in $O(L)$), establishing key theoretical relationships like Causal Dissipation ($\delta$), and finally addressing implementation details via spectral tagging for unitary updates.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

How much work can an agent extract from a temporal sequence of quantum states when it can only operate online under causal constraints---deciding which energy extraction method to use with knowledge of what it has observed before? Here, we study this problem in the context of quantum state sequences that are potentially non-Markovian---generated by some underlying hidden Markov machine that the agent cannot observe. Using techniques from dynamic programming and computational mechanics, we present a method to identify the provably optimal agent strategy, with time complexity that scales linearly with sequence length. This motivates us to introduce the maximum work such agents can extract---time-ordered free energy(TOFE)---as a fundamental measure of free energy available in a temporally correlated quantum system subject to causal considerations.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces 'finite-window recoverability' as a benchmark to quantify if quantum information seemingly lost from a target qubit is actually displaced into neighboring degrees of freedom. Using a disordered kicked-Ising chain, the authors demonstrate that bounded-depth local control can successfully diagnose this residual information, establishing a crucial diagnostic tool for off-site quantum memory.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous, resource-constrained diagnostic tool for characterizing quantum memory in complex, noisy environments typical of solid-state or engineered quantum systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study develops a benchmark, finite-window recoverability, to determine if quantum information initially stored locally on a qubit remains accessible after apparent loss due to dynamics.

**Main result.** The authors establish that local control can diagnose whether information is merely displaced into nearby degrees of freedom or truly lost, showing positive certified gains in disordered systems.

**Method.** They introduce an operational channel benchmark comparing optimal recovery (unrestricted) against shallow-depth variational recovery using a localized window decoder.

**Model / system.** The primary testbed is a disordered kicked-Ising Floquet chain, analyzed by considering the evolution over multiple Floquet periods and embedding the task in longer open chains.

**Key observables.** Certified gain ($\Delta_{	ext{cert}}^r$), Shallow-accessibility fraction ($\eta^r$), and recovery fidelity ($Q$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Recovery radius ($r$), Decoder depth ($L$), Disorder strength ($W$), and Floquet period count ($D$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The definition of 'local quantum information' is operational, relying on the specified probe ensemble and local-access model.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate optimization reliability across disorder strengths, compare shallow decoder performance against optimal CPTP benchmarks across varying resource limits (radius/depth), and show time-dependence robustness.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the finite-window recoverability benchmark by defining three recovery metrics ($Q_{	ext{opt}}^0$, $Q_{	ext{shallow}}^r$, $Q_{	ext{opt}}^r$). It applies this to a disordered kicked-Ising chain, analyzing gains and accessibility fractions. Finally, it tests the concept on a carrier-deletion task.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

When quantum information initially stored in a local qubit disappears, it need not be lost: it may have moved into nearby degrees of freedom or become inaccessible to shallow local control. We introduce finite-window recoverability as an operational channel benchmark that separates these possibilities. It compares optimal recovery from the target site, recovery by a bounded-depth decoder on a finite window, and the unrestricted optimum for that window. Its operational component, local variational recovery, uses local state preparation, window-local control, and target-qubit Pauli readout to certify recoverable memory beyond the target and quantify how much of the same-window advantage is accessible to shallow control. In a disordered kicked-Ising Floquet chain, a depth-6 decoder on a five-site window realizes $Q^{\mathrm{opt}}_0<Q^{\mathrm{shallow}}_2<Q^{\mathrm{opt}}_2$ across the crossover regime, with positive certified gain for most disorder realizations and substantial shallow-accessibility fractions. The signal differs from target-site persistence and reconstructed coherent-information increments. Positive radius-2 gain also persists when the task is embedded in longer open chains using an independent tensor-network backend. Guided by this hierarchy, we test a carrier-deletion task in which the original target register is reset after the dynamics. A depth-8 decoder repairs the input from a radius-3 surrounding halo with held-out median $F_{\mathrm{avg}}=0.758$, above the single-qubit classical benchmark $2/3$, and outperforms optimal one-, two-, and three-site halo-subwindow counterfactuals. These results establish finite-window recovery as a local-control benchmark for off-site quantum memory, diagnosing both where local quantum information remains and whether bounded-depth control can refocus it.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper develops a dual gauge theory to model turbulent hydrodynamics in 2D superfluids, treating the system via coupled vortex and emergent gauge fields. It successfully derives equations of motion that reproduce known hydrodynamic behaviors, such as Kolmogorov scaling and an inverse energy cascade. This provides a rigorous theoretical framework for understanding quantum turbulence.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work connects superfluid dynamics (a topic often studied with BECs) to advanced field theories (gauge theory), providing a powerful mathematical toolset for understanding non-equilibrium many-body fluid phenomena relevant to quantum gases.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study focuses on theoretically describing the turbulent hydrodynamics of superfluids confined to two spatial dimensions.

**Main result.** The authors show that a dual gauge theory framework successfully models superfluid turbulence, predicting an inverse energy cascade consistent with theoretical expectations for 2D fluids.

**Method.** They employ a dual gauge theory approach, mapping the fluid dynamics onto coupled equations governing emergent U(1) gauge fields and point-like vortices.

**Model / system.** The system is a two-dimensional superfluid described by a complex order parameter $\psi$. Turbulence is modeled using quantized vortices coupled to an emergent 2+1 dimensional U(1) gauge field, leading to dual equations of motion.

**Key observables.** Kinetic energy spectrum $E(k)$, energy transfer fluxes ($\Pi_a^\chi$), and the evolution of magnetic ($b$) and electric ($e$) fields.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The system exhibits distinct scaling regimes, including Kolmogorov's $k^{-5/3}$ law and a predicted inverse energy cascade towards low wavenumbers.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Key approximations include treating vortices as point-like particles and deriving the hydrodynamics from the dual gauge theory in specific limits (e.g., $g 	o \infty$).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate snapshots of vortex clustering, energy spectra confirming power-law scaling consistent with Kolmogorov's law, and time evolution plots showing energy transfer fluxes.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from the classical fluid description to the proposed dual gauge theory framework. It then details the coupled equations for the fields, analyzes the resulting energy balance equation in Fourier space, and finally presents numerical results confirming scaling laws and cascade behavior.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We describe turbulent hydrodynamics of superfluids in two spatial dimensions via the dynamics of point-like vortices coupled to an emergent 2+1 dimensional $U(1)$ gauge field. The cascade of superfluid kinetic energy is equivalently described by a cascade of dual electric field energies. We study superfluid turbulence using the equations of motion of the dual gauge theory in the presence of a drive and dissipation. In the limit that the vortices are point-like, the dual equations of motion directly yield the hydrodynamical equations of the superfluid. We obtain a turbulent cascade consistent with Kolmogorov's scaling law for two dimensional fluid turbulence. We observe clustering of like-signed vortices and compute the kinetic energy flux to show that the turbulent regime exhibits an inverse energy cascade.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper improves a specialized simulator called the SSBM for solving combinatorial optimization problems. It achieves this by modifying the machine's driving conditions to mimic continuous-variable Ising machines and by incorporating a pseudo-annealing effect. These modifications allow the physical simulator to search more effectively, reaching near state-of-the-art solutions.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses how non-equilibrium dynamics (like those in dissipative physical simulators) can be tuned using concepts borrowed from thermal physics (annealing) to solve hard optimization problems, which is highly relevant for quantum computation and complex system modeling.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Developing a high-performance solver for complex combinatorial optimization problems, such as finding the best cut value for K2000.

**Main result.** By restricting driving conditions and adding a pseudo-annealing effect to the SSBM, the system can achieve search performance comparable to known state-of-the-art methods, approaching the best known cut values.

**Method.** The authors analyze the dynamics of the Spontaneous Symmetry Breaking Machine (SSBM) by imposing specific driving condition restrictions and introducing a pseudo-annealing term into its discrete evolution equations.

**Model / system.** The core system is the SSBM, a physical simulator based on spontaneous symmetry breaking phenomena using dissipative elements. Its dynamics are governed by an iteration equation ($\phi_{i+m} = \sin^2(\gamma^2 \phi_i + 	heta_B)$) and pseudo-spin interactions.

**Key observables.** The primary observable is the 'cut value' achieved during the solution search process, alongside the evolution and histogram of pseudo-spin patterns ($\phi_i$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Nesting number (restricted to 12), noise intensity schedule for annealing, and coupling parameters ($\gamma, 	heta_B$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The SSBM is treated as a discrete system, requiring the introduction of a *pseudo*-annealing effect to mimic continuous-variable techniques like those involving Nosé–Hoover methods.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare SSBM behavior under simple restrictions (Point 1) versus when pseudo-annealing (Point 2) is added. The histograms show that adding annealing shifts the cut value distribution toward higher, better values.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the baseline performance of SSBM; it then discusses imposing quasi-static restrictions analogous to continuous IMs; finally, it introduces and validates the addition of a pseudo-annealing effect to further enhance search capability.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

In our previous paper, we described the proposal and validation of a new physical implementation-type simulator (spontaneous symmetry breaking machine (SSBM)) for a combinatorial optimization problem that utilizes a phenomenon dual to the model in which a pseudo-spin many-body system is created by spontaneous symmetry breaking. Furthermore, we performed numerical simulations with different initial fluctuations on a large-scale benchmark problem (K_{2000}) and reported that we identified a condition under which pseudo-spin patterns gradually converge during the solution search process to ultimately form a single pattern and that the cut value of this converged solution reached 99.7% of the known best. In this paper, we discuss the conditions under which the SSBM can exhibit behavior analogous to that of a continuous-variable-type Ising machine, and report that by adding an effect similar to physical annealing to these conditions, the SSBM can be made to search for the best known cuts in K_{2000}.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors present a novel Rydberg-atom-based Mach-Zehnder interferometer using a dual-local-oscillator configuration to measure microwave fields. This setup overcomes limitations of prior methods by achieving high phase resolution and exceptional sensitivity enhancement near critical interference points. The resulting framework is simple, scalable, and applicable for multi-parameter metrology including distance and polarization sensing.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a highly scalable quantum platform for microwave metrology by engineering an interference scheme that converts weak phase shifts into large measurable intensity changes, which is crucial for advancing practical quantum sensors.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core challenge is achieving high-precision phase measurement of microwave fields while overcoming trade-offs in resolution, range, and system complexity inherent in existing Rydberg-atom sensing methods.

**Main result.** The proposed dual-LO Mach-Zehnder interferometer achieves direct phase retrieval with $>0.1^\circ$ resolution and demonstrates a sensitivity enhancement exceeding 25 dB near the critical interference point.

**Method.** The technique uses two local oscillators to create two coherent interferometric pathways in the Rydberg medium, whose recombination forms an intermediate-frequency signal sensitive to the input microwave phase.

**Model / system.** The system is a Mach-Zehnder type interferometer implemented using Rydberg atoms. It employs a dual-local-oscillator (dual-LO) configuration that establishes two coherent pathways for mixing with the signal field, avoiding complex optical setups.

**Key observables.** Phase ($\phi$), intensity ($I_{	ext{Itf}}$), propagation distance ($\delta d$), and polarization angle ($	heta$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Phase resolution ($>0.1^\circ$), sensitivity enhancement ($>25 	ext{ dB}$), distance precision ($< 20 \mu	ext{m}$ at $5.7 	ext{ GHz}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes the ability to establish and control two coherent LO pathways and relies on operating near a critical interference point for maximum sensitivity.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the energy-level diagram, the dual-LO setup schematic, the coherent mixing process forming $	ext{IF}_{	ext{total}}$, and response curves demonstrating phase retrieval across different resolution regimes (LR, MR, HR).

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem of microwave metrology, details the dual-LO Mach-Zehnder interferometer model, derives the intensity dependence on accumulated phase, quantifies the enhanced sensitivity near critical points, and finally demonstrates applications to distance and polarization sensing.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

High-precision phase measurement of microwave fields underpins a wide range of applications, including wireless communications, distributed radar, plasma diagnostics, and antenna metrology. Existing Rydberg-atom-based approaches, however, often face trade-offs among phase resolution, measurement range, and system complexity. Here we demonstrate a Rydberg-atom-based microwave Mach-Zehnder-type interferometer using a dual-local-oscillator configuration. The two local oscillators establish two coherent interferometric pathways in the Rydberg medium. Their coherent mixing with the signal field produces an interferometric intermediate-frequency output governed by a phase-to-intensity transfer characteristic that enables critical-point enhancement. This scheme supports direct phase retrieval with a resolution exceeding $0.1^\circ$ and unambiguous full $360^\circ$ phase coverage with the reconfigurable dual-LO architecture. Moreover, near the critical interference point, the system exhibits a sharply enhanced phase-to-amplitude transduction, where weak amplitude variations are converted into pronounced phase responses, yielding a sensitivity enhancement exceeding 25 dB. Besides, the same interferometric transfer mechanism enables microwave propagation-distance and polarization metrology, achieving a propagation-distance precision below 20 $μ$m at 5.7 GHz together with a polarization-angle resolution exceeding $0.1^\circ$. This approach eliminates the need for complex optical configurations and lock-in detection, providing a simple, scalable, and reconfigurable Mach-Zehnder-type quantum microwave interferometry framework for multifunctional high-precision microwave metrology.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper investigates how entanglement—both bipartite and genuine multipartite—evolves in two-walker discrete-time quantum walks. Using advanced measures like the GGM, the authors demonstrate that open boundary conditions naturally lead to the generation of maximal, robust multipartite entanglement. This establishes these quantum walk systems as promising tools for quantum information processing.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses quantum information processing by providing a physical platform (quantum walks) where multipartite correlations can be engineered robustly, which is highly relevant for quantum simulation and computation.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study focuses on characterizing the generation, evolution, and robustness of genuine multipartite entanglement in composite quantum systems using two-walker discrete-time quantum walks.

**Main result.** Maximal genuine multipartite entanglement generation is shown to be a robust and generic feature of open-boundary two-walker DTQWs, making them promising platforms for engineering quantum correlations.

**Method.** The authors employ logarithmic negativity to track bipartite entanglement and the generalized geometric measure (GGM) to quantify genuine multipartite entanglement dynamics.

**Model / system.** The model is a two-walker discrete-time quantum walk on a one-dimensional lattice, incorporating both internal (coin) and external (position) degrees of freedom. The analysis compares open-boundary (free movement) versus closed-boundary (periodic) regimes.

**Key observables.** Logarithmic negativity (for bipartite entanglement) and the Generalized Geometric Measure (GGM) for genuine multipartite entanglement.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Lattice topology (open vs. closed boundary), initial Bell state, and local coin operator choice (e.g., Hadamard or Pauli-X).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the validity of using DTQWs as a model for quantum correlation transport and relies on specific mathematical measures to quantify entanglement.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the one-dimensional lattice structure, showing periodic boundary conditions, and demonstrate time evolution plots of GGM under different coin operators (e.g., Pauli-X).

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes entanglement dynamics by first defining the two-walker DTQW framework, then comparing correlation redistribution in open versus closed boundaries, culminating in demonstrating the robustness of maximal GME generation.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Discrete-time quantum walks provide a versatile framework for investigating the generation, redistribution, and transport of quantum correlations in composite quantum systems. Here, we study the dynamics of bipartite and genuine multipartite entanglement in a two-walker discrete-time quantum walk on a one-dimensional lattice. By employing logarithmic negativity and the generalized geometric measure (GGM), we systematically characterize the redistribution of bipartite entanglement among different subsystem partitions and the emergence of genuine multipartite entanglement involving the two coin and two position degrees of freedom. We show that the entanglement dynamics are strongly influenced by the lattice topology. The open-boundary regime exhibits a monotonic redistribution of quantum correlations, whereas the closed-boundary regime gives rise to pronounced oscillatory behavior due to boundary-induced interference and recurrent wave-packet overlap. In the open-boundary regime, the GGM rapidly approaches its theoretical maximum value of $1/2$ and remains largely insensitive to the choice of the initial Bell state as well as to continuous variations of the local coin operator over a broad parameter range, except near the Pauli-$X$ coin. These results demonstrate that maximal genuine multipartite entanglement generation is a robust and generic feature of open-boundary two-walker discrete-time quantum walks, establishing them as promising platforms for engineering multipartite quantum correlations in quantum information processing and quantum simulation.

</details>

**Summary.** This work proposes the Spectral Localization Principle, stating that extractable entanglement depends only on how localized the field's spectral density is. Using an analytically solvable model of qubits coupled to a leaky cavity, the authors derive a closed-form expression for maximum concurrence based on the parameter $Q$. This framework bridges deterministic gate operations with vacuum harvesting by drawing formal analogies to localization phenomena.

**Why it may be interesting.** It directly connects concepts from condensed matter physics (Anderson localization, IPR) to quantum information theory (entanglement harvesting), providing a quantitative diagnostic tool for vacuum correlations observable in superconducting circuit QED experiments.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To establish a unified physical principle—the Spectral Localization Principle—governing how much entanglement can be extracted from a quantum field by localized detectors.

**Main result.** The maximum harvestable concurrence, $C_{\max}(Q)$, is determined solely by the spectral localization parameter $Q$, showing robustness in the high-$Q$ limit and exponential decay in the low-$Q$ limit.

**Method.** Analytical derivation of the maximum concurrence using an analytically solvable model and applying adiabatic elimination to derive an effective master equation for the qubits.

**Model / system.** The system consists of two qubits coupled to a leaky single-mode cavity, which is itself coupled to a continuous electromagnetic bath. The dynamics are governed by a Lindblad master equation after eliminating the cavity degrees of freedom.

**Key observables.** Maximum harvestable concurrence ($\mathcal{C}_{\max}$), which quantifies accessible vacuum correlations.

**Important parameters / regimes.** $Q \equiv |\Delta|/\kappa$, the ratio of qubit-cavity detuning to cavity linewidth; $\kappa$ (linewidth); IPR (Inverse Participation Ratio).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The model assumes a Born-Markov approximation and uses adiabatic elimination, which requires a large separation between qubit and cavity timescales.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the principle, models the system using coupled qubits/cavity/bath Hamiltonian, derives the master equation via adiabatic elimination, calculates $C_{\max}(Q)$ in closed form, and analyzes its behavior in high-Q (deterministic) and low-Q (vacuum harvesting) limits.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We propose a unified physical principle for entanglement harvesting: the entanglement that two localized detectors can extract from a quantum field is determined solely by how localized the field's effective spectral density is. We demonstrate this in an analytically solvable model of two qubits coupled to a leaky single-mode cavity, which in turn couples to a continuous electromagnetic bath, and derive the maximum harvestable concurrence in closed form, $\mathcal{C}_{\max}(Q)=2e^{-π/(2Q)}(1+e^{-π/(2Q)})/(1+3e^{-π/Q})$, where $Q\equiv|Δ|/κ$ is the ratio of the qubit-cavity detuning $Δ$ to the cavity linewidth $κ$. In the high-$Q$ limit, $\mathcal{C}_{\max}\simeq1-π^{2}/(16Q^{2})$, so the entanglement is robust against cavity loss; in the low-$Q$ limit it decays exponentially to zero, consistent with the irreversible-reservoir character of a continuous field, where maximal entanglement is unattainable. Since $Q$ is proportional to the inverse participation ratio (IPR) of the effective spectral density, it is the single dimensionless parameter governing the crossover from deterministic gate-based entanglement ($Q\to\infty$) to vacuum harvesting ($Q\to0$). Our framework operationalizes the Reeh-Schlieder theorem by quantifying the fraction of vacuum correlations accessible to localized detectors. It also reveals a formal correspondence of the maximal concurrence with the IPR, analogous to the conductivity-participation-ratio relation in Anderson localization. The predicted $\mathcal{C}_{\max}(Q)$ curve is, in principle, directly observable in superconducting circuit QED experiments.

</details>

**Summary.** This work establishes that coupling a single controllable qubit to a conventional sensor can yield an exponential quantum advantage for learning classical signals. Using the novel QΨ theory, the authors demonstrate this capability experimentally with superconducting qubits, showing massive reductions in measurement requirements for tasks like Fourier analysis and signal correlation estimation.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a concrete, near-term pathway to quantum advantage by showing that simple systems (single qubit + sensor) can exponentially enhance fundamental signal processing tasks like spectral analysis and time-series learning, which is highly relevant for quantum sensing applications in AMO physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper demonstrates an exponential quantum advantage for learning classical signals, such as Fourier coefficients or temporal correlations, by coupling a single controllable qubit to a conventional sensor.

**Main result.** Quantum Feature Sensing (QFS) protocols achieve exponential reductions in the required number of measurements compared to classical or standard quantum methods, demonstrated with up to $10^7$-fold improvements experimentally.

**Method.** The authors introduce Quantum Phase-Space Inference (QΨ), a unifying theory that derives optimal quantum-enhanced learning algorithms and rigorous lower bounds for sensing tasks.

**Model / system.** The primary platform is a superconducting cavity–qubit architecture. The theoretical framework models the signal estimation by characterizing the probability density $P(\alpha)$ in phase space, extending beyond standard quantum Fisher information limits.

**Key observables.** Prediction accuracy (e.g., 70% for Fourier-amplitude learning), query complexity ($O(k)$ vs $\exp(\Omega(k))$ queries), and Accessible Feature Information ($	ext{AFI}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The advantage is shown to hold using only a single ancilla qubit, making it relevant for near-term quantum technology.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The protocols are designed to be robust even in the presence of decoherence and noise (Markovian, white, or thermal).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the exponential advantage: Fig. 2(c) shows QFS achieving high accuracy with few shots compared to conventional Gaussian approaches; Fig. 4 demonstrates superior performance for sensing dark matter angles and identifying wireless communication symbols.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from a general overview of quantum sensing (Fig. 1), introduces the theoretical framework ($	ext{Q}\Psi$), proves exponential advantages for specific tasks like Fourier coefficient learning (Theorem 1) and temporal correlation estimation using memory (Theorem 3), and concludes with experimental validation on superconducting qubits.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum technology has the potential to transform scientific discovery, but quantum advantages often require processing capabilities well beyond the reach of experimental platforms. We show that coupling a single controllable qubit to an otherwise conventional sensor can exponentially reduce the number of measurements required to learn classical signals. These rigorous quantum advantages apply to fundamental sensing tasks, including learning Fourier coefficients, extracting temporal correlations from time-varying signals, and estimating transformations of physical observables. Using a superconducting cavity--qubit architecture, we experimentally demonstrate $10^7$-fold reductions in the number of measurements required for Fourier-amplitude and time-varying signal learning. Our $\textit{quantum feature sensing}$ algorithms further enable orders-of-magnitude improvements in simulations of weak-signal dark matter detection and wireless communication applications. These quantum advantages are derived from Quantum Phase-Space Inference (Q$Ψ$), a unifying theory of quantum-enhanced experiments that simultaneously converts a set of experimental objectives and constraints into tight lower bounds and optimal quantum-enhanced learning algorithms while producing a certificate of quantum advantage. Q$Ψ$ extends beyond the regimes captured by quantum Fisher information and provides a framework for systematically identifying rigorous quantum advantages in practical experimental tasks. Together, our results establish that near-term quantum technology can exponentially enhance our ability to learn from classical signals.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces Quantum Phase Bicoherence (QPBC) spectroscopy, a novel quantum-inspired technique designed to measure orientation order in complex images by probing gauge-invariant phase relationships. By encoding image sectors into an entangled qubit state and measuring three-body bicoherence, the method generates a robust descriptor that outperforms classical statistics for classifying biological textures across multiple modalities.

**Why it may be interesting.** While applied to biology, the core technique relies on simulating complex quantum interference measurements (bicoherence) to extract robust phase information that is fundamentally inaccessible classically. This methodology of mapping classical physical constraints onto quantum observables is highly relevant for developing new measurement protocols in AMO physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Classical image analysis fails to capture structurally meaningful, gauge-invariant orientation signatures encoded in Fourier phase due to local cellular rotations.

**Main result.** The proposed Quantum Phase Bicoherence (QPBC) spectroscopy successfully resolves angular-phase order and discriminates distinct biological phenotypes across multiple multi-modal datasets with high statistical significance.

**Method.** It introduces a quantum-interferometric framework that embeds image angular sectors into an entangled qubit state to probe three-body bicoherence, yielding a 16-dimensional descriptor vector.

**Model / system.** The analysis is applied to multi-modal microscopic imaging datasets (fluorescence, bright-field, histopathology) containing biological textures exhibiting directional order. The method simulates quantum interference on an abstract qubit register representing angular sectors.

**Key observables.** The 16-dimensional QPBC vector ($	ext{f}_{	ext{QPBC}}$), which measures gauge-invariant angular phase coupling via three-body bicoherence $\cos(\phi_a + \phi_c -2\phi_b)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Optimal probing frequency convergence to $(0, 1)$ after principal-axis alignment; the number of sectors ($n_d=8$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that quantum interference mechanisms can support invariant nonlinear phase composition where classical real-valued pipelines fail.

**Figures summary.** Figures schematically illustrate the four stages: Sectorisation $ightarrow$ Quantum Encoding $ightarrow$ Three-body Bicoherence Measurement $ightarrow$ Final Vector Output, demonstrating the process flow.

**Paper structure.** The paper follows a structure detailing the scientific limitation (classical phase loss), introducing QPBC as the solution, describing the multi-stage quantum simulation methodology, validating it across three diverse datasets using rigorous statistical testing, and concluding with its utility in establishing 'quantum morphometry'.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Classical image analysis routinely discards structurally meaningful orientation signatures encoded within Fourier phase, which are easily corrupted by local cellular rotation. Although quantum-inspired data processing offers new avenues for complex signal characterization, practical tools for directly extracting gauge-invariant angular correlations without explicit phase reconstruction remain scarce. Here we introduce Quantum Phase Bicoherence (QPBC) spectroscopy, a novel quantum-interferometric framework for capturing gauge-invariant angular order. The method embeds image angular sectors into a nine-qubit entangled state and probes three-body bicoherence via an ancilla, yielding 16 interpretable readout channels. We validate our framework on three independent public multi-modal imaging datasets covering fluorescence (BBBC021), bright-field (BBBC041) and histopathology (PathMNIST). QPBC consistently resolves angular-phase order and discriminates distinct biological phenotypes with high statistical significance. After principal-axis alignment, the optimal probing frequency universally converges, driven by Fourier directional sensitivity; negative-control experiments fully eliminate discriminative capacity, demonstrating frequency tuning acts as an on-off switch. Cross-dataset benchmarks confirm QPBC outperforms conventional Fourier-phase statistics, where inherent inversion symmetry serves as a built-in pipeline self-check. QPBC delivers a universal, classically unachievable quantitative texture observable, establishes interpretable quantum morphometry, and broadens the toolbox for quantum-inspired analysis applicable to diverse multi-modal microscopic measurements.

</details>

**Summary.** This work rigorously analyzes how symmetries emerge in Self-Organized Criticality models by examining different spatial and temporal scales. It establishes a profound connection between the sandpile model's relaxation process and solutions to the Monge-Ampère equation from optimal transport theory. This provides a powerful mathematical framework for understanding symmetry emergence in complex, non-equilibrium physical systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical SOC, the deep connection established between critical phenomena and advanced PDEs like the Monge-Ampère equation provides powerful mathematical machinery applicable to non-equilibrium quantum systems or complex many-body dynamics where symmetry breaking is key.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the emergence of affine symmetry in Self-Organized Criticality (SOC) models, particularly when analyzed across different mathematical scales.

**Main result.** The scaling limit of the toppling function is shown to be a unique concave solution to the Monge-Ampère equation, linking SOC dynamics to optimal transport theory and differential geometry.

**Method.** The analysis employs rigorous mathematical tools including scaling limits, tropical geometry, and solving non-linear partial differential equations like the Monge-Ampère equation.

**Model / system.** The system is based on a prototypical Abelian sandpile model defined on a compact convex domain in $\mathbb{R}^2$. The analysis compares micro (Abelian), meso (Tropical), and macro scales of this critical process.

**Key observables.** The toppling function (odometer) and the deviation of density from its maximal value are key observables, which converge to solutions of the Monge-Ampère equation.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis focuses on the regime where the inverse square of the lattice mesh ($1/h^2$) is much larger than the number of random perturbation points.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The results rely on specific scaling limits and assumptions regarding the relative sizes of lattice discretization versus external perturbations in the maximal density regime.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates scale invariance by showing the restoration of the neutral element of the sandpile group. Figure 2 compares defect structures resulting from localized vs. uniform perturbations.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by establishing a mathematical hierarchy: first analyzing microscale Abelian dynamics, then bridging to mesoscale tropical models via scaling limits, and finally characterizing the macroscale limit using solutions to the Monge-Ampère equation.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We describe a mechanism of affine symmetry emergence in the maximal density regime of the prototypical model of self-organized criticality when the inverse square of the mesh of the underlying lattice is much larger than the number of random perturbation points distributed according to a prescribed probability measure supported in the interior of the ambient convex domain. Moreover, an appropriate scaling limit of the toppling function (aka odometer), which counts the number of operations per site, is a solution to a non-linear partial differential equation well known in the context of optimal transport and differential geometry, making it possible to accurately estimate the deviation of the density from its maximal value in any macroscopic window. The mechanism for the affine symmetry emergence is due to the novel empirical fact, supported in addition by inductive arguments that have recently being upgraded to a rigorous proof, that the scaling limit of the toppling function is the unique concave solution of the Monge-Ampère equation with Dirichlet boundary condition on the convex domain with the potential given by the probability measure used above as the infinite-perturbation profile.

</details>

**Summary.** This work shows that the confinement of fractionalized excitations, modeled after Polyakov's mechanism in gauge theory, can drive a Dirac Spin Liquid into a robust Symmetry-Protected Topological (SPT) phase. By analyzing lattice models and using diagnostics like Wilson loops, the authors confirm this transition while simultaneously revealing protected edge physics via spin pump measurements.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a concrete mechanism—gauge field dynamics—for realizing topological order in strongly correlated magnets, linking gauge theory concepts directly to observable condensed matter phenomena like spin-Hall effects.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core challenge is understanding how exotic, fractionalized excitations emerging from quantum spin systems reconfine into stable Symmetry-Protected Topological (SPT) phases.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrate that Polyakov's confinement mechanism can dynamically transform a Dirac Spin Liquid into an SPT state by confining bulk spinons while preserving gapless edge modes.

**Method.** The analysis combines field theory techniques, including mean-field approximations and Hubbard-Stratonovich transformations to derive dual Lagrangians, with numerical diagnostics like Wilson loop calculations and entanglement entropy.

**Model / system.** The study uses quantum spin systems on a triangular lattice, starting from a Dirac Spin Liquid (DSL) model involving coupled spinons and an emergent U(1) gauge field. The Hamiltonian includes Heisenberg, $J_2$, and Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya interactions.

**Key observables.** Wilson loop expectation value $\langle W[C] angle$ (diagnosing confinement), Topological Entanglement Entropy ($\gamma$), and the spin-pump response encoding Berry curvature.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The ratio $J_2/J_1$, the DM interaction strength $D$, and the magnetic flux threading $\phi_z$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on projecting the theory via a Gutzwiller operator to enforce single-occupancy constraints, leading to compact gauge fields.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the mean-field Ansatz, flux insertion effects, and calculations comparing Wilson loop scaling for DSL vs. SPT states, alongside Berry curvature distributions from spin pump measurements.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the theoretical framework (DSL $	o$ Chern-Simons theory), then applying confinement diagnostics (Wilson loops/TEE) to identify the transition to an SPT state, and finally probing the protected edge physics using magnetic field response (spin pump).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

A main theme in modern condensed matter physics is the emergence of fractionalized excitations and gauge structures from quantum spin systems. However, understanding how these exotic degrees of freedom reconfine into new phases of matter remains a fundamental challenge. In this work, we demonstrate that Polyakov's confinement mechanism-a foundation of compact gauge theory-can serve as a dynamical engine to transform a Dirac spin liquid into a symmetry-protected topological (SPT) state. Starting with a Dirac spin liquid with spin-dependent gauge fluxes, we show that the single-occupancy constraint inherent to the physical Hilbert space triggers monopole condensation, dynamically confining bulk spinons while preserving gapless edge modes-realizing a spinon analog of the quantum spin Hall effect. Using a large-scale variational Monte Carlo simulation on a triangular antiferromagnet with an additional Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya interaction, we provide microscopic evidence for this confined SPT phase, including a characteristic area law for the Wilson loop and vanishing topological entanglement entropy. Furthermore, we identify a measurable spin-pump response under magnetic fields, which directly encodes the Berry curvature of the parent Dirac cones. Our results reveal a previously unexplored pathway to SPT physics, bridging the fields of gauge theory, quantum magnetism, and topological matter.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper addresses the challenge of analytically continuing imaginary-time correlation functions (iTCFs) to real frequencies, an inherently ill-posed problem. By employing a diffusion model framework, the authors move beyond simple regression to learn the entire probability distribution of possible spectra consistent with the input iTCF. This allows for rigorous quantification of uncertainty and assessment of the physical ambiguity in the measurement.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, theoretically grounded tool for analyzing spectral data in quantum many-body physics by moving beyond single-point estimates to quantify inherent physical uncertainties arising from ill-posed inverse problems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is the ill-posed analytic continuation of imaginary-time correlation functions (iTCFs) to estimate real-frequency power spectra.

**Main result.** The authors introduce a diffusion-based generative framework that models the full probability distribution of plausible spectra, allowing for direct quantification of uncertainty and assessment of inversion hardness via an 'uncertainty pseudo-volume.'

**Method.** They adapt conditional diffusion models (like DiT) to learn $P(C(\omega)|G(	au))$, treating the continuation as a generative sampling task rather than a single regression point estimate.

**Model / system.** The methodology is applied to iTCFs derived from path-integral molecular dynamics simulations, exemplified by liquid parahydrogen. The underlying physics involves relating imaginary-time correlation functions $G(	au)$ to the real-frequency power spectrum $C(\omega)$.

**Key observables.** Self-diffusion coefficient (with error bar), reconstructed power spectra $C(\omega)$, and the uncertainty pseudo-volume.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Imaginary time $	au \in [0, eta\hbar]$, diffusion time steps ($N=40$), and the conditioning input channels (raw iTCF, log(iTCF), normalized signal, histogram equalization).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that the true solution space of compatible spectra can be accurately modeled by a continuous probability distribution learned via diffusion processes.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the non-uniqueness of the inverse Laplace transform using synthetic data. Figure S1 shows Principal Components (PCs) derived from an ensemble, quantifying the variance explained by successive components.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the ill-posed nature of iTCF continuation. It critiques existing NN regression methods for ignoring solution uncertainty. The core contribution is presenting and detailing the diffusion model framework, including its conditional training objective, advanced sampling techniques (DPM-Solver++), and multi-channel input representation.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Inverse problems are ubiquitous in physics, chemistry, and engineering, arising when reconstructing hidden quantities from indirect measurements. A key example is the analytic continuation of imaginary-time correlation functions (iTCFs) to the real-frequency domain. This process requires an inverse Laplace transform, which is inherently ill-posed and highly sensitive to small input variations. Recent neural network (NN)-based methods have shown promising results by learning mappings from imaginary-time to real-frequency spectra, often outperforming traditional techniques such as maximum entropy. However, because the problem is ill-posed, many spectra fit the same iTCF. Regression-based approaches output a single solution, which approximates an average over the true solution space, and therefore fail to capture the full distribution of plausible power spectra. To address this issue, we introduce a diffusion-based framework for analytic continuation that learns the distribution of spectra consistent with a given iTCF. It offers two key advantages. First, it quantifies uncertainty directly from the learned distribution. Second, by analyzing the spread and structure of this distribution, we can quantitatively assess the intrinsic hardness of each inversion problem. We measure this hardness with a new metric, the uncertainty pseudo-volume. Applying the framework to an iTCF from a path-integral molecular dynamics simulation of liquid parahydrogen, we obtain the self-diffusion coefficient with an error bar and flag a secondary high-frequency peak as a possible spurious artifact. In contrast to previous attempts at uncertainty quantification, our generative approach rests on a concrete probabilistic basis, providing a more theoretically grounded measure of confidence in the reconstructed power spectra.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes the decoherence of asymmetric N-qubit W states by separating the effects of network geometry from the excitation sector. Using closed-form concurrence dynamics under various noise models, the authors show that different pair classes exhibit distinct vulnerabilities to noise. The work establishes that entanglement robustness is governed by three independent factors: geometry, excitation sector, and noise symmetry.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a rigorous theoretical framework to decouple structural properties (geometry vs. excitation) governing entanglement decay in complex quantum networks, which is crucial for designing robust quantum communication protocols.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how network geometry and excitation sector independently govern the decay of pairwise entanglement within asymmetric N-qubit W states.

**Main result.** The analysis distinguishes between structural fragility (seen in Base-Base pairs) and cross-sector effects, establishing that geometry, sector, and noise symmetry are distinct controls on entanglement robustness.

**Method.** The authors derived closed-form concurrence dynamics for generalized asymmetric W states under various one-sided local quantum noise models.

**Model / system.** The system involves asymmetric multipartite N-qubit W states ($\langle W_L angle_N$), which are compared against symmetric references ($|W_Nangle$). The analysis uses an N-qubit generalization of the Lohmayer geometry.

**Key observables.** Pairwise entanglement decay, measured by concurrence; comparison between Vertex-Base (VB) and Base-Base (BB) pair classes.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Noise types include Phase Damping (PD), Amplitude Damping (AD), Depolarization (DP), and Generalized Amplitude Damping (GAD). The system size N is a key parameter.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on a one-sided local-noise convention, where the noise channel acts only on one qubit in any bipartite subsystem.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare AD concurrence decay for symmetric vs. asymmetric states and illustrate how ESD thresholds depend on N under different noise models.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically compares decoherence dynamics of symmetric versus asymmetric W states by separating network geometry from the excitation sector, deriving closed-form concurrences for various pair classes.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate how network geometry and excitation sector separately control pairwise entanglement decay in asymmetric multipartite $W$ states. To disentangle these effects, we introduce an analytically tractable $N$-qubit generalization of the asymmetric Lohmayer geometry and its complementary-excitation partner, yielding inequivalent vertex-base (VB) and base-base (BB) pair classes that can be compared directly with symmetric $W$-state references. We derive closed-form concurrence dynamics under representative one-sided noise models and find that, within either excitation sector, the VB concurrence has exactly the same noise dependence as the corresponding symmetric reference, preserving a noise-independent proportional advantage wherever both remain entangled. The amplitude-damping reordering previously identified for the three-qubit Lohmayer state is therefore a cross-sector effect rather than an intrinsic fragility of the VB geometry. In contrast, the BB pair exhibits a genuine same-sector structural fragility, with lower entanglement-sudden-death thresholds than the VB pair under depolarizing noise and, in the $(N-1)$-excitation sector, under amplitude damping. The results establish network geometry, excitation sector, and noise symmetry as distinct ingredients governing pairwise entanglement robustness in asymmetric quantum networks.

</details>

Each vertex of Γ has a Majorana fermion (red dot), and each honeycomb lattice plaquette p has a qubit σp. We also place a fictitious qubit on each triangle plaquette, whose state is fixed by the majority rule of three qubits surrounding the triangle. The Z2 domain wall forms a closed loop (thick blue line).</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** This work develops a rigorous framework to characterize fermionic 't Hooft anomalies on quantum lattices using anomaly indices derived from the obstruction to symmetric Short-Range-Entangled states. The key finding is that lattice constraints are often stricter than continuum predictions, revealing systematic mismatches between microscopic lattice symmetries and their macroscopic QFT counterparts.

**Why it may be interesting.** The mismatch found between lattice and continuum anomalies provides crucial theoretical constraints for designing physical models that realize specific topological phases or symmetries, which is vital for understanding emergent phenomena in strongly correlated electron systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper develops a lattice characterization of fermionic 't Hooft anomalies associated with finite internal symmetries in (1+1)D and (2+1)D.

**Main result.** It establishes that exact lattice symmetries do not always realize the full continuum anomaly structure, demonstrating mismatches between lattice obstructions (like forbidding symmetric SRE states) and continuum predictions.

**Method.** The analysis uses group cohomology to define hierarchy of fermionic anomaly indices ($(n_2, 
u_3)$ or $(n_2, n_3, 
u_4)$) derived from the obstruction to finding a symmetric Short-Range-Entangled (SRE) state on lattice tensor product Hilbert spaces.

**Model / system.** The system consists of lattice models formed by the tensor product of onsite fermionic and bosonic Hilbert spaces. The symmetry is described by a finite group $G_f$ via a central extension $\mathbb{Z}_2 	o G_f 	o G_b$.

**Key observables.** Anomaly indices ($(n_2, 
u_3)$ in 1D; $(n_2, n_3, 
u_4)$ in 2D), and the existence/obstruction of symmetric SRE states.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimensionality (1+1)D or (2+1)D, and the structure of the bosonic group $G_b$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on characterizing symmetries via obstructions to SRE states and comparing these lattice invariants with continuum QFT anomaly classifications.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by developing the index machinery for (1+1)D, showing how trivial indices imply onsiteability. It then extends this to (2+1)D, identifying multiple anomaly layers and culminating in explicit constructions demonstrating mismatches between lattice and continuum anomalies.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We develop a lattice characterization of fermionic 't Hooft anomalies of finite internal symmetries in (1+1)D and (2+1)D, formulated in terms of obstructions to symmetric short-range-entangled (SRE) states. We consider lattice systems formed by tensor product of onsite fermionic and bosonic Hilbert spaces, and finite internal symmetry given by a central extension $\mathbb Z_2^F\to G_f\to G_b$. We extract a hierarchy of fermionic anomaly indices for a given symmetry operator. In (1+1)D, an exact lattice symmetry is characterized by a pair of cohomological data $(n_2,ν_3)$. For $G_f=G_b\times\mathbb Z_2^F$, we show that a symmetry with trivial anomaly indices $(n_2,ν_3)$ is onsiteable and hence admits a symmetric SRE state, establishing that these indices faithfully detect the lattice anomaly. Comparing with continuum QFT, we find that exact lattice symmetries do not realize the additional $H^1(BG_b,\mathbb Z_2)$ anomaly layer in continuum QFT. In particular, for $G_b=\mathbb Z_2$, exact lattice symmetries realize only the even $\mathbb Z_4$ subgroup of the continuum $\mathbb Z_8$ classification. In (2+1)D, we identify three successive anomaly layers of cohomological data $(n_2,n_3,ν_4)$. We show that a nontrivial value of any layer obstructs symmetric SRE. For $G_f=G_b\times\mathbb Z_2^F$, it also forbids a symmetric invertible state. When the bosonic group $G_b$ is non-trivially extended by fermion parity, the lattice obstruction to SRE states does not generally coincide with the continuum 't Hooft anomaly. We explicitly construct a $\mathbb Z_4^F$ lattice symmetry in (2+1)D with nontrivial lattice anomaly index that forbids symmetric SRE states, even though its continuum anomaly is trivial. Our results highlight a mismatch between lattice and continuum anomalies and motivate a systematic study of which continuum anomalies admit exact microscopic lattice realizations.

</details>

Stage 4 processes the extracted classical eigenvalues m1, mA, and m2 within a classical control unit to evaluate the net displacement vector mΣ = f(m1, mA, m2), executing the active feed-forward displacement ˆDB(−mΣ) on...</figcaption>

**Summary.** This paper presents an active, measurement-based protocol for homomorphically aggregating logical information encoded in CV GKP states. It proves that passive optical methods fail due to lattice compression and introduces a solution using homodyne measurements and feed-forward control. The result is crucial for scalable distributed quantum computing networks.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete theoretical blueprint for scaling quantum computation beyond simple linear chains by addressing topological aggregation challenges inherent to continuous-variable encoding schemes.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core challenge is developing a method for homomorphically aggregating logical quantum information encoded in continuous-variable (CV) Gottesman-Kitaev-Preskill (GKP) states across multiple distributed nodes.

**Main result.** An active, measurement-based framework utilizing GKP Bell states and homodyne feed-forward successfully computes the logical sum while preserving code space geometry up to correctable deformations.

**Method.** The authors propose a Completely Positive Trace-Preserving (CPTP) map that bypasses the limitations of passive linear optics by incorporating active syndrome extraction and classical feed-forward displacement.

**Model / system.** The system uses CV GKP states, which encode discrete logical information into an infinite-dimensional Hilbert space via a phase-space lattice. The process is analyzed within a measurement-based quantum computing (MBQC) framework.

**Key observables.** Logical fidelity ($F$), total variation distance ($	ext{TV}$), output position quadrature ($\hat{q}_{	ext{out}}$), and the intrinsic state noise variance ($\sigma_{	ext{out}}^2$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Squeezing level ($r$), critical Voronoi cell boundary threshold ($q_{	ext{thresh}} = \sqrt{\pi}/2$), and channel attenuation ($\eta$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The protocol assumes the ability to perform intermediate syndrome extraction at network vertices to reset physical noise accumulation, and it operates within a finite-energy GKP subspace.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate performance scaling under various conditions: (a) linear cascade vs. logarithmic tree topology for variance ($\sigma_{	ext{out}}$); (b) logical fidelity convergence; and (c)-(f) the impact of physical channel attenuation ($\eta < 1$) on achievable fidelity.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the failure modes of passive optics due to lattice compression. It then introduces the active, measurement-based protocol using GKP Bell states and homodyne measurements. Finally, it derives analytical bounds on fidelity and security, showing that logarithmic tree routing is necessary for scalability.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Aggregating logical quantum information encoded in continuous-variable phase space is essential for distributed quantum computing. However, passive linear optics fail for non-Gaussian Gottesman-Kitaev-Preskill (GKP) codes due to symplectic lattice compression and entanglement-induced decoherence. We present an active, measurement-based framework for the homomorphic aggregation of multi-node GKP states. Utilizing GKP Bell states and homodyne feed-forward, we construct a completely positive trace-preserving map that computes the logical sum of distributed states while preserving the logical code space geometry up to correctable finite-squeezing deformations. We prove this protocol operates as an approximate quantum non-demolition measurement, bound its cryptographic leakage for continuous one-time pads, and derive analytical logical fidelity limits under finite-squeezing constraints.

</details>

**Summary.** This work tackles the long-standing mystery of superconductivity in the 2D doped Hubbard model by introducing a novel Symmetry-Preserving Neural-Network Quantum State (SBP) ansatz. By mathematically enforcing translational symmetry, the method bypasses variational biases that favor incorrect broken-symmetry states. The results strongly suggest d-wave superconductivity exists for key parameters, providing significant theoretical progress.

**Why it may be interesting.** The development of symmetry-preserving NQS is a powerful general technique applicable across many strongly correlated systems where ground state degeneracy or symmetry breaking is a major theoretical hurdle. This methodology could be adapted to study exotic phases in AMO platforms simulating Hubbard physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Determining the true nature of the ground state, particularly superconducting order, in the highly debated two-dimensional doped Hubbard model.

**Main result.** The authors provide robust evidence for d-wave superconductivity in the 1/8-doped model by using a symmetry-preserving ansatz that avoids artificial biases found in previous variational methods.

**Method.** They employ Neural-Network Quantum States (NQS) via a novel Symmetry-Preserving Backflow Pairing (SBP) ansatz, which is designed to enforce translational symmetry during optimization.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on the two-dimensional doped Hubbard model, governed by an extended Hamiltonian involving hopping ($t, t'$) and onsite repulsion ($U$). The system is simulated at finite doping ($\delta=1/8$) relevant to cuprate superconductors.

**Key observables.** Superconducting order parameter ($\Delta_{SC}$), pairing correlation function $C(r)$, and measures of spatial modulation ($\delta_n, \delta_s$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** $t'/t=-0.2$, $U/t=8.0$, doping $\delta = 1/8$. Lattice sizes up to $24	imes24$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that enforcing symmetries directly into the wave function ansatz (SBP) resolves ambiguities caused by variational parameterization biases leading to broken-symmetry minima.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare energy optimization and symmetry breaking parameters between SBP and standard Backflow Pairing (BP) ansätze, showing SBP's superior convergence toward a symmetric ground state. Other figures illustrate local charge/spin modulations for the BP ansatz.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the physical problem and limitations of existing variational methods. It then introduces the SBP ansatz, details its mathematical formulation using neural networks (Transformer architecture), presents results comparing SBP to competing ansätze, and finally quantifies the evidence for d-wave superconductivity via correlation functions.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Despite its fundamental importance in the theory of strongly correlated electrons, the nature of the ground state of the two-dimensional doped Hubbard model remains intensely debated. Variational approaches provide a powerful route to this problem, but their conclusions can depend sensitively on the chosen wave-function parameterization, the mean-field initialization, or the pinning fields used to guide the optimization, as well as on boundary conditions. This can favor one type of symmetry breaking over another, making it difficult to distinguish the genuine interplay of intertwined or competing orders from biases induced by the variational parameterization. Here, we introduce the Symmetry-Preserving Backflow Pairing (SBP) ansatz, a neural-network wave function that respects translational symmetry by construction and thereby avoids these broken-symmetry minima. The SBP ansatz reaches state-of-the-art variational energies for the $t$-$t'$ Hubbard model on lattices up to $24\times24$ with $504$ electrons, below those of competing pure stripe solutions. By extrapolating to the thermodynamic limit, we find robust evidence for $d$-wave superconducting order, resolving a long-standing question about the $1/8$-doped model at $t'/t=-0.2$ and $U/t=8.0$. Built on general principles of symmetry and locality, the SBP wave function provides a broadly applicable variational representation for challenging interacting fermionic systems.

</details>
