**Summary.** This paper develops a theory showing that an electrostatic 'moiré capacitor effect' stabilizes Fractional Chern Insulators (FCIs) in graphene superlattices. Using advanced numerical methods like ED, the authors confirm FCI existence at $
u=2/3$ while demonstrating that this moiré potential is essential to suppress competing Charge Density Wave phases.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides a detailed theoretical mechanism (the moiré capacitor effect) explaining the stability of exotic topological states like FCIs in solid-state systems, which is highly relevant for understanding correlated electron physics and emergent quantum phases.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to provide a theoretical understanding of how the moiré potential stabilizes Fractional Chern Insulators (FCIs) in rhombohedral graphene superlattices, and to distinguish these states from competing Charge Density Wave (CDW) phases.

**Main result.** The 'moiré capacitor effect,' arising from electrostatically imprinting valence charge density onto conduction bands, is crucial for stabilizing the FCI state. Furthermore, band mixing is shown to be necessary to suppress CDW tendencies and realize a clean FCI ground state.

**Method.** The study employs theoretical derivations of effective Hamiltonians combined with advanced numerical techniques, including Hartree-Fock (HF), Time-Dependent HF (TDHF), and multi-band Exact Diagonalization (ED) calculations on the many-body Hilbert space.

**Model / system.** The system is rhombohedral graphene superlattices ($	ext{RLG/hBN}$). The physics is governed by conduction electrons interacting with a moiré potential derived from occupied valence bands, leading to an effective Hamiltonian incorporating this electrostatic term.

**Key observables.** Chern number ($C$), FCI gap size ($\Delta_{	ext{FCI}}$), Potential Energy Surface (PES) gaps, and the Particle Entanglement Spectrum (PES) counts used to distinguish FCI from CDW states.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Filling factors ($
u=1$ and $
u=2/3$), moiré potential strength ($V_{	ext{val.}}$), and various interaction parameters ($V, V_{	ext{tb}}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on specific orbital truncations for ED calculations and assumes that the moiré capacitor effect provides a dominant electrostatic contribution to stabilize the correlated state.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate band structure splitting due to the moiré potential, compare HF/TDHF spectra showing instability cure by finite $V_{	ext{val.}}$, and use PES analysis to distinguish FCI (168 states) from CDW counting (63 states).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the necessity of the moiré capacitor effect for stabilizing a parent CI state ($
u=1$). It then develops a general theory, using ED calculations at $
u=2/3$, to confirm FCI stability while simultaneously ruling out CDW competition through entanglement spectrum analysis.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

While the necessity of a moiré potential for fractional Chern insulators (FCIs) in rhombohedral graphene-hBN superlattices, first predicted in Ref. 1, is now grounded in experiments, a theory of its origin and importance remains at large. We present a mechanism---the moiré capacitor effect---that enhances the moiré potential by electrostatically imprinting the valence charge density onto the conduction bands. We derive the analytical form of this term and reveal its crucial role in stabilizing a parent state with Chern number $C=1$ at filling $ν=1$. We propose a parent state theory which posits that stability of the Chern insulator and flatness of its hole excitations are necessary for obtaining FCIs upon doping. We then perform multi-band exact diagonalization calculations to confirm the emergence of FCIs at $ν= 2/3$ in the presence of the moiré capacitor effect. Our FCI state is stabilized by inter-band fluctuations, unlike in the moire-free case which collapses with band-mixing. We provide the first consistent theory for this state in aligned samples and explain its absence in unaligned ones.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper presents an efficient tensor network contraction method to classically simulate IBM's large-scale doped Clifford random circuit sampling experiment. By mapping the 70-qubit circuit onto a structured tensor network, they achieved a significant computational result within minutes using advanced GPU clusters. This work establishes a quantitative classical benchmark for assessing quantum advantage in randomized quantum circuits.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete, scalable classical benchmark for simulating complex random quantum circuits, which is crucial for understanding the resource requirements needed to surpass current NISQ-era hardware capabilities in quantum information processing.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To classically simulate the complex amplitude evaluation for IBM's doped Clifford random circuit sampling (RCS) experiment to establish a resource baseline.

**Main result.** The authors successfully simulated all 2051 output batches in 37.3 minutes using 32 H100 GPUs, yielding a log-XEB estimate compatible with IBM's fidelity lower bound.

**Method.** They developed and applied a deterministic temporal-boundary tensor network contraction approach to evaluate the exact circuit amplitude by mapping the quantum circuit onto a PEPS-like structure.

**Model / system.** The system is an open-boundary, one-dimensional brickwork circuit involving 70 qubits, 70 entangling layers (CZ gates), and 468 inserted T gates, mimicking IBM's experimental setup.

**Key observables.** Log-XEB estimate of 0.35034; contraction width $\lceil d/2 ceil$; total simulation time (37.3 minutes).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Number of qubits ($n=70$), circuit depth ($d=70$), number of nodes (32), and GPU type (NVIDIA H100).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The compatibility of the log-XEB estimate with fidelity relies on Porter–Thomas and scrambled-noise assumptions.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 maps the circuit to a tensor network; Figure 2 details the deterministic temporal-state contraction path (Algorithm 1); Figure 4 compares the cost/width of constructive vs. heuristic paths.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem, details the novel deterministic tensor network contraction method for open boundaries, presents the computational results on the IBM instance, and concludes by positioning the technique as a general diagnostic tool for future quantum experiments.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We classically simulate the IBM doped Clifford random circuit sampling experiment, comprising $70$ qubits, $70$ entangling layers, and $468$ inserted $T$ gates. A deterministic temporal-boundary tensor network contraction approach is specifically designed to tackle such open-boundary one-dimensional brickwork circuits with operator-Schmidt-rank-$2$ entangling gates. For an $n$-qubit circuit of depth $d$, the resulting unsliced path evaluates an exact amplitude with contraction width $\lceil d/2\rceil$; Ratcatcher calculations certify that no smaller width is possible for the tested instances. Because one-qubit gates are absorbed without changing the network topology, the width and dense scheduled contraction cost are independent of their values and of the number and placement of $T$ gates. For the IBM instance, its largest intermediate tensor contains $2^{35}$ complex64 entries (256 times smaller than IBM's estimation), corresponding to a tensor payload of $256$ GiB, and is distributed across eight GPUs within a node. Using 32 nodes, with eight NVIDIA H100 GPUs per node, we completed all 2051 amplitude batches corresponding to IBM's published output bitstrings in 37.3 minutes. The resulting probabilities yield a log-XEB estimate of $0.35034$ with a 95\% interval of $[0.29763,0.40305]$. Under the Porter--Thomas and scrambled-noise assumptions, this is numerically compatible with IBM's fidelity lower bound; separately, fidelity-weighted resource accounting projects a 583-contraction workload with a 10.6-minute makespan on the same 32 nodes. More broadly, the approach provides a practical diagnostic for experimental outputs and a quantitative tool for designing future doped Clifford sampling experiments.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper proposes a Hybrid DFT-Quantum Embedding (QDFT) framework to overcome the limitations of standard Density Functional Theory in simulating strongly correlated molecular systems. It achieves this by partitioning large molecules, treating the critical active space with a Variational Quantum Eigensolver (VQE) and the rest classically via DFT. This hybrid approach aims to combine classical scalability with quantum mechanical accuracy for complex electronic structure problems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This directly addresses the core challenge in modern quantum chemistry: bridging the gap between the massive scale of classical computation (HPC/DFT) and the high accuracy required for strongly correlated phenomena, making it highly relevant to AMO physics and open quantum systems modeling.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Developing scalable simulations for molecular electronic structure that accurately capture strong electron correlation effects, which are poorly handled by standard DFT approximations.

**Main result.** The hybrid QDFT framework successfully integrates classical HPC scalability with quantum accuracy potential, demonstrating improvement in selected electronic-structure properties.

**Method.** A novel Quantum Embedding (QDFT) approach partitions large systems: the active space is treated via VQE on a quantum computer, while the environment is handled by classical DFT.

**Model / system.** The focus is on molecular systems undergoing electronic structure calculations. The methodology combines DFT for long-range screening with variational quantum circuits (VQE) to model short-range, strongly correlated interactions.

**Key observables.** Electronic-structure properties, energy eigenvalues ($\Delta E$), computational bottlenecks, and QPU execution requirements.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Energy difference thresholds ($10^{-5} 	ext{ Ha}$), range-separation parameters ($\mu, \alpha, eta$), and qubit resources/noise levels.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The initial work focuses on noiseless quantum simulation to systematically evaluate the framework's potential before tackling hardware noise limitations.

**Figures summary.** Not specified in detail, but concepts involve illustrating strong electron correlation, bond dissociation with state changes, and multi-reference character.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the QDFT concept by identifying the limitations of DFT for correlated systems. It details the framework's components (active space selection, embedding) and presents results showing potential improvements while maintaining HPC scalability. Future work focuses on noisy simulation and runtime estimation.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Scientific simulations demand methods combining scalability with predictive accuracy. Density Functional Theory (DFT) on High-Performance Computing (HPC) enables large-scale electronic-structure simulations but is limited by approximations affecting strongly correlated systems and band-gap predictions. Quantum computing offers a pathway to address this, though current Noisy Intermediate-Scale Quantum (NISQ) hardware remains constrained by qubit resources, noise, and execution cost.   This work presents a hybrid DFT-Quantum Embedding (QDFT) framework integrating classical HPC-based DFT with a quantum electronic-structure solver. Large systems are partitioned to isolate a chemically relevant active space, treated via the Variational Quantum Eigensolver (VQE), while the remaining degrees of freedom are described by DFT. The framework incorporates active-space selection, embedded Hamiltonian construction, symmetry preservation, operator mapping, self-consistent density updating, and modular classical-quantum coupling.   We focus on noiseless quantum simulation to systematically evaluate accuracy, convergence, active-space dependence, computational cost, and HPC scalability without hardware noise. Detailed profiling identifies computational bottlenecks and highlights limitations of CPU-based quantum simulation. A QPU runtime-estimation methodology is additionally developed to assess execution requirements on actual quantum hardware.   Results demonstrate quantum embedding's potential to improve selected electronic-structure properties while retaining classical HPC's scalability. Noisy quantum simulation and QPU execution remain key future directions, providing a pathway toward practical, scalable HPC-quantum hybrid simulations as hardware matures.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper tackles the computational bottleneck in using quantum computers to find molecular energies via the Nonorthogonal Quantum Eigensolver (NOQE). By introducing a scalable thresholding scheme based on the overlap matrix's condition number, the authors theoretically improve the required measurement shot count scaling from $O(M^3)$ to $O(M)$. This advancement makes simulating larger and more complex quantum chemical systems feasible with current noisy hardware.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the resource estimation (measurement time/shots) required for near-term quantum simulation of chemistry problems, which is a core concern in applying quantum algorithms to physical systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is improving the measurement cost scaling for quantum subspace diagonalization methods, specifically the Nonorthogonal Quantum Eigensolver (NOQE). The bottleneck is accurately estimating matrix elements when errors can lead to large eigenvalue errors.

**Main result.** The authors prove that by using a scalable thresholding scheme on the overlap matrix, the required shot count per matrix element improves from a worst-case $O(M^3)$ bound to a linear scaling of $O(M)$.

**Method.** The work combines theoretical perturbation analysis with numerical simulations. It involves formulating the problem as solving a generalized eigenvalue problem and applying thresholding techniques to control error propagation.

**Model / system.** The method is applied to estimating low-lying energies of molecular Hamiltonians, constructing a multireference subspace from dressed unrestricted Hartree-Fock states ($\vert\phi_Jangle$). Testing was done on hydrogen chains and rings.

**Key observables.** Eigenvalue sensitivity, measurement shot count ($N_{	ext{shots}}$), bias relative to noiseless results, and the condition number of the retained overlap matrix ($\kappa(S_{>\epsilon})$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** $M$ (number of reference states), $E$ (target accuracy), $\epsilon$ (thresholding parameter), and $\kappa(S)$ (overlap condition number).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the eigenvalue sensitivity is controlled by the condition number $\kappa(S)$ rather than dimension factors, and it relies on a scalable thresholding scheme to manage near-singularity.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 compares noiseless NOQE results against classical methods. Figure 2 shows how bias, retained condition number, and spin contamination remain bounded as system size $N$ increases when using appropriate thresholding schemes.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the theoretical bottleneck in measurement cost scaling for NOQE. It then introduces a scalable thresholding scheme to control errors by replacing dimension-based bounds with those based on the overlap condition number, leading to improved complexity bounds and numerical validation on molecular systems.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum subspace diagonalization methods are promising algorithms for quantum chemistry on near-term quantum computers. These methods can estimate low-lying energies of molecular systems using shallow quantum circuits, at the cost of many circuit repetitions to estimate the projected matrix elements. Errors in these matrix elements can be converted into much larger eigenvalue errors by an ill-conditioned overlap matrix. We study this bottleneck for the nonorthogonal quantum eigensolver (NOQE), which constructs a compact multireference subspace from dressed unrestricted Hartree-Fock states. We prove a finite-shot perturbation bound showing that, after overlap thresholding, the eigenvalue sensitivity is controlled by the condition number of the retained overlap matrix rather than by a worst-case dimension factor. With a scalable thresholding scheme, the upper bound on the per-matrix-element shot count required to reach a target accuracy scales as $\mathcal{O}(M)$, improving on the previously known $\mathcal{O}(M^3)$ bound, where $M$ is the number of reference states. Numerical experiments on hydrogen chains and rings suggest that, in practice, the measurement cost of structured NOQE instances can grow even more slowly than this linear bound.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors demonstrate that flexible chains assembled at a liquid-air interface can self-fold based on local geometric interactions encoded by capillary charges. They show that while short chains fold predictably, longer chains possess an exponentially growing number of metastable folding states. This platform allows for the experimental and theoretical study of how simple local rules give rise to complex collective behavior, analogous to biomolecular folding.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a highly controllable mesoscale platform where local rules (capillary charges) dictate emergent collective behavior (folding). The study of the exponential growth of metastable states and the role of external agitation mirrors concepts in glassy dynamics, energy minimization pathways, and frustrated systems relevant to statistical mechanics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To investigate how a minimal, static, geometry-based interaction alphabet (capillary charges) can control not only the target conformation but also the complex metastable folding landscape of a connected chain.

**Main result.** The complexity and degeneracy of the folding landscape grow exponentially with chain length for long chains, and mechanical agitation is required to transition between these many metastable states.

**Method.** Numerical mapping of energy landscapes derived from pair interaction potentials, complemented by experimental measurement using Schlieren profilometry and physical manipulation (agitation).

**Model / system.** The system consists of flexible polymer chains made of floating monomers at a liquid-air interface. Interactions are governed by capillary charges ($Q$) encoded in the monomer geometry, leading to pair potentials derived from the modified Laplace equation.

**Key observables.** Number of metastable macrostates ($|G|$), energy landscape $U(s)$, Hamming distance ($d_H$), and the final folded ground state configuration.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Capillary length ($\lambda \approx 2.7$ mm for water-air interface); local interaction rules defined by capillary charges $\{+,+\}, \{-,-\}, \{-,+\}, \{+-\}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The use of the Linear Superposition Approximation (LSA) to model the total interface deformation.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate chain structures, measured/simulated liquid profiles for monomer assembly, energy landscapes for dimer cases, and scaling plots showing exponential growth of metastable states with chain length N.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the physical platform and interaction model using capillary charges. It then analyzes the folding behavior, demonstrating that while short chains are deterministic, longer chains exhibit exponentially growing complexity in their energy landscape. Finally, it explores how single-element mutations affect this complex folding landscape.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Mesoscale self-assembly provides a route toward the design of programmable microsystems. Here, we construct flexible chains of floating monomers whose curved branches impose upward or downward deformations of the liquid interface, corresponding to effective positive or negative capillary charges. These geometrically encoded deformations generate local attractive or repulsive interactions along the chain. By tuning the capillary sequence, we obtain distinct folded configurations, including straight lines, zigzag patterns, and loops. For short chains, folding is largely governed by nearest-neighbor interactions and leads to well-defined structures. As the chain length increases and non-neighboring segments come into proximity and interact, however, the folding landscape becomes increasingly complex, with multiple metastable states whose number grows exponentially with chain length. We map these landscapes numerically and demonstrate experimentally that mechanical agitation allows the chains to transition between metastable configurations. Beyond encoding a target geometry, the capillary sequence therefore controls the complexity of the folding landscape as well as the degeneracy and mutational robustness of folded structures. These results establish capillary chains as a controllable mesoscale platform for investigating how local interaction rules give rise to collective folding and complex sequence-to-structure relationships reminiscent of those encountered in biomolecular systems.

</details>

(e) Same as (d) but for parameters corresponding to the orange cross in (b): B⊥/fp = 0 and B∥/fp = 1. (f) Eigenvalues evolution versus B⊥/fp for fixed B∥/fp = 0. (g) Eigenvalues evolution versus B∥/fp for fixed B⊥/fp = 0. The color indicates the spin projection along the SO axis ⟨σx⟩. The external flux is fixed to...</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** The paper introduces the Inductively-protected Andreev (IPA) spin qubit as an advanced platform for realizing robust quantum bits. By incorporating an inductor shunt, it significantly enhances protection against environmental noise compared to standard designs. This theoretical work demonstrates how engineering the phase space potential can combine long coherence times with operational spin degrees of freedom.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses noise suppression in superconducting qubits by engineering the underlying quantum mechanical potential structure, a core topic for quantum information theorists working on solid-state hardware.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is mitigating environmental noise (relaxation and dephasing) to build scalable quantum computers using superconducting spin qubits.

**Main result.** The Inductively-protected Andreev (IPA) qubit enhances coherence by separating spin-qubit states into distinct potential wells in phase space, achieving protection superior to standard ASQ or fluxonium models.

**Method.** Theoretical analysis involving Hamiltonian construction, solving for energy spectra, and calculating wavefunction overlap ($\xi$) using both exact diagonalization and harmonic approximations.

**Model / system.** The system is an Andreev spin qubit (ASQ) realized in a semiconductor-superconductor Josephson junction. Protection is achieved by shunting the ASQ with a linear inductor to form the IPA qubit, which behaves like two uncoupled fluxoniums.

**Key observables.** Coherence time ($T_1$), wavefunction overlap ($\xi$), and transition energies between spin states.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Inductive energy ratio ($EL/EC$), Josephson energy ratio ($	ilde{EJ}/EC$), and external flux detuning ($\delta\phi_{	ext{ext}}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on approximations, including the harmonic limit for calculating overlaps, which is noted to show quantitative discrepancies with exact solutions.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate circuit diagrams comparing ASQ, IPA, and fluxonium potentials; they also plot qubit spectra, wavefunctions, and the overlap ($\xi$) as a function of inductive coupling strength.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the basic ASQ Hamiltonian to introducing the inductor-protected IPA model. It then analyzes the resulting potential landscape, derives spectral properties using harmonic approximations, and quantifies protection via wavefunction overlap calculations across different parameter regimes.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The spin of a quasiparticle trapped in a quantum dot Josephson junction forms the basis of an Andreev spin qubit (ASQ): a semiconductor-superconductor device where the interplay between a localized spin degree of freedom and superconductivity leads to a spin-resolved Josephson potential. In this work, we show that shunting an ASQ with a linear inductor enhances its relaxation time by separating the spin-qubit states into distinct potential wells in phase space, nearly eliminating wavefunction overlap. The resulting inductively protected Andreev (IPA) spin qubit is equivalent to two fluxoniums in the heavy regime, one for each spin. Thus, the IPA qubit combines the long coherence times, low-frequency ground-state manifold, and large anharmonicity of a protected superconducting qubit with the operational advantages of a spin degree of freedom.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces a novel, efficient framework to design physical neural networks using random lasing structures. By establishing a three-layer link between simple graph metrics and complex lasing physics, they guide an optimization algorithm that yields superior network topologies for vision tasks compared to random designs.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful methodology for designing complex quantum or nonlinear optical systems by abstracting the design space into graph theory, bypassing expensive full-scale physical simulations of many-body dynamics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Optimizing physical neural network designs, which rely on complex nonlinear dynamics, is computationally prohibitive due to the high dimensionality of possible topologies.

**Main result.** The authors established a quantitative link where simple graph-theoretic metrics can predict and guide the optimization of lasing physics, leading to network topologies that significantly outperform random designs in image classification tasks.

**Method.** They developed an evolutionary algorithm guided by graph metrics (like clique sizes sum and edge betweenness centrality) to optimize random lasing networks, which are then tested on simulated image-classification benchmarks.

**Model / system.** The exemplar system is a random lasing network composed of interconnected nanoscale waveguides hosting strongly coupled lasing modes. The analysis maps graph structure metrics onto the nonlinear lasing physics and subsequent vision performance.

**Key observables.** Graph metrics (e.g., clique sizes sum, median edge betweenness centrality), physical observables (number of lasing modes, inter-mode competition), and vision proxies (Excitation-inhibition metric, Feature detection metric).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Total edge length ($L$), shot size $S$ for training, and hyperparameters $(k, C)$ for the logistic regression classifier.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The core assumption is that graph properties can predict emergent nonlinear dynamics and learning performance in active, strongly-interacting physical networks, allowing optimization in an abstract graph space rather than simulating substrate physics.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate a three-layer pipeline (Graph $ightarrow$ Physics $ightarrow$ Application), schematics of the lasing network, and show correlations between various graph parameters and vision metrics across different network sets.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes a theoretical framework linking graph theory to physical optics via random lasing networks. It then uses this link to guide an evolutionary optimization process based on graph metrics, culminating in testing the optimized topologies on simulated image classification tasks using logistic regression.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Physical neural networks perform learning through the intrinsic nonlinear dynamics of matter. Optimising their design presents a considerable challenge: complex many-body physics can provide powerful computation, but are expensive to simulate and large experimental optimisation runs are impractical to fabricate. Hence, the high-dimensional space of possible network topologies cannot be effectively directly searched. Here, we show that this search can be efficiently performed in an abstract graph space that is vastly cheaper to explore. Using random lasing networks -- composed of interconnected nanoscale waveguides and hosting strongly coupled lasing modes -- as an exemplar physical vision system, we establish a quantitative three-layer link: simple graph-theoretic metrics predict the nonlinear lasing physics, which in turn predicts vision performance. After validating this relationship using physical simulations, we exploit it to drive an evolutionary algorithm using graph metrics, producing network topologies that outperform random designs at a fraction of the computational cost (3000$\times$ speed-up compared to physical simulation). On simulated image-classification tasks, graph-optimised networks substantially improve classification accuracy. As our framework operates on network topology rather than substrate-specific physics, we anticipate it can transfer to other network-based physical learning systems, providing an efficient route for the directed design and optimisation of complex, strongly-interacting physical neural networks.

</details>

### [Parity Floors in Quantum Denoisers: A Closed-Form Benchmark for Fixed-Map Denoising Networks](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12712v1)

**Authors:** Jaeuk Kim  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.12712v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `driven-dissipative phase transition` **2/5** · `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **1/5** · `correlated / nonlocal dissipation` **1/5** · `methods for driven-dissipative` **1/5** · `quantum measurements` **1/5**

<div class="figure-strip-hint">↔ Scroll figures horizontally</div>

<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 1.</b> Figure 1: (a) The measured depth-1 quantum excess closely tracks the parity proxy (selected even reference −Rref) ≈∥Πoddmσ∥2 across noise levels, while a classical bank that adds the sine sector matches Rref. (b) The signed empirical residual ˆδσ,N is small in absolute value (≤0.018); error bars are paired bootstrap 95% CIs (Table 2). The negative value at σ=1.4 reflects that the finite even reference need not contain the quantum even feature span.</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** This work introduces a novel benchmark to analyze fixed quantum feature maps used in diffusion denoisers. By decomposing the reconstruction error into even and odd components, the authors prove that the primary limitation (the 'parity floor') is dictated by the target's odd spectral content rather than the map's ability to represent all features. This guides future research toward nonclassical data access methods.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides rigorous mathematical tools for diagnosing fundamental limitations in variational quantum algorithms applied to physical processes like diffusion modeling, suggesting that parity constraints are a more fundamental barrier than simple model capacity.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to determine which structural constraint limits fixed quantum feature maps when used within diffusion denoisers, distinguishing between representational limitations and other sources of error.

**Main result.** The excess risk in the denoising process is shown to be dominated by an irreducible 'parity floor,' which is a property of the noise-conditioned target rather than static map capacity.

**Method.** The authors develop a closed-form benchmark using analytic heat-kernel noising on a torus, decomposing the excess risk into parity and within-sector residual components based on even/odd subspaces.

**Model / system.** The model involves quantum feature maps (e.g., depth-1 RY+CNOT+Pauli-Z) applied to a diffusion process defined by a Gibbs law prior on the torus ($\mathbb{T}^n$).

**Key observables.** Excess risk, parity proxy ($\hat{P}_\sigma$), signed empirical residual ($\hat{\delta}_{\sigma,N}$), and the irreducible lower bound $\|\Pi_{	ext{odd}}m_\sigma\|^2$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Noise scale ($\sigma$), feature map depth, and the structure of the prior (e.g., 3-body coupling on $\mathbb{T}^n$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the assumption that the excess risk can be decomposed into parity and within-sector components; the results are limited to fixed feature maps.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate how quantum excess tracks the calculated parity proxy across varying noise levels, confirming parity dominance over capacity limitations.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes a theoretical decomposition of the risk based on even/odd subspaces. It then applies this framework to specific fixed quantum feature map families (depth-1) and compares performance against classical benchmarks using various priors (torus, signed-XY).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Fixed quantum feature maps are increasingly inserted into diffusion denoisers, but standard image benchmarks do not reveal which structural constraint limits them. We introduce CoupledPhaseTexture, a torus-diffusion benchmark with analytic heat-kernel noising that separates parity, within-sector approximation, and sample-complexity limitations. For the depth-1 RY+CNOT+Pauli-Z family we prove a containment-free parity floor: all reachable features are even functions of the encoded angles while the sine components of the Bayes denoiser are odd, so the excess risk splits exactly into an inaccessible odd part and a within-sector residual. The first term is an irreducible, noise-scale-resolved lower bound holding for every even feature class, with no containment, linearity, or closedness assumption on the feature class. The obstruction is a property of the noise-conditioned denoising target rather than static representability: the floor is re-derived at each noise scale because the target's parity content changes with noise. The measured excess is dominated by the parity proxy on two distinct priors. Higher-order Z readouts improve the even sector, but entanglement does not lower the floor and re-uploading does not reliably close it. Classical controls confirm the deficit is parity rather than quantumness: a cosine-only bank is floored similarly, while adding the sine sector matches the reference. Among tested constructions, odd readouts and a noise-coupled encoder do not match the sine-carrying classical bank. These results motivate nonclassical data access or feature classes without efficient classical surrogates; they do not establish either as sufficient for quantum advantage.

</details>

**Authors:** Mudassir Moosa  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.12512v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `analog quantum simulation` **2/5** · `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **1/5** · `correlated / nonlocal dissipation` **1/5** · `quantum measurements` **1/5**

<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 2.</b> Fig. 1. If we denote the algebra of operators that act trivially on LN by AN ⊂B(HN), we</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** This paper connects the mathematical classification of local operator algebras (von Neumann algebras) to computational resources. It argues that systems whose local structure requires a Type III algebra—typical in QFTs—must inherently possess an infinite amount of 'magic' resource for their description to be mathematically consistent.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a deep connection between abstract algebraic structures (Type III factors) and concrete physical requirements for computation/simulation, which is highly relevant to understanding quantum field theories and holographic duality.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The work establishes a fundamental link between the mathematical structure of local von Neumann algebras (specifically Type III) and the required quantum resources ('magic') for simulating physical systems in the thermodynamic limit.

**Main result.** It is proven that Type III von Neumann algebras fundamentally require an infinite amount of 'magic' resource, linking algebraic classification to computational complexity.

**Method.** The analysis uses the formalism of von Neumann algebras and examines the inductive limits of finite lattice systems (thermodynamic limit) while quantifying resources using the concept of 'magic' gates.

**Model / system.** The study considers quantum field theories, quantum gravity models, and general lattice systems analyzed in their thermodynamic limit. The mathematical framework relies on local von Neumann algebras describing these infinite-dimensional systems.

**Key observables.** Magic (number of non-Clifford gates), Type III von Neumann Algebras, Local Subregions in QFTs.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Thermodynamic limit ($N 	o \infty$), Bounded vs. Infinite Magic Resource.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the formal structure of local algebras and assumes that the thermodynamic limit can be properly constructed via inductive limits of finite systems.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically represents a nested sequence of lattices ($\Lambda_N$) partitioning into subregions, leading to an inductive limit algebra $A$.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by defining the mathematical tools (von Neumann algebras, magic resource) and then constructing the argument: showing that bounded local resources preclude the existence of Type III algebras in the infinite limit.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The number of non-Clifford gates needed to perform a task, or simply \textit{magic}, is a resource for fault-tolerant quantum computation. Von Neumann algebras provide a formal mathematical structure to describe infinite-dimensional quantum systems, such as those in quantum field theory or quantum statistical mechanics. A particularly important class of von Neumann algebras is called Type III algebras, for which the standard notions of finite-dimensional systems such as density matrices and traces break down. In this work, we argue that Type III von Neumann algebras fundamentally require an infinite amount of magic. Specifically, we consider the thermodynamic limit of finite-dimensional quantum systems, such as lattice systems, and show that if the states in the thermodynamic limit possess only a bounded amount of magic, the resulting local von Neumann algebra cannot be of Type III. Our result has direct implications for the quantum simulations of quantum field theories, for which the algebra of a local subregion is known to be of Type III.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper analyzes the distribution of basis-independent coherence and quantum correlations in two particles coupled by gravity within double-well potentials. By decomposing total coherence into collective and localized parts, it demonstrates that local coherence is more resilient to temperature effects than inter-particle correlations. This highlights a resource hierarchy where local superpositions persist longer than globally entangled states as the system heats up.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a detailed resource quantification framework for non-local quantum resources (coherence) in a physically relevant setting (gravity), showing how local versus global entanglement survives thermalization differently.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how quantum correlations and basis-independent coherence are distributed between collective (inter-particle) and localized (intra-particle) components in a system interacting via gravity.

**Main result.** Localized coherence ($C_L$) is found to be more robust against thermal fluctuations than the collective coherence ($C_C$), while increasing gravitational coupling ($\Delta$) preferentially enhances $C_C$.

**Method.** The analysis uses quantum information measures like Bures distance, Quantum Discord (QD), and a basis-independent measure of coherence derived from the Quantum Jensen–Shannon Divergence (QJSD).

**Model / system.** The system consists of two massive particles confined in a double-well potential and coupled by their mutual Newtonian gravitational interaction. The dynamics are governed by a bipartite Hamiltonian involving parameters for single-particle energy ($w$) and coupling strength ($\Delta$).

**Key observables.** Total coherence ($C_T$), Collective coherence ($C_C$), Localized coherence ($C_L$), Bures entanglement, Quantum Discord.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature ($T$ or $eta$), Gravitational coupling ($\Delta$), Single-particle energy scale ($w$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on a two-level approximation for the potential eigenstates and assumes thermal equilibrium with a reservoir characterized by temperature $T$.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate how entanglement (Bures measure) and Quantum Discord vary with $T$, $\Delta$, and $w$; coherence measures show distinct dependencies, confirming that $C_L$ is more robust than $C_C$ against thermal noise.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the physical model and Hamiltonian, defines various quantum correlation and coherence metrics ($C_T = C_C + C_L$), and then systematically analyzes the dependence of these measures on temperature, gravitational coupling ($\Delta$), and single-particle energy scale ($w$).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the distribution of quantum correlations and basis-independent coherence in a pair of massive particles confined in a double-well potential and coupled through their mutual Newtonian gravitational interaction. Non-classical correlations are characterized using Bures distance of entanglement and quantum discord, while coherence is quantified through the square root of the quantum Jensen--Shannon divergence (QJSD) from the maximally mixed state, yielding a measure that is invariant under arbitrary unitary transformations and is therefore genuinely basis-independent. The total coherence $C_T$ decomposes into two operationally distinct contributions: the collective coherence $C_C$, which captures quantum correlations between the two subsystems, and the localized coherence $C_L$, which captures the intrinsic quantum coherence of each individual subsystem. We analyze how temperature $T$, the gravitational coupling $Δ$, and the single-particle energy scale $w$ govern the redistribution of coherence between its collective and localized components. Our results show that $C_L$ is more robust against thermal fluctuations than $C_C$, and that increasing $Δ$ preferentially enhances collective coherence by strengthening gravitationally induced inter-particle correlations.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper investigates a three-component quantum walker whose dynamics are controlled by an SU(3) coin operator. By tuning the coupling parameters, researchers map out a complex phase diagram that allows for engineering specific transport behaviors. This demonstrates a powerful method to control spreading and localization in multi-component quantum systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a general framework for engineering quantum transport using internal degrees of freedom (the qutrit components) rather than relying solely on external lattice geometry or topological constraints.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to systematically tune and control the transport properties of a multi-component quantum walker by manipulating pairwise couplings within its coin operator.

**Main result.** The system exhibits a rich phase diagram in parameter space, allowing for engineered regimes ranging from rapid ballistic spreading to strongly anisotropic dynamics with partial localization.

**Method.** Analysis involves simulating the time evolution of the state using an SU(3) coin operator and quantifying transport via spatio-temporal probability distributions and side-resolved mean/variance ratios.

**Model / system.** The model is a discrete-time quantum walk of a three-component particle (qutrit) on a 1D lattice. The evolution is governed by $U = S C(\alpha, eta, \gamma)$, where the coin operator $C$ uses Gell-Mann matrices to couple internal components.

**Key observables.** Spatio-temporal probability distribution $P(x, t)$; ballistic spreading rate $m(eta, \gamma)$; side-resolved mean position ratios ($R_X$) and variance ratios ($R_\sigma$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The coupling parameters $(\alpha, eta, \gamma)$, particularly exploring regimes near $eta \sim\pm\pi/2$ and $\gamma \sim\pm\pi/2$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the walk remains ballistic across all tested parameter regimes; a fixed initial state $|\Psi(0)angle = rac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(|Rangle + |Bangle) \otimes |0angle$ is used.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate $P(x, t)$ for different couplings and show how side-resolved observables ($\langle X_L angle, \langle X_R angle$) reveal directional asymmetry across the parameter space.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the dynamics by first defining the controlled Hamiltonian/evolution operator, then applying diagnostics like $\sigma(t)$ and side-resolved ratios to map out how varying $(eta, \gamma)$ controls transport regimes in a structured phase diagram.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate a discrete-time quantum walk of a three-component quantum particle on a one-dimensional lattice. As coin operators, we employ parameterized rotations generated by the Gell-Mann matrices, which enable systematic tuning of the couplings between the internal components. We analyze how these couplings influence and control the dynamics by systematically exploring the position-space probability distribution across a broad region of the parameter space. To quantify the impact of different inter-component couplings, we further examine the ratio of the mean position to the variance in each half of the lattice. Our results indicates that the system supports a rich variety of transport regimes, ranging from nearly symmetric, rapidly spreading walks to strongly anisotropic dynamics with partial localization. This framework thus provides a new avenue for engineering targeted spreading and trapping behavior in multicomponent discrete-time quantum walks.

</details>

**Summary.** This work investigates how structural defects, specifically antisite mixing, modify the magnetic properties of topological magnetic insulators like $	ext{MnSb}_2	ext{Te}_4$. By combining inelastic neutron scattering with spin-dynamics simulations, the authors demonstrate that these defects induce a strong, measurable antiferromagnetic coupling responsible for an observable optical magnon gap. This highlights defect engineering as a powerful tool to tune fundamental magnetic interactions in quantum materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** The study provides a concrete example of how disorder (antisite defects) fundamentally alters the magnetic Hamiltonian and excitation spectrum of a quantum material, which is relevant for understanding engineered correlated states in condensed matter systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to directly probe defect-induced magnetic interactions and understand how antisite mixing affects the spin dynamics of topological magnetic insulators.

**Main result.** Antisite mixing generates disordered sublattices, leading to a distinct optical magnon mode with a large gap ($\Delta' \approx 1.5$ meV), indicating strong defect-mediated antiferromagnetic coupling ($J'$).

**Method.** The research combines inelastic neutron scattering (INS) experiments on single crystals with semi-classical spin-dynamics simulations using Landau-Lifshitz Dynamics (LLD).

**Model / system.** The physical system is $	ext{MnSb}_2	ext{Te}_4$, a topological magnetic insulator in the $	ext{Mn}(	ext{Bi},	ext{Sb})_2	ext{Te}_4$ family, which exhibits significant antisite defects. The analysis uses spin Hamiltonians incorporating various exchange couplings ($J_0, J_c, J'$).

**Key observables.** Optical magnon gap ($\Delta'$), spin wave dispersion curves (acoustic and optic branches), magnetic dimer structure factor.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Defect concentration ($\sim 15\%$ in MST vs. $<3\%$ in MBT); coupling strengths ($J' \approx 0.7$ meV, $J_0 = -0.075$ meV).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the observed optical magnon mode is a signature of defect-induced ferrimagnetism and relies on fitting spin wave dispersions to model parameters.

**Figures summary.** Figures show INS data (constant energy slices, in-plane dispersion) for MST compared to MBT, illustrating the distinct magnetic excitation spectrum caused by defects.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from identifying the problem of defect influence in topological insulators to presenting experimental INS measurements, followed by theoretical modeling using spin Hamiltonians and LLD simulations to quantify the defect-induced couplings.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Magnetic defect engineering offers a route to manipulate and control magnetic and electronic states in quantum materials. In the Mn(Bi,Sb)2Te4 family of topological magnetic insulators, Sb substitution facilitates site mixing between Mn and Sb atoms that affects magnetic order and band topology. Here we directly probe these defect-induced magnetic interactions using inelastic neutron scattering on single crystals of MnSb2Te4. We find that antisite mixing generates inequivalent and disordered magnetic sublattices where strong defect-induced antiferromagnetic coupling produces an optical magnon with a large gap. Semi-classical spin-dynamics simulations accurately capture magnetic excitations in the ordered and paramagnetic states and identify that linear Mn-Te-Mn bonds mediate coupling to antisite magnetic defects.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces SPLIT-Q, a scalable quantum framework designed to solve large power system islanding problems. It overcomes the exponential resource scaling limitations of monolithic quantum formulations by employing regionalization and sequential solving techniques within QAOA. The results demonstrate that this method can accurately find optimal partitions on real quantum hardware for very large networks while maintaining a fixed, manageable qubit footprint.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on power grids, the core methodology—decoupling exponentially scaling combinatorial problems into fixed-width, sequentially solvable quantum subproblems—is highly relevant to developing scalable quantum algorithms for general hard optimization problems in condensed matter physics or materials science.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the computational challenge of solving large-scale controlled islanding problems in power grids, which is a complex combinatorial optimization task.

**Main result.** A novel sequential distributed QAOA framework was proposed that successfully solves these large problems on current quantum hardware by keeping the required qubit width fixed, unlike monolithic approaches whose complexity scales exponentially with network size.

**Method.** The method formulates the problem as boundary-conditioned regional QUBO subproblems solved sequentially. This approach partitions the optimization variables into manageable regions to fit within limited quantum resources.

**Model / system.** The physical system is an electrical power grid modeled as a weighted graph (buses and transmission lines). The goal is to partition this network into electrically sustainable 'islands' by minimizing the total weight of cut edges.

**Key observables.** Disruption cost (cut value in MW), quantum resource demand (two-qubit gate count $G$, circuit depth $d$), and solution quality compared against classical solvers like Gurobi.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Network size (up to 300 buses), fixed qubit budget ($Q_{\max}$), and the scaling factor $\chi$ which measures width reduction relative to network size.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that regionalization successfully decouples the quantum resource requirement from the total network size, allowing fixed-width devices to solve arbitrarily large systems.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the workflow of partitioning free buses into regions for sequential QAOA sampling and show reconstructed islanding solutions on various IEEE benchmark systems.

**Paper structure.** The paper first defines the physical problem (controlled islanding) using graph theory and optimization formulations. It then critiques monolithic quantum approaches due to scaling issues, proposes the scalable regionalized framework, details its implementation via sequential QUBO solving, and finally validates it by comparing results across various IEEE benchmark systems on real hardware.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Growing integration of distributed energy resources increases power-system variability and uncertainty. During disturbances, these effects can intensify generation-load imbalances and cascading failures. Controlled islanding limits their propagation by partitioning a compromised grid into connected, electrically sustainable islands. However, classical methods face rapidly growing computational costs as network size and island count increase. Quantum optimization offers an alternative for exploring this combinatorial partition space. Yet monolithic quantum formulations encode all assignment decisions in one circuit, causing qubit demand and circuit complexity to scale with network size. In this study, a qubit-bounded sequential distributed quantum approximate optimization algorithm (QAOA) framework is proposed to tackle coherent controlled islanding under limited quantum resources. It formulates the optimization as boundary-conditioned regional quadratic unconstrained binary optimization (QUBO) subproblems that are solved sequentially within a fixed qubit budget. Thus, circuit width remains independent of network size, with aggregate quantum workload scaling linearly on bounded-degree networks. Evaluation covers eleven IEEE systems from 9 to 300 buses using IBM quantum computing resources, with Gurobi and monolithic QAOA as references. Across all systems, the framework recovers feasible Gurobi-optimal partitions under noise, confirming the resilience of its solution quality. The results further show that the proposed method substantially reduces quantum-resource demand and circuit complexity relative to monolithic QAOA, allowing large islanding problems to be addressed within current hardware limits. The proposed framework provides a feasible and scalable pathway for quantum optimization in large-scale power systems.

</details>

**Authors:** Maxwell West, M. Cerezo, Martin Larocca  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.13528v1>

The invariant state effectively acts as a resource for converting a unitary from the group to its Hermitian conjugate. Indeed, for U ∈G we have U ⊗2(V ⊗1H) |Ψ⟩= ((UV U †) ⊗1H) |Ψ⟩, so that there are regions of (V ⊗1H) that are “undisturbed” if (and with high probability only if) U is of sublinear-depth. Modulo some mild technical assumptions on the entanglement structure of |Ψ⟩and the scrambling capacity of G, this can be used to rule out such sublinear-depth...</figcaption>
</figure>

**Summary.** This paper proves that achieving approximate unitary designs over several important symmetry groups requires circuit depths that scale linearly with system size, even when utilizing unitaries from outside the defined subgroup. This result closes a loophole in prior work and has major implications for quantum information theory by setting fundamental depth overheads for benchmarking protocols.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides rigorous theoretical constraints on the resource overhead required for practical quantum characterization protocols (like tomography or benchmarking), directly impacting hardware feasibility assessments.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the fundamental limits on how efficiently approximate unitary designs can be constructed when sampling from specific subgroups of the full unitary group, particularly when using local nearest-neighbor gates.

**Main result.** It proves that these limitations hold even if 'ambient' unitaries (from outside the subgroup) are used, implying that many natural quantum tomography and benchmarking schemes suffer from a dramatic circuit depth overhead compared to ideal protocols.

**Method.** The analysis uses lower bounds derived from comparing the $k$-th moment channel fidelity ($\Phi^{(k)}$) against the Haar measure using invariant projectors associated with maximally entangled states.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on various quantum groups, including the matchgate, orthogonal $	ext{O}(d)$, symplectic $	ext{SP}(d/2)$, and Clifford groups, applied to nearest-neighbor gates on a one-dimensional lattice.

**Key observables.** Approximate $k$-design fidelity ($\epsilon$), circuit depth ($L$), and the diamond distance between moment channels.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The required depth scales linearly with system size ($n$) for all tested groups, establishing an $\Omega(n)$ lower bound.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The core contribution is extending previous results by showing that the limitation holds even when unitaries used are not strictly confined to the target subgroup (i.e., using ambient unitaries).

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates how sublinear-depth designs over groups with invariant states are disallowed via lightcone arguments.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes a general lower bound on design fidelity based on invariant projectors, specializes this for pure entangled states, and then applies these theorems to specific quantum groups (matchgate, orthogonal, symplectic, Clifford) to prove the necessary linear depth scaling.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Characterising the efficiency with which designs over various subsets of the unitary group may be constructed is an important goal of quantum information theory. While it is now known that approximate unitary designs can be realised in depth logarithmic in the system size, it has recently been shown that ensembles of local nearest-neighbour sublinear-depth one-dimensional circuits over the matchgate, orthogonal, and symplectic groups cannot form approximate 2-designs over their parent groups; similarly, sublinear-depth ensembles of Cliffords cannot form a Clifford 4-design. In this note we show that this remarkable exponential separation is not merely an artefact of restricting to ensembles consisting of unitaries from the subgroups themselves, but rather that no ensemble of local nearest-neighbour sublinear-depth unitaries can realise approximate designs in the aforementioned cases, even when employing "ambient" unitaries from beyond the subgroup itself (possibly acting on ancilla qubits). This implies that various natural tomography and benchmarking schemes which involves sampling from these groups suffer from a dramatic circuit depth overhead compared to similar protocols which involve sampling from the full unitary group. We additionally conclude that, in all of the above cases, the known linear-depth design constructions are up to constant factors optimal.

</details>

**Summary.** This work introduces 'causation,' defined as the static susceptibility, as an alternative to standard correlation functions for studying quantum criticality. It demonstrates that causation decays much faster than correlation due to mathematical constraints involving time derivatives. This difference allows researchers to pinpoint fundamental physical operators and accurately predict phenomena like edge-mode splittings in topological phases.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a powerful new diagnostic tool for critical systems that can distinguish between physical influences (causation) and mere statistical correlations, which is crucial for understanding emergent phenomena in strongly interacting quantum matter.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper contrasts standard correlation functions with a novel measure called 'causation' (static susceptibility) to investigate how physical influences decay at quantum critical points.

**Main result.** Causation can exhibit significantly faster decay rates than correlations, revealing physics—such as specific primary operators or edge-mode splittings—that are otherwise masked by the slower decaying correlation terms.

**Method.** The analysis employs conformal field theory (CFT) techniques, resolvent calculations involving Green's functions, and numerical methods like DMRG to compare scaling laws for both observables.

**Model / system.** The study applies to various critical systems, including the $(2+1)$D Ising model, gapless symmetry-protected topological (SPT) phases in 1D, and general quantum critical points described by CFTs.

**Key observables.** Correlation functions $\langle O_1(x) O_2(0) angle$ vs. Causation/Static Susceptibility $\lim_{\lambda	o 0} \partial_\lambda \langle A(x) angle_\lambda$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Dimensionality (1+1D, 2+1D), scaling exponents ($\Delta$), and the perturbation strength ($\lambda$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on identifying operators that are not time derivatives to correctly calculate causation, contrasting this with standard correlation measurements.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate numerical comparisons of correlation vs. causation decay rates for various Ising variants in (1+1)D and show the scaling of edge-mode splittings derived from bulk causal influence in SPTs.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically introduces the concept of causation, derives its theoretical scaling predictions via CFT, applies this framework to analyze boundary effects in 1D SPTs, and demonstrates how it reveals hidden primary operators in higher dimensions.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Correlation functions $\langle O_1(x) O_2(0) \rangle$ reveal scaling dimensions through spatial decay. We instead consider static susceptibility, the change in $\langle O_1(x) \rangle$ from perturbing the Hamiltonian by $O_2(0)$, which we term causation for short. In a conformal field theory (CFT), dimensional analysis predicts decay of $|x|^{-2Δ}$ for correlation and $|x|^{-2Δ+1}$ for causation. Yet we find causation can decay up to fifteen additional orders in $x$ through a general mechanism, which we trace to time-derivative fields being unable to contribute to static response. In higher-dimensional CFTs, this mechanism ensures leading causation arises from primaries, even when descendants dominate correlation, which we leverage with DMRG to identify a previously unresolved corner primary of $Δ\approx 8.8$ and a heavy magnetic line defect primary of $Δ\approx 4.6$ in the $(2+1)$D critical Ising model. Moreover, the same mechanism governs edge-mode localization in $(1+1)$D gapless symmetry-protected topological phases, explaining previously observed anomalously small edge-mode splittings and guiding our construction of spin chains with splittings as small as $1/L^{18}$ and $1/L^{25}$.

</details>

The first two columns compare the numerical distribution of ⟨q|P|q⟩against the exact theoretical prediction for two representative microcanonical window sizes (ϵ = 0.04 and ϵ = 0.39). First column: for sufficiently small values of ϵ, our assumption holds very well. Second column: as ϵ increases beyond the RMT energy scale, ϵ &gt; ERMT, the assumption begins to break down. The rightmost columns quantify the degree of validity of this randomness assumption, plotting the KL and KS divergences as a function of the...</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** This paper calculates the stabilizer complexity of reduced density matrices derived from partially entangled thermal states in holographic CFTs. Using Wigner negativity, the authors show that this complexity is exponentially sensitive to a feature called the 'python's lunch' in the bulk geometry. This links quantum information measures directly to gravitational structure via AdS/CFT correspondence.

**Why it may be interesting.** While highly theoretical, the connection between quantum complexity measures (like stabilizer complexity) and bulk geometry (via AdS/CFT) provides a powerful tool for understanding entanglement structure in strongly coupled systems relevant to condensed matter physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the stabilizer complexity of the reduced density matrix for a partially entangled thermal (PET) state within the framework of holographic CFTs.

**Main result.** The relative Wigner negativity, which measures stabilizer complexity, is found to be exponentially enhanced by the presence of a 'python's lunch' region in the bulk geometry compared to its absence.

**Method.** The analysis employs concepts from quantum information theory (Wigner negativity) applied to holographic models, utilizing assumptions about spectral pseudorandomness and averaging over Haar random unitaries via the replica trick.

**Model / system.** The system involves PET states in a holographic CFT context, dual to black hole geometries containing a 'python's lunch' region. Calculations are performed on microcanonical truncations of these states, sometimes modeled using the SYK Hamiltonian.

**Key observables.** Wigner negativity ($N$), relative Wigner negativity $N(ho_R|ho^{(0)}_R)$, and geometric quantities like extremal surface areas ($A_{	ext{out}}, A_{	ext{min}}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The energy window size ($\epsilon$) must be small compared to the characteristic RMT energy scale ($E_{	ext{RMT}}$); $G_N$ (Newton's constant) appears in the exponent.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Assumes pseudorandomness of the CFT spectrum and heavy operator insertion; relies on fixed energy boundary conditions for universal statements.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the Euclidean path integral preparation of the PET state, depicting two black holes connected by a wormhole.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the physical system (PET states in holography) and key diagnostics (Wigner negativity) to deriving the complexity formula based on geometric areas. It then applies these methods using approximations derived from random matrix theory and tests them against specific models like SYK.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

In this note, we study the stabilizer complexity of the reduced density matrix corresponding to one side of a partially entangled thermal (PET) state with fixed energy boundary conditions in a holographic CFT. In particular, we study Wigner negativity, an operationally meaningful magic monotone which can be interpreted as the complexity of classically simulating any quantum circuit preparation of the reduced state on the subregion. Using assumptions on the pseudorandomness of the CFT spectrum and the heavy operator insertion, we observe that the Wigner negativity of the PET state relative to the microcanonical density matrix at the given energy is given by $\exp\left[\frac{1}{8G_N}(A_{\text{out}} - A_{\text{min}})\right]$, where $A_{\text{out}}$ is the area of the outer extremal surface, while $A_{\text{min}}$ is the area of the minimal extremal surface. Thus, the stabilizer complexity of the reduced density matrix on the boundary subregion is $O(1)$ in the absence of a python's lunch, but gets exponentially enhanced in the presence of a python's lunch in the bulk geometry.

</details>

**Summary.** This work investigates the pseudogap physics across the superconductor-insulator transition in disordered superconductors using both advanced tunneling spectroscopy and Quantum Monte Carlo simulations. The key finding is that a robust energy gap persists and grows with disorder deep into the insulating state, indicating localized Cooper pairing without global phase coherence. This establishes a crucial link between the high-temperature pseudogap and the insulating gap.

**Why it may be interesting.** The findings provide direct evidence for pairing mechanisms persisting deep into an insulating regime, which is highly relevant to understanding unconventional superconductivity in correlated materials.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To investigate the nature of the pseudogap in the insulating phase of highly disordered superconductors and establish a unified connection between this gap and the superconducting gap above Tc.

**Main result.** The single-particle gap survives across the superconductor-insulator transition, increases substantially with disorder, and fills upon increasing temperature rather than closing, confirming a Cooper-pair insulating state.

**Method.** Combining ultra-high-resistance planar tunneling spectroscopy on amorphous indium oxide films with Quantum Monte Carlo simulations of the attractive Hubbard model.

**Model / system.** The study uses highly disordered thin films undergoing an SIT, modeled by the attractive Hubbard Hamiltonian incorporating random onsite disorder. The system is probed via single-particle excitation spectra.

**Key observables.** Single-particle excitation spectrum (DOS), energy gap ($\Delta$), temperature dependence of spectral weight, and coherence peak behavior.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Disorder strength ($V$), interaction strength ($U/t$), Temperature ($T$), and the proximity to the Superconductor-Insulator Transition (SIT).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on interpreting tunneling conductance ($	ext{d}I/	ext{d}V$) as a measure of the single-particle density of states, and that QMC simulations accurately capture local pairing physics.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare DOS measurements showing gap growth with disorder (Fig. 1), demonstrate temperature filling rather than closing of the gap (Fig. 2), and illustrate spectral weight redistribution to high energies (Fig. 3).

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the problem by noting experimental difficulty, presents the theoretical framework using the attractive Hubbard model, details the combined experimental/simulation approach, and concludes by showing quantitative agreement confirming a localized Cooper-pair insulating state.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Disordered thin films undergoing a superconductor-insulator transition provide a controlled setting for studying pseudogap physics in the absence of competing electronic orders. Although theory predicts that local Cooper pairing can survive deep into the insulating phase, direct spectroscopic confirmation has remained experimentally inaccessible because tunneling measurements in highly insulating films require ultra-high-resistance junctions and picoampere current sensitivity. Here we combine ultra-high-resistance planar tunneling spectroscopy on amorphous indium oxide films with quantum Monte Carlo simulations of the attractive Hubbard model to probe the single-particle excitation spectrum deep in the insulating regime. We find striking agreement between experiment and theory: the single-particle gap not only survives across the superconductor-insulator transition, but increases substantially with disorder, reaching values more than twice those observed on the superconducting side. With increasing temperature, the gap fills rather than closes, while coherence peaks are suppressed and spectral weight redistributes to energies far exceeding the gap scale. Our results provide direct quantitative experimental confirmation of the theoretically predicted Cooper-pair insulating state with localized Cooper pairs and establish a unified connection between the pseudogap above $T_c$ and the insulating gap as manifestations of pairing without global phase coherence.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper develops a theoretical framework showing that the thermal Hall conductance of chiral superconductors is quantized and directly measures the system's topological invariant, the Chern number. Using graphene as a model, they predict that $\kappa_{xy}/T$ reads out this integer, offering a powerful diagnostic tool for detecting exotic superconducting phases.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a highly specific, experimentally testable prediction ($\kappa_{xy}/T$ quantization) linking abstract topological invariants (Chern numbers) to measurable transport properties in condensed matter systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to measure the Bogoliubov–de Gennes Chern number ($	ext{CBdG}$), an integer characterizing chiral Majorana modes in superconductors, which has proven difficult using traditional magnetic signatures.

**Main result.** The low-temperature thermal Hall conductance ($\kappa_{xy}/T$) directly reads out this topological integer $	ext{CBdG}$, providing a robust diagnostic tool for chiral superconductivity. The sign of this plateau reverses when imaging domain walls, confirming the topological nature of the state.

**Method.** Theoretical calculations employing the Kubo formula approach on the BdG Hamiltonian are used to predict quantized thermal transport coefficients based on calculated Chern numbers derived from momentum-space winding rules.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on chiral superconductivity, modeled using rhombohedral graphene. The theory utilizes the Bogoliubov-de Gennes (BdG) framework, splitting the Hamiltonian into symmetric and antisymmetric parts to isolate physical effects like trigonal warping and finite Cooper pair momentum.

**Key observables.** Low-temperature thermal Hall conductance ($\kappa_{xy}/T$), Chern number ($	ext{CBdG}$), Bogoliubov Fermi surface gap ($\delta_{BdG}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature ($T$), pairing amplitude ($\Delta$), graphene warping strength, and the vortex winding enclosed by occupied regions in momentum space.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The quantization of $\kappa_{xy}/T$ is assumed to hold at low temperatures, and the analysis relies on mapping topological invariants (Chern number) onto measurable transport quantities.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the finite-temperature response framework showing quantized plateaus for different Chern numbers ($	ext{CBdG}=1, 2$), and show how the thermal Hall conductance is related to the topological invariant.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the theoretical link between the chiral topology (Chern number) and the measurable low-temperature thermal Hall conductance. It details the Hamiltonian decomposition for graphene, calculates the Chern number using momentum-space winding rules, and predicts the quantized plateau $\kappa_{xy}/T \propto 	ext{CBdG}$.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

A chiral superconductor carries chiral Majorana modes along its edges, and a single integer, the Bogoliubov--de Gennes Chern number, counts them. Thirty years of candidate materials have not yielded a measurement of that integer, because the magnetic signatures usually invoked are not topologically protected. Rhombohedral graphene makes the question both urgent and answerable: magnetic imaging resolves rewritable time-reversal-breaking domains inside the superconducting phase, while quantum oscillations reveal a normal state too intricate to reconstruct pocket by pocket. We show that the low-temperature thermal Hall conductance returns the integer directly, with no such reconstruction. For band-projected pairing it equals the pairing-vortex winding enclosed by the occupied regions of momentum space. Splitting the intravalley Hamiltonian into symmetric and antisymmetric parts isolates the trigonal warping and finite Cooper pair momentum of the real material: the antisymmetric part is topologically inert, direct Chern calculations across $525$ parameter points show the invariant preserved, and one inequality marks where a Bogoliubov Fermi surface removes quantization. The plateau $κ_{xy}/T=(π^2k_B^2/6h)\,C_{\rm BdG}$ then reads out the integer, its sign reverses with the imaged domain, a written domain wall should carry $2|C_{\rm BdG}|$ Majorana channels, and the thermometry required already resolves single thermal quanta in encapsulated graphene at millikelvin temperatures.

</details>
