**Summary.** This work uses molecular dynamics simulations to study how ions interact with water molecules beyond simple diffusion models. By analyzing the isotopic mass dependence of structural and dynamic properties, the authors reveal multiple vibrational 'rattling' frequencies within the solvation shell. This provides a detailed spectroscopic fingerprint for understanding ion-water interactions relevant to biological processes.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical liquid dynamics, the detailed analysis of frequency spectra ($	ext{C}_{\#}(\omega)$) and their sensitivity to mass provides insights into quantum mechanical effects (like Zero-Point Energy corrections) within a many-body context, relevant for understanding energy landscapes in open quantum systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the coupled dynamics of ions and water molecules within the first hydration shell, aiming to move beyond simplified Brownian approximations.

**Main result.** The vibrational power spectra consistently show two or three distinct peaks, indicating multiple 'rattling frequencies' sensitive to isotopic mass, which may serve as signatures for ion solvation processes.

**Method.** Molecular Dynamics (MD) simulations are employed to calculate time-dependent correlation functions and spectral densities by systematically varying the isotopic mass of the ions.

**Model / system.** The system consists of various alkali and alkaline earth metal ions ($	ext{Li}$ to $	ext{Ba}$) and chloride in bulk liquid water, modeled using established force fields (TIP4P2005/MADRID-2019).

**Key observables.** Diffusion coefficients ($D'$), water residence time ($	au_w$), vibrational power spectra ($	ext{C}_{\#}(\omega)$), radial distribution functions ($g(r)$), and mean-square force ($\langle\mathbf{F}angle$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Isotopic mass dependence (varying $	ext{m}_{	ext{ion}}$) is a central parameter, analyzed via power-law scaling ($eta$ and $\gamma$ coefficients).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The Born-Oppenheimer approximation is assumed to hold, meaning interatomic potentials are invariant with isotopic mass.

**Figures summary.** Figures summarize the dependence of diffusion coefficients ($D'$) on ion radius and mass ($\log D'$ vs. $\log m!$), while tables provide quantitative comparisons of structural parameters ($r_{	ext{max}}, N_O$) and dynamic properties across different computational models and experiments.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically calculates static structure (RDFs, $N_O$) and dynamic transport properties ($D', 	au_w$) for multiple ion species. It then analyzes the isotopic mass dependence of these observables using spectral analysis to extract vibrational signatures.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The coupled dynamics of ions and water molecules in their first hydration shell impact a variety of processes including ion diffusion, selective ion transport in water-filled nanopores, and the kinetics of ion-pairing, ion adsorption, and metal-ligand binding reactions. In this work, we study these coupled dynamics for alkali metals (Li, Na, K, Rb, Cs), alkaline Earth metals (Mg, Ca, Sr, Ba), and chloride through the lens of their dependence on ion isotopic mass. Results are validated against previous measurements of the isotopic mass-dependence of ion diffusion coefficients in water and previous ab initio calculations of ion high-frequency dynamics in water. We find that the vibrational power spectra of ions in water consistently exhibit either two or three peaks, i.e., ions have several rattling frequencies within their solvations shells as previously reported for a subset of the species examined here. These frequencies have different sensititivies to isotopic mass that may serve as signatures of ion solvation processes (such as the tendency of ions to orient their first-shell water molecules) and that also may relate to Hofmeister-like effects including the relative affinity of different metals for ribonucleic acid (RNA).

</details>

**Summary.** This study investigates the critical behavior of the ferrimagnet $	ext{Eu}_2	ext{MnSi}_2	ext{O}_7$, demonstrating that its magnetic ordering transition is governed by long-range dipolar interactions. By measuring magnetization and performing neutron diffraction, the authors confirm mean-field critical exponents, establishing this material as a key platform for studying dipolar criticality in insulating magnets.

**Why it may be interesting.** The confirmation of mean-field criticality in a complex insulating ferrimagnet provides an experimental realization for testing universality classes beyond simple ferromagnets, which is relevant for understanding phase transitions in many interacting quantum systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To establish mean-field critical behavior driven by long-range dipolar interactions in an insulating ferrimagnet, extending previous findings limited to ferromagnets.

**Main result.** The material exhibits mean-field critical exponents ($eta \approx 0.42$, $\gamma \approx 0.9$) confirming that long-range magnetic dipole-dipole interactions govern the asymptotic critical behavior in this ferrimagnet.

**Method.** Magnetization measurements, neutron powder diffraction (NPD), and analysis of critical exponents using scaling laws (e.g., Kouvel-Fisher plots).

**Model / system.** The physical system is $	ext{Eu}_2	ext{MnSi}_2	ext{O}_7$, a melilite-type ferrimagnet with spin-only Eu$^{2+}$ and Mn$^{2+}$ moments. The critical behavior is modeled by considering the long-range magnetic dipole-dipole (MDD) interactions.

**Key observables.** Critical exponents ($eta, \gamma$), magnetic susceptibility ($\chi$), magnetization ($M$), and magnetic reflection intensities from NPD.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The system operates in a regime where MDD interactions dominate the critical fluctuations, leading to mean-field universality class behavior.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that dipolar interactions are the dominant long-range force controlling the asymptotic critical exponents, despite the presence of exchange interactions stabilizing the order.

**Figures summary.** Figures show temperature dependence of susceptibility ($\chi$), NPD data comparing 3 K and 20 K reflections, and plots used to extract critical exponents using scaling analysis.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically measures magnetic properties across temperatures, analyzes structural refinement via NPD to determine the magnetic space group ($P2_12'12'$), and then applies various thermodynamic/critical analyses (Arrott plot, scaling) to confirm mean-field critical exponents governed by dipolar interactions.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We report mean-field critical behavior in Eu$_2$MnSi$_2$O$_7$, a melilite-type ferrimagnet with spin-only Eu$^{2+}$ and Mn$^{2+}$ moments and negligible orbital contributions. Magnetization measurements combined with neutron powder diffraction reveal critical exponents close to the mean-field values, indicating that long-range dipolar interactions govern the asymptotic critical behavior in this insulating ferrimagnet. The refined magnetic structure, described by the magnetic space group $P2_12_1^\prime2^\prime$, exhibits a tilted ferrimagnetic configuration driven by the Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya interaction, reflecting the noncentrosymmetric nature of the lattice. These results extend the applicability of mean-field theory to complex insulating magnets and establish Eu$_2$MnSi$_2$O$_7$ as a platform for exploring ferrimagnetism and long-range interactions. To our knowledge, this is the first insulating ferrimagnet in which dipolar interactions drive mean-field criticality.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper proves a major result showing that every PPT quantum channel must eventually become entanglement-breaking. By establishing the finite entanglement-breaking index for all such channels, it strengthens conjectures about the structure of quantum operations. The methods involve advanced linear algebra applied to map theory, providing deep insights into how entanglement is lost over repeated applications of physical processes.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides fundamental structural results about quantum operations (channels) that are crucial for understanding decoherence, channel capacity, and entanglement dynamics in open quantum systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central problem is proving that every Positive Partial Transpose (PPT) quantum channel eventually becomes entanglement-breaking, establishing the eventual entanglement-breaking property for this class of channels.

**Main result.** The authors prove that every PPT linear map acting on a quantum system has a finite entanglement-breaking index ($	ext{nEB}(\Phi) < \infty$).

**Method.** The proof utilizes inductive arguments, decomposing the channel $\Phi$ based on spectral properties (Perron eigenmatrices) and relating the EB index to bounds derived from specific subclasses of maps like $k$-superpositive maps.

**Model / system.** The study concerns linear maps $\Phi: M_d(\mathbb{C}) 	o M_d(\mathbb{C})$ acting on quantum systems, characterized by their Choi matrix. Key concepts include PPT and Entanglement-Breaking (EB) channels.

**Key observables.** Entanglement-breaking index ($	ext{nEB}(\Phi)$), the structure of the Choi matrix $C\Phi$, and spectral properties like the Perron radius.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimension $d$ of the system, and indices related to map composition (e.g., $	ext{DSP}_2, 	ext{DSP}_3$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on structural decompositions of the channel into components supported by projections derived from eigenvalue problems.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds upon defining PPT and EB maps, establishing relationships between different classes of positive maps ($	ext{SP}_k$), and culminates in proving Theorem 1.1 via induction on the dimension $d$.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We prove that every PPT linear map has finite entanglement-breaking index, thereby establishing the eventual entanglement-breaking property of PPT channels in full generality. Furthermore, by utilizing completely positive maps with low entanglement dimensionality, we show that a large family of PPT maps, which strictly containing the class of 2-superpositive maps, has entanglement-breaking index bounded above by 3, uniformly in the dimension. In particular, these results provide strong evidence that the PPT-cubed conjecture may hold in full generality.

</details>

### [Formal Verification of Quantum Ancilla Safety](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13099v1)

**Authors:** Jiqi Li, Jingyi Mei, Wang Fang, Ji Guan  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.13099v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `correlated / nonlocal dissipation` **1/5** · `quantum measurements` **1/5**

<div class="figure-strip-hint">↔ Scroll figures horizontally</div>

</figure>

<img src="2608.13099_figures/2608.13099_fig2.jpg" alt="Figure 2">
<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 2.</b> Fig. 3: Quokka-Sharp and QCEC performance on MCX and Grover circuits.</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a comprehensive verification-and-repair framework to mathematically guarantee 'ancilla safety' in quantum circuits. It achieves this by reducing complex, hard-to-verify dirty ancilla checks into manageable algebraic commutativity tests involving Pauli operators. The resulting tool is efficient, scalable, and provides actionable diagnosis and repair routines for local faults.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on computation theory, the rigorous mathematical tools developed for subspace preservation and error characterization are highly relevant to understanding decoherence and state preparation fidelity in physical quantum hardware implementations.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is formally verifying 'ancilla safety'—ensuring temporary ancilla qubits are restored to a known state after complex quantum operations, especially when they start in unknown ('dirty') states.

**Main result.** The authors propose an efficient end-to-end framework that reduces dirty safety checks to independent clean safety checks, which are further reduced to algebraic commutativity conditions involving Pauli operators.

**Method.** They develop a two-step reduction strategy: first decomposing the problem into multiple clean ancilla checks, and then mapping each check onto checking commutation relations $[U, Q] = 0$ using specialized reflection operators $Q$.

**Model / system.** The model is general quantum circuits operating on data qubits ($W$) and ancillary qubits ($A$). Safety definitions distinguish between 'clean' (restoring to $|0angle^{\otimes m}$) and 'dirty' (restoring regardless of initial state $|\varphiangle_A$).

**Key observables.** The primary diagnostics are the commutator checks $[U, Q] = 0$, and failure modes are classified as LogicError or PhaseError.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Scalability is demonstrated up to thousands of qubits; the analysis involves unitary transformations $U$ acting on composite Hilbert spaces $\mathcal{H}_W \otimes \mathcal{H}_A$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The framework assumes purely unitary circuits without mid-circuit measurements or classical feed-forward for its core theorems. Repair routines are limited to separable error cases.

**Figures summary.** Not specified, but the notes reference Figure 1 illustrating clean vs. dirty ancilla usage and Table 3 comparing verification efficiency across benchmarks like Grover's algorithm.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from defining the physical problem (clean/dirty safety) to establishing theoretical equivalences via lemmas (subspace invariance $\iff$ commutation), culminating in a practical, efficient framework for diagnosis and repair using algebraic checks.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Ensuring ancilla safety is a critical correctness requirement for quantum compilation, since ancilla qubits are routinely introduced to implement complex operations with fewer gates and reduced depth. However, formally verifying this property is computationally hard due to state-space explosion in the number of qubits, particularly for dirty ancillae, which carry unknown initial states and must be restored after use. We propose an end-to-end verification-and-repair framework that rigorously addresses both clean and dirty ancilla safety. Our core contribution is a two-step reduction strategy: we first prove that verifying an $m$-qubit dirty ancilla register decomposes into $2m$ independent clean ancilla safety checks; subsequently, we reduce each clean ancilla safety instance to an algebraic commutativity check against Pauli-$Z$ and Pauli-$X$ operators. This approach yields an efficient and naturally parallel verifier and enables actionable diagnosis by classifying violations into logic errors and phase errors. Leveraging this diagnosis, we further design lightweight repair routines that append local single-qubit rotations to eliminate a broad class of local ancilla faults. We implement the full pipeline in a prototype tool using a dual-backend architecture combining decision diagrams and weighted model counting, and validate it on diverse circuits ranging from arithmetic benchmarks to Grover's algorithm. Our experiments demonstrate scalability to thousands of qubits and show that the proposed repairs effectively improve ancilla safety while preserving circuit functionality.

</details>

**Summary.** This work provides a comprehensive analysis of magnon bound states in the J1-J2 square-lattice model by solving the two-magnon Schrödinger equation using multiple rigorous methods. The key finding is that the introduction of the J2 coupling stabilizes a d-wave symmetric ground state, offering a clear physical picture for magnon pairing evolution controlled by effective dimensionality changes.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous comparison between different theoretical approaches (exact vs. perturbative) to solve many-body quantum problems in magnetic materials provides a robust framework for understanding collective excitations beyond simple mean-field treatments, which is relevant to strongly correlated electron physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates magnon bound states (BSs) and pairing mechanisms in quantum magnets, aiming to reconcile theoretical predictions with observed behavior.

**Main result.** Introducing the J2 term stabilizes a d-wave symmetric BS as the ground state, showing that the physics is governed by effective dimensional reduction/enhancement rather than being fundamentally different from continuum problems.

**Method.** The study employs advanced theoretical techniques, including deriving and comparing results from non-orthogonal spin-flip wavefunctions and the 1/S-expansion of the bosonized Hamiltonian to solve the two-magnon Schrödinger equation.

**Model / system.** The system is the Heisenberg S=1/2 square-lattice J1-J2 ferro-antiferromagnetic (FM-AFM) model. The analysis focuses on the two-magnon bound state problem governed by a derived Schrödinger equation in momentum space.

**Key observables.** Binding energies of the two-magnon BSs, symmetry of the ground state BS (d-wave), and phase boundaries for BS existence as a function of pair momentum K and model parameters J2.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The exchange couplings J1 and J2, the spin quantum number S=1/2, and the pair momentum vector K across the Brillouin zone.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The $1/S$-expansion is used for fluctuating ground states (AFM), while the non-orthogonal basis approach provides an exact result for the fully polarized FM-like model; care must be taken regarding non-orthogonality corrections in pairing problems.

**Figures summary.** Figure 17 illustrates threshold boundaries ($J_{2,c}$ vs $K_x$) showing distinct existence regions for d-wave BSs originating from different high-symmetry points ($\Gamma$ and M).

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the two-magnon bound state problem by deriving the governing Schrödinger equation using multiple formalisms. It then studies how the inclusion of J2 modifies the band structure, leading to the stabilization of a d-wave BS, and finally calculates critical parameters like $J_{c2}^2$ where this binding occurs.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

A comprehensive analysis of pairing and bound states of magnons in the Heisenberg $S\!=\!1/2$ square-lattice $J_1\!-\!J_2$ ferro-antiferromagnetic model is presented. We highlight the similarities and differences between the bound states of spin flips on a lattice and those of particles in the continuum. Magnon bound states at finite pair momentum are studied throughout the Brillouin zone and a convenient lattice partial-wave nomenclature is advocated. The mechanisms of enhanced stability or fragility of these bound states for the high-symmetry pair momenta are identified and quantified as relating to the effective dimensional reduction or enhancement, respectively. These effects are also shown to control the evolution of the bound states with the model parameters, providing a transparent framework for understanding magnon pairing in a more general setting. A method to determine the bound state phase boundaries is presented.

</details>

**Summary.** This work theoretically investigates the gapped Kondo effect, mapping out its complex phase diagram by analyzing how quantum and classical spin descriptions connect. The key finding is that these transitions are robustly characterized by changes in multiple topological invariants, such as Chern numbers. This provides a powerful topological lens for understanding strongly correlated impurity physics.

**Why it may be interesting.** The explicit use of topological invariants (Chern numbers) to characterize a many-body phase transition in an impurity model provides a powerful theoretical tool for understanding correlated electron physics beyond simple energy gap analysis.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the gapped Kondo effect, focusing on its topological characterization and the continuous interpolation between quantum-spin and classical-spin impurity coupling regimes.

**Main result.** The Kondo transition is characterized by changes in multiple topological invariants (Chern numbers), demonstrating a continuous connection between the quantum and classical phase diagrams.

**Method.** Numerical investigation using Lanczos transformation combined with a self-consistent configuration-interaction (RASCI) scheme to solve the many-body Hamiltonian.

**Model / system.** The model involves an impurity spin coupled via an antiferromagnetic exchange interaction to a conduction-electron system described by the gapped Qi-Wu-Zhang (QWZ) model, which serves as a prototypical 2D insulator.

**Key observables.** Topological invariants including conventional momentum-space Chern number $C(\mathbf{k})$, B-space Chern number $C(B)$, and S-space Chern number $C(S)$; Kondo correlation $\langle\hat{s} \cdot \hat{S}angle$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Kondo coupling strength ($J$), gap magnitude ($\Delta$), fictitious magnetic field strength ($B$), and impurity spin quantum number ($S$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The classical-spin limit is treated as an approximation, and the method relies on the electronic structure gap exceeding finite-size gaps for results to be robust.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate phase diagrams mapping critical couplings ($J_K(m)$ vs $m$) separating screened/unscreened states for both quantum ($\hat{S}$) and classical ($S$) spin limits, alongside spectral flow analysis.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the gapped Kondo effect using numerical methods, first establishing the topological characterization via Chern numbers, then demonstrating the continuous interpolation between quantum and classical regimes, and finally extending the study to overscreened two-channel effects.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The gapped Kondo effect describes the screening of an $S=\frac12$ impurity spin locally coupled via an antiferromagnetic exchange interaction to a conduction-electron system exhibiting a finite hard gap. Using a combination of a Lanczos transformation and a self-consistent configuration-interaction scheme, we numerically investigate the local phase diagram. Furthermore, we show that the different phases can be characterized by several topological invariants: the conventional momentum-space Chern number of the underlying two-dimensional host system, corresponding to a Chern insulator; the B-space Chern number, defined by coupling the impurity spin to a fictitious local magnetic field $\boldsymbol B$, in the limit $B \to 0$; and the S-space Chern number, defined for a classical impurity spin, i.e., a vector of fixed length. The classical-spin limit is obtained for $B \to \infty$. By varying the field strength, we can therefore continuously interpolate between quantum-impurity-spin and classical-impurity-spin Hamiltonians and investigate whether the corresponding phase diagrams are likewise continuously connected. The gapped underscreened Kondo effect is studied for an impurity spin $S>\frac12$ as well as in the classical-spin limit approached via $B \to \infty$ or $S \to \infty$. Different variants of scattering theory are employed to interpret the resulting phases. Finally, the gapped two-channel overscreened Kondo effect, realized by coupling a quantum impurity spin equally to the local electron spins of both orbitals within a unit cell, is shown to be characterized by spontaneous particle-hole symmetry breaking. This leads to a highly nontrivial quantum-classical phase diagram.

</details>

### [A Minimum-Cardinality Genuinely Unextendible Product Basis in Three Qutrits](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12785v1)

**Authors:** Fei Shi, Ge Bai, Xiande Zhang, Qi Zhao, Lvzhou Li  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.12785v1>

**Summary.** This paper resolves the open problem regarding the minimum size of a Genuinely Unextendible Product Basis (GUPB) in three-qutrit systems. The authors construct an explicit basis of cardinality 14, proving this is the minimum possible size for this system. They also demonstrate that the associated projector exhibits strong quantum nonlocality while remaining bound entangled across all bipartitions.

**Why it may be interesting.** The concept of GUPBs is central to understanding quantum nonlocality and separability criteria. Demonstrating a concrete example that exhibits strong nonlocality without violating PPT conditions provides deep insight into multipartite quantum correlations beyond simple entanglement measures.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The authors addressed the open question of determining the minimum cardinality required for a Genuinely Unextendible Product Basis (GUPB) in tripartite quantum systems.

**Main result.** They constructed an explicit three-qutrit GUPB with a minimum cardinality of fourteen, proving this is the smallest possible size for such a basis in this setting.

**Method.** The proof combines graph theory analysis (using orthogonality graphs) with rigorous mathematical constructions and computer-assisted searches to establish both existence and minimality bounds.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on three-qutrit systems ($\mathbb{C}^3 \otimes \mathbb{C}^3 \otimes \mathbb{C}^3$) in quantum information theory, generalizing the results to arbitrary local dimensions $d_A, d_B, d_C$.

**Key observables.** The constructed basis exhibits 'strong quantum nonlocality without entanglement,' and the projector onto its orthogonal complement is shown to be positive under partial transposition (PPT) and bound entangled across every bipartition.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Minimum cardinality = 14 for the three-qutrit case; local dimensions $d \ge 3$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The proof relies on Lemma 1, which provides necessary and sufficient conditions relating the UPB property to rank conditions on local vector subsets.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the local orthogonality graphs ($G_A, G_B, G_C$) whose union covers $K_{14}$, while Tables I-IV detail the specific vectors, determinant certificates, and index sets used in the construction and verification process.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the problem definition (GUPB), uses graph theory to constrain possible bases, constructs the explicit basis of size 14 for three qutrits, proves its minimality, generalizes the construction to higher dimensions, and finally analyzes the entanglement properties of the orthogonal complement.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

It has remained an open question whether a genuinely unextendible product basis (GUPB) exists. We resolve this problem by constructing an explicit three-qutrit GUPB of cardinality fourteen in the smallest tripartite Hilbert space in which a GUPB can exist. Together with the nonexistence of three-qutrit GUPBs of cardinality less than fourteen, our construction proves that fourteen is the minimum cardinality. A padding procedure further extends the construction to all tripartite systems whose local dimensions are at least three. As applications, the normalized projector onto the thirteen-dimensional orthogonal complement of the three-qutrit GUPB is positive under partial transposition and bound entangled across every bipartition, while the GUPB exhibits strong quantum nonlocality without entanglement.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces AutoQuREO, an automated framework designed to estimate and optimize resources across the entire quantum computing stack. It overcomes limitations of existing tools by using surrogate modeling and integrating optimization directly into deployment pipelines. This positions it as a general platform for exploring complex resource trade-offs necessary for practical quantum technology development.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a high-level, systematic tool for analyzing resource bottlenecks across the entire quantum stack, which is crucial for researchers designing algorithms or hardware architectures that must account for real-world constraints beyond idealized models.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The transition of quantum computing from proof-of-concept to practical utility requires optimizing algorithms across complex, heterogeneous hardware/software stacks.

**Main result.** AutoQuREO provides a general-purpose digital twin platform that systematically discovers unexploited resource trade-offs intractable with existing Quantum Resource Estimation (QRE) tools.

**Method.** The framework uses surrogate modeling via algorithmic profiling and neuro-symbolic learning to estimate layer-wise resources, integrating this into a multi-objective optimization pipeline.

**Model / system.** It models the entire quantum computing stack, including algorithms, compilation, error correction (QEC), and hardware execution layers, supporting NISQ through FTQC regimes.

**Key observables.** Resource trade-offs involving qubits, circuit depth, fidelity, classical control cost, logical/physical qubit counts, and wall-clock runtime.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The framework is designed to handle resource estimation across multiple computational regimes (NISQ, EFT, FTQC).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Existing QRE tools are often too compilation-heavy, domain-knowledge dependent, or strictly tied to long-term fault-tolerant assumptions.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the overall AutoQuREO workflow, detailing the process from user-defined stack layers through surrogate model creation to automated multi-objective optimization.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the framework by identifying limitations in current QRE methods, then details the four novel components of AutoQuREO (abstraction, modularity, surrogate modeling, optimization), and finally validates its utility through co-design case studies.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

As quantum computing progresses from proof-of-principle demonstrations toward practical utility, a significant impediment is the need to augment algorithmic feasibility with system-level optimization across heterogeneous hardware and software stacks. Quantum resource estimation (QRE) plays a central role in this transition, yet existing approaches remain largely compilation-heavy or domain-knowledge-guided symbolic annotations, and tightly coupled to long-term fault-tolerant assumptions, limiting their topical applicability.   In this work, we introduce AutoQuREO, an Automated framework for full-stack Quantum Resource Estimation and Optimization. AutoQuREO is built around four core novelties: (i) a flexible, user-defined abstraction of the quantum computing stack; (ii) a modular library of reusable stack components enabling rapid full-stack prototyping; (iii) surrogate modeling of layer-wise resources via algorithmic profiling and neuro-symbolic learning; and (iv) integrated multi-objective optimization that embeds QRE directly into deployment pipelines. Together, these design choices enable AutoQuREO to serve as a digital twin for quantum computing stacks, supporting the tractable exploration of complex design spaces.   We demonstrate the capabilities of AutoQuREO through representative co-design case studies, including early-fault-tolerant quantum algorithms, small error correction codes, gate decomposition and variational training of parametric quantum circuits. These examples illustrate how AutoQuREO enables systematic discovery of unexploited resource trade-offs that are computationally intractable or abstruse using existing QRE tools. AutoQuREO is positioned as a general-purpose platform for advancing quantum technology readiness.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops and proves the completeness of an extended set of flow-preserving rewrite rules within the ZX-calculus. By establishing this completeness, the authors provide a rigorous mathematical foundation to show that certain classes of quantum computations (those admitting efficient circuit extraction) can be fully manipulated using these graphical rules. This is vital for optimizing and verifying measurement-based quantum computation protocols.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a rigorous mathematical toolset—a complete rewriting system—to prove the equivalence and implementability of complex quantum circuits derived from abstract graphical models, which is crucial for optimizing quantum algorithms.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central problem is establishing completeness for flow-preserving rewrite rules within the ZX-calculus framework to ensure fully graphical reasoning about quantum computations.

**Main result.** The authors extend existing flow-preserving rules and prove that the resulting rule set is complete for all flow-preserving translations between appropriate ZX-diagrams.

**Method.** The proof involves a two-stage process: transforming diagrams with Pauli flow into those with causal flow (circuit extraction) using only flow-preserving rules, relying on established circuit rewriting completeness.

**Model / system.** The model is quantum computation represented by ZX-diagrams, particularly in the context of measurement-based quantum computation (MBQC). Flow properties (Pauli flow, gflow, causal flow) are key constraints ensuring deterministic implementability.

**Key observables.** The presence and type of 'flow' (e.g., Pauli flow, gflow, causal flow), which dictates the physical validity and efficient circuit extraction capability of the diagram.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The structure of the graph derived from the computation, including input/output vertices and measurement labels ($\lambda$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The work focuses on restricting to diagrams possessing a defined flow property (like Pauli or causal flow) to enable efficient circuit extraction; general ZX-diagrams are too complex.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 details the complete equational theory for the ZX-calculus, while Figure 2 contains the specific set of extended flow-preserving rewrite rules central to the completeness proof.

**Paper structure.** The paper reviews the ZX-calculus and its connection to MBQC. It then defines various graph-theoretic 'flow' properties (Pauli flow, gflow) for labelled open graphs. The core is proving that an extended set of flow-preserving rewrite rules achieves completeness for these constrained diagrammatic transformations.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Complete sets of graphical rewrite rules enable fully graphical reasoning about quantum computations and have been an area of active research for more than a decade. Many recent applications of the ZX-calculus have made use of the close correspondence between ZX-diagrams and computations in the one-way model of measurement-based quantum computation. In this model, various kinds of flow properties ensure deterministic implementability; for ZX-diagrams, these same properties allow efficient translation into quantum circuits (a problem that is known to be #P-hard in general). Therefore, flow-preserving ZX-calculus rewrite rules are of strong interest.   Here, we extend the set of flow-preserving rules appearing in the literature with a few new rules and extensions of existing rules. We then show that the resulting rule set is complete for all flow-preserving translations between ZX-diagrams of appropriate form. The proof employs a manifestly flow-preserving equivalent of circuit extraction, where a diagram with gflow is transformed, using only flow-preserving rewrite rules, into a diagram with causal flow.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper rigorously investigates the tensorization property associated with the Strong Data Processing Inequality for various quantum divergences. The authors demonstrate that this desirable property fails for both quantum $\chi$-square divergences and the quantum relative entropy using explicit counter-examples. This work highlights fundamental limitations in how information loss scales when considering composite quantum systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** The failure of such fundamental additivity properties (like tensorization) in quantum information theory is crucial because it indicates limitations in applying classical intuition from information theory to quantum channels.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates whether the tensorization property of the Strong Data Processing Inequality (SDPI) holds for quantum divergences.

**Main result.** It reports two negative results, demonstrating that the tensorization property fails for certain quantum $\chi$-square divergences and generally for the quantum relative entropy.

**Method.** The authors construct explicit counter-examples using specific noisy channels and states to show that the inequality relating the joint divergence to the maximum of individual divergences is violated.

**Model / system.** The analysis is conducted within quantum information theory, involving general quantum channels $E$ acting on density matrices $ho$, and considering composite systems via tensor products ($E \otimes E$).

**Key observables.** The key observable is the constant $\eta_D(E, \sigma)$ derived from the SDPI, and the divergences themselves include the quantum $\chi$-square divergence $D_\kappa$ and the quantum relative entropy $D_{QRE}$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Parameters include the damping parameter $\gamma$, state parameters $s$, and limits taken as $\epsilon 	o 0$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The failure of tensorization is shown for general cases, suggesting it is not a universal property in the quantum setting.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the SDPI and the concept of tensorization. It then presents separate sections detailing counter-examples: one using the amplitude-damping channel for $\chi$-square divergences, and another utilizing minimum output entropy non-additivity to disprove tensorization for $D_{QRE}$.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The data processing inequality is a fundamental property that describes the loss of information through noisy channels. A more refined description is characterized by the strong data processing inequality (SDPI). In classical information theory, the tensorization of strong data processing inequality holds for a whole family of $f$-divergences. However, its quantum counterpart is less known. The tensorization of SDPI was shown only for some special cases previously, and the general understanding about the tensorization property of SDPI for quantum divergences remains open. In this work, we report two negative results: the tensorization property fails for certain quantum chi-square divergences, and it also does not hold for the quantum relative entropy.

</details>

**Summary.** This work presents an equivariant machine learning framework to learn the excess free-energy functional for classical fluids directly from equilibrium density data. By enforcing local chemical potential balance, the model achieves remarkable transferability, accurately predicting macroscopic properties like phase behavior and interparticle forces in complex 3D environments without needing explicit labels for these targets.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical liquids, the rigorous application of deep learning principles (equivariance, local constraints) to derive fundamental thermodynamic potentials is highly relevant for developing predictive models in quantum many-body physics where exact functionals are unknown.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central challenge is developing a transferable, three-dimensional classical density functional theory (cDFT) that can predict complex thermodynamic and structural properties from equilibrium density fields.

**Main result.** The authors successfully learned the excess free-energy functional using only density data, enabling predictions of structure factors, equation of state, phase coexistence, and even non-monotonic forces in confined geometries.

**Method.** They introduced an equivariant learning framework (Equi-cDFT) that enforces local chemical potential balance ($\mu_{	ext{loc}, g} = 0$) to train the functional without requiring explicit free-energy or chemical-potential labels.

**Model / system.** The theory applies to classical fluids, modeled using Lennard-Jones interactions, and is tested in various geometries including bulk fluid, colloid suspensions, and porous materials like gyroid structures.

**Key observables.** Structure factor $S(k)$, equation of state ($P(ho, T)$), liquid–vapor coexistence curves, one-body direct correlation function ($c^{(1)}_g$), and effective forces ($\langle F angle_D$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature ($T$), density ($ho$), system size (e.g., $L=20\sigma$ for colloids), and external potential parameters.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method relies on enforcing the local chemical-potential balance condition ($\mu_{	ext{loc}, g} = 0$) as the primary learning signal, and it uses a finite receptive field approximation for transferability.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the learned functional architecture using symmetry-adapted features, show recovered thermodynamic properties like $S(k)$ and isotherms across different states, and benchmark performance on colloid bridging forces and adsorption in porous media.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the Equi-cDFT framework by enforcing local chemical potential balance. It then derives key observables (like $c^{(1)}_g$ and $c^{(2)}$) via functional differentiation. The results are benchmarked across homogeneous fluids to recover EOS/phase behavior, followed by applications to complex systems like colloid bridging and adsorption in porous media.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Liquids exhibit collective behavior that depends sensitively on thermodynamic conditions, interfaces and confinement, yet predicting each new state commonly requires a separate atomistic simulation. Classical density functional theory offers a reusable variational description, but its central excess free-energy functional is generally unknown, and learned approximations have largely remained restricted to planar or lower-dimensional settings. Here we show that this functional can be learned directly from fully three-dimensional equilibrium density fields while preserving spatial symmetry and variational consistency, without free-energy or chemical-potential labels. A single learned functional transfers across temperatures, system sizes and statistical ensembles, and recovers structure factors, the equation of state, liquid--vapor coexistence and interfacial broadening, none of which are used as training targets. Applied to complex three-dimensional geometries, it predicts the non-monotonic force associated with formation and rupture of a solvent-depleted bridge between colloids and adsorption in an interconnected gyroid pore. These results demonstrate that equilibrium density data can be converted into a transferable thermodynamic generator connecting microscopic liquid structure to response, phase behavior and collective phenomena.

</details>

### [Magnetism in antiperovskite (Li$_2$\textit{M})\textit{Ch}O (\textit{M} = Fe, Mn, Co; \textit{Ch} = S, Se) diluted magnets with fixed 1/3 filling: the key role of magnetic anisotropy](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13111v1)

**Summary.** This work characterizes the magnetism in antiperovskite magnets where the magnetic ions are randomly diluted. The authors find clear evidence for long-range antiferromagnetic order whose temperature is strongly suppressed by this dilution effect. They conclude that while disorder plays a role, the intrinsic magnetic anisotropy of the transition metal dictates the overall magnetic coupling strength.

**Why it may be interesting.** While primarily experimental, the focus on random site occupation and percolation-like behavior places it directly within the study of disordered magnetic systems, which shares conceptual links with spin glass physics studied in condensed matter theory.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how magnetic order evolves in antiperovskite magnets with a strongly diluted, randomly distributed magnetic sublattice at fixed 1/3 filling.

**Main result.** Long-range antiferromagnetic order is observed with increasing Néel temperatures ($T_N$) from Mn to Co compounds, and the ordering temperature is significantly diminished by magnetic dilution. The key controlling factor for $T_N$ appears to be the magnetic anisotropy of the transition metal ion.

**Method.** The research combines synthesis (high-energy ball milling) with extensive characterization, including static magnetic susceptibility ($\chi$), isothermal magnetization ($M(B)$), and specific heat measurements.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a series of lithium-rich antiperovskites, $(	ext{Li}_2	ext{M})	ext{ChO}$, where the random distribution of $	ext{Li}^+$ and $	ext{M}^{2+}$ ions creates a strongly diluted magnetic sublattice.

**Key observables.** Magnetic susceptibility ($\chi_{	ext{ZFC}}, \chi_{	ext{FC}}$), Néel temperatures ($T_N$), saturation magnetization ($M_{	ext{FM,sat}}$), and the Pauli-like high-field susceptibility ($\chi_0$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Fixed 1/3 filling; magnetic anisotropy of transition metals (Fe, Co, Mn); dilution level; temperature range up to $\sim 350	ext{ K}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the intrinsic magnetic properties can be extracted by carefully subtracting contributions from ferromagnetic impurities ($	ext{Fe}^0$, $	ext{Fe}_3	ext{O}_4$).

**Figures summary.** Figures show pXRD patterns, ZFC/FC susceptibility curves revealing $T_N$ anomalies, and specific heat measurements exhibiting step-like features indicative of magnetic ordering.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by characterizing the synthesis and impurity phases using XRD and SEM, followed by detailed magnetic measurements ($\chi(T), M(B)$) to determine the intrinsic magnetic coupling mechanisms and the role of anisotropy in diluted systems.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We report the magnetic properties of a series of lithium-rich antiperovskites (Li$_2M$)$Ch$O ($M$ = Fe, Co, Mn and $Ch$ = Se, S) where transition metal and lithium ions are randomly distributed on the X-sites of the X$_3$BA structure, thereby forming a strongly diluted magnetic sublattice. Our study hence enables us to investigate the evolution of magnetic order at fixed 1/3-filling -- which is in the vicinity but slightly above the percolation threshold -- upon variation of the spin size, the magnetic anisotropy, and the orbital configuration. The data imply the absence of a distinct Curie-Weiss behavior up to 350~K but show rather large and weakly temperature-dependent magnetic susceptibility. We observe clear signatures of long-range antiferromagnetic order evolving in the 1/3-filled and strongly diluted magnetic X-site lattice with increasing Néel temperatures from $T_{\rm{N}}\simeq 30$~K in (Li$_2$Mn)$Ch$O to $\simeq 50$~K in (Li$_2$Fe)$Ch$O and $70-90$~K in (Li$_2$Co)$Ch$O. Except for $M$ = Co, the chalcogenide has no sizable effect on $T_{\rm N}$. We conclude significant magnetic coupling and short-range magnetic correlations at well above $T_{\rm N}$ which is in line with the observation of a broad electron spin resonance signal at room temperature. The actual ordering temperatures are strongly diminished by magnetic dilution. While structural parameters such as the tolerance factor and bonding angles do not strongly affect $T_{\rm N}$, a key parameter is the magnetic anisotropy of the transition metals.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a novel topological framework using Topological Data Analysis (TDA) to secure Quantum Key Distribution over dynamic inter-satellite links. By modeling the network state as a Cellular Sheaf and deriving acceptance criteria from classical beacon telemetry, it aims to overcome phase sensitivity issues caused by orbital noise. While showing promising candidate rates, the authors caution that the full composable security proof is not yet complete.

**Why it may be interesting.** It directly addresses quantum networking challenges by integrating advanced mathematical tools (TDA) to handle complex, non-stationary environmental noise inherent in space-based quantum communication links.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The extreme phase sensitivity of Twin-Field QKD over dynamic Inter-Satellite Links (ISLs) makes traditional post-selection heuristics fail in non-IID orbital environments.

**Main result.** A reference-only topological post-selection oracle, utilizing Topological Data Analysis (TDA) on a Cellular Sheaf model, is proposed to derive public acceptance events from classical telemetry.

**Method.** The protocol models the constellation as a Cellular Sheaf and applies TDA metrics (like persistent homology) to filter out noise sources that violate security assumptions in finite-key analysis.

**Model / system.** The system is an ISL constellation undergoing dynamic orbital changes, modeled using physical components like pointing jitter (log-normal fades) and phase drift (Gauss–Markov process).

**Key observables.** Median conditional candidate rates ($2.14 	imes 10^{-6}$ vs $5.87 	imes 10^{-7}$ bit/pulse at 2,000 km), accepted-block fraction, and topological metrics like persistence energy.

**Important parameters / regimes.** ISL separations (2,000–5,000 km); simulation parameters include aperture size (0.40 m) and repetition rate ($
u=1.0$ GHz).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The exported rates are only *conditional numerical candidates*; the full composable-security proof remains incomplete, resulting in a certified composable rate of zero.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare TDA-enabled vs. disabled configurations showing candidate rates and accepted fractions across different distances, with results confirming zero performance at 3,000–5,000 km.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the problem (TF-QKD vulnerability), introduces the topological framework using Cellular Sheaves/TDA on public telemetry, simulates the protocol under noise, and compares TDA's effect against baseline methods while noting the incompleteness of the final security proof.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Inter-satellite links (ISLs) are the mandatory backbone for global quantum networks. While Twin-Field Quantum Key Distribution (TF-QKD) successfully surpasses linear rate-loss bounds, its extreme phase sensitivity makes it highly vulnerable to dynamic, non-IID (Independent and Identically Distributed) orbital environments. In composable finite-key analyses governed by the Generalized Entropy Accumulation Theorem (GEAT), traditional adaptive post-selection heuristics either violate strict independence conditions or incur massive second-order penalties that collapse the secret key rate. To overcome this, we introduce a reference-only topological post-selection oracle. By modeling the constellation as a Cellular Sheaf and applying Topological Data Analysis (TDA), our protocol derives a public acceptance event ($Ω$) exclusively from classical beacon telemetry. To rigorously validate this mechanism, we develop a modular simulation framework equipped with stochastic noise injection and an explicit GEAT security ledger. Simulations across 2,000-5,000 km ISL separations compare the same Hodge-Koopman gate with TDA disabled and enabled. At 2,000 km, the median conditional candidate rates are $2.14 \times 10^{-6}$ and $5.87 \times 10^{-7}$ bit per emitted pulse, respectively; both configurations return zero at 3,000-5,000 km. TDA is active in all 4,788 evaluated windows, but does not extend the positive-candidate range in this scenario. These exported rates are conditional numerical candidates: the full protocol-level composable-security proof remains incomplete and the certified composable rate is therefore zero throughout.

</details>

**Authors:** Jacopo Rizzo, Lorenzo Leone  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.13376v1>

**Summary.** The paper introduces 'universal magic state concentration,' a fixed protocol that converts multiple copies of an unknown pure qubit state into a target magic state required for universal quantum computing. It rigorously identifies the linearized order-three stabilizer Rényi entropy as the key resource measure, establishing optimal thresholds and asymptotic rates for this process.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a deep theoretical understanding of resource quantification in quantum computation, linking fundamental entropy measures directly to operational limits for state preparation.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses developing a universal magic state concentration protocol—a fixed stabilizer method to convert multiple copies of an unknown pure non-stabilizer qubit state into an exact target magic state.

**Main result.** They establish that the linearized order-three stabilizer Rényi entropy, $M_{	ext{lin}}^3(\psi)$, is the fundamental operational quantity governing this concentration. Specifically, they show a sharp six-copy threshold for achieving universal CCZ injection from any pure non-stabilizer state.

**Method.** The analysis employs advanced quantum information theory tools, including stabilizer Rényi entropies and bounding techniques derived from Pauli measurements and algebraic constraints on the symmetric subspace.

**Model / system.** The system is abstractly defined by single-qubit pure states $|\psiangle$ used as resources to generate a target magic state (specifically $	ext{CCZ}$ or $	ext{T}$-state) required for universal quantum computation.

**Key observables.** Success probability of distillation protocols ($	ext{Pr}_{	ext{opt}}$), the linearized order-three stabilizer Rényi entropy ($M_{	ext{lin}}^3(\psi)$), and asymptotic distillation rates $R(\psi 	o 	ext{CCZ})$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of input copies ($k$), particularly the optimal threshold at $k=6$, and the scaling behavior governed by $M_{	ext{lin}}^3(\psi)$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Protocols must be fixed (independent of the specific input state $\psi$) and operate using stabilizer measurements; the analysis often assumes pure-state inputs.

**Figures summary.** Not specified in the notes, but the structure implies showing explicit formulas for success probabilities vs. $k$ and asymptotic rate bounds.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first defining the problem of universal concentration, then deriving necessary mathematical tools (like stabilizer entropies), establishing sharp thresholds using specific copy numbers ($k=6, 8$), and finally proving general upper bounds on the achievable rates.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Magic plays a dual role in quantum computation: it promotes stabilizer dynamics from efficient classical simulability to universality, but it presents a central challenge for fault tolerance, since non-stabilizer operations are harder to protect against noise. Magic state distillation addresses this issue; however, existing protocols typically assume prior structure in the input, such as proximity to the target or a specified noise model. Here we introduce universal magic state concentration: a fixed stabilizer protocol that converts a few copies of an unknown pure non-stabilizer qubit state into an exact target magic state. Motivated by the obstruction to exact $T$-state concentration, we show that $\mathrm{CCZ}$ states behave fundamentally differently. Six input copies are necessary and sufficient to distill one exact $\mathrm{CCZ}$ state, with an optimal success probability determined by the linearized order-three stabilizer Rényi entropy $M^{\mathrm{lin}}_3$. Beyond this, we show that $M^{\mathrm{lin}}_3$ governs the optimal state dependence of any protocol up to nine input copies, and we showcase an eight-copy protocol with improved success probability. Furthermore, block repetition of our protocols yields asymptotic distillation rates that achieve optimal scaling up to logarithmic factors. As a corollary, any unknown pure qubit magic state suffices for universal quantum computation via exact $\mathrm{CCZ}$ injection. Together, these results identify the stabilizer Rényi entropy as a fundamental operational quantity in magic state distillation.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper presents a general framework to mathematically certify when a desired unitary transformation cannot be implemented by a given quantum circuit architecture. By formulating the problem as an SDP optimization over fidelity, it generates quantitative 'witnesses' of incompatibility. This is crucial for resource estimation and benchmarking real quantum hardware.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides powerful mathematical tools (SDP/LP) to rigorously prove limitations on quantum computation resources, which is fundamental knowledge for designing fault-tolerant quantum hardware or optimizing algorithms.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is determining if a target unitary transformation can be realized using a specific, prescribed quantum circuit architecture. The authors aim to provide rigorous certificates of incompatibility where existing methods are only constructive.

**Main result.** They introduce a general framework using 'quantum circuit architecture witnesses' derived from maximizing fidelity via Semidefinite Programming (SDP). This yields quantitative lower bounds on implementation resources like gate count or depth.

**Method.** The compatibility check is formulated as an optimization problem maximizing the fidelity between the target unitary's Choi state and those achievable by circuits compatible with the given architecture. For Clifford unitaries, this reduces to a Linear Program (LP).

**Model / system.** The model centers on quantum circuits acting on qubits, analyzed using the Choi-Jamiołkowski isomorphism to represent unitary channels. The compatibility is tested against a prescribed circuit structure $\Gamma$.

**Key observables.** The optimized fidelity ($\alpha_\Gamma(U)$) is the key diagnostic; if it is less than 1, incompatibility is certified.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Target Unitary ($U$), Circuit Architecture ($\Gamma$), Fidelity ($\alpha_\Gamma(U)$), and the resulting Witness ($W$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The general optimization problem requires relaxation to an SDP due to non-convex constraints. Specific simplifications rely on exploiting the stabiliser formalism for Clifford unitaries or assuming graph state structures.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate circuit decomposition examples, showing how compatibility is tested between target and test architectures using sequential gates (e.g., two-qubit gates).

**Paper structure.** The paper develops a general framework based on SDPs for witness construction, specializes this to LPs for Clifford unitaries, explores alternative relaxations like the quantum comb approach, and concludes with analytical derivations for specific staircase architectures.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Determining whether a target unitary can be implemented within a prescribed quantum circuit architecture is a fundamental problem in quantum information, with direct implications for optimisation and compilation of quantum circuits, and hardware-efficient quantum computation. While existing synthesis and compilation methods are primarily constructive, they generally do not provide rigorous certificates that a unitary cannot be realised using given implementation resources. Here we introduce a general framework to define quantum circuit architecture witnesses, which certify the incompatibility of a unitary transformation with a specified quantum circuit architecture. We formulate the witness construction as a semidefinite program by maximising the fidelity between the Choi state of the target unitary and those of tested circuits. The resulting witnesses provide practical and quantitative certificates of incompatibility, implying lower bounds on implementation resources such as the gate count or circuit depth, and can also be used experimentally to benchmark quantum devices by certifying that an implemented unitary channel goes beyond the capabilities of a given circuit architecture. For Clifford unitaries, we exploit the stabiliser formalism to reduce the construction to linear programming, enabling both more efficient numerical certification for circuits containing on the order of seven two-qubit gates, and analytical witnesses for some families of architectures made of an arbitrary number of gates.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents advanced theoretical techniques for synthesizing quantum circuits intended for distributed quantum processors. It minimizes the number of required non-local gates by abstracting the circuit into block matrices and applying optimized elimination procedures. This research provides crucial algorithmic tools for scaling up quantum computation beyond single, monolithic qubit systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the resource overhead associated with implementing large quantum computations across physically separated modules, which is a major bottleneck in realizing fault-tolerant quantum computers.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is synthesizing equivalent quantum circuits for distributed architectures while minimizing the number of costly non-local gates, especially when connectivity is restricted.

**Main result.** The authors provide asymptotically optimal synthesis methods (BlockRowCol and DistRowCol) that bound the required non-local gates based on circuit structure and partitioning.

**Method.** The approach uses block-matrix Gaussian elimination techniques applied to an abstracted Pauli exponential circuit representation, allowing optimization across multiple physical partitions.

**Model / system.** The model is distributed quantum computing architectures where $n$ qubits are divided into $k$ smaller, interconnected blocks. The circuits involve Clifford and generalized CNOT gates.

**Key observables.** The primary observable minimized is the total number of non-local (inter-block) gates required for synthesis.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Key parameters include the total number of qubits ($n$), the number of partitions ($k$), and the specific connectivity constraints imposed on the architecture.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes that local Clifford operations can be abstracted away, allowing focus on non-local gate synthesis; some practical details require heuristic optimization.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the CNOT circuit optimization process: showing conversion to a block parity matrix and subsequent synthesis using BlockRowCol into a final circuit realization.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops representations for distributed Clifford+RZ circuits, focusing on capturing non-local operations via commutation DAGs. It then details specific synthesis algorithms (BlockRowCol) and compares their asymptotic scaling against existing methods like Patel-Markov-Hayes' algorithm.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

To achieve large-scale fault-tolerant quantum computation, it may be easier to combine many small sets of qubits than to construct a single large set. For example via quantum error correction with block codes, or distributed quantum processors utilizing shared entanglement. In these regimes, the time or error budget of the overall quantum computation may be dominated by non-local operations. Hence, it is worthwhile to minimize the number of these operations. We consider the case where both non-local and local connectivity may be arbitrarily restricted, and give an asymptotically optimal synthesis method for distributed CNOT and Clifford circuits, based on block-matrix Gaussian elimination. We extend this to all Clifford+RZ circuits by generalizing the Pauli exponential circuit representation; this naturally integrates with existing methods for optimizing T-count. As an application, we show how to implement CNOT circuits in a CSS code encoding n logical qubits in k blocks using O(nk) inter-block transversal CNOTs and intra-block Pauli measurements.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes large-scale quantum networks connecting users via challenging free-space channels. It derives capacity limits based on atmospheric and diffraction losses, demonstrating that integrating an optimally placed fiber backbone—determined by a Voronoi tessellation—significantly boosts overall network performance and connectivity compared to purely free-space links.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides crucial theoretical frameworks for scaling quantum internet infrastructure by quantifying the synergistic benefits of combining free-space links with high-fidelity fiber backbones, which is vital for practical quantum networking design.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study focuses on maximizing the end-to-end capacity of large-scale quantum communication networks operating in free space, while optimizing performance by integrating fiber-based backbone links.

**Main result.** Optimal network reinforcement requires placing backbone nodes according to a capacity-maximizing Voronoi tessellation, which substantially improves both end-to-end capacity and reduces edge consumption for both mobile and fixed users.

**Method.** The authors derive mean network capacity, analyze connectivity phase transitions using graph theory models (Waxman graphs), and employ geometric optimization techniques like the centroidal Voronoi tessellation.

**Model / system.** The system is a quantum communication network connecting randomly distributed nodes (fixed or mobile) via free-space links susceptible to diffraction and atmospheric loss, augmented by high-capacity fiber backbone segments.

**Key observables.** Network capacity ($	ext{Q}_2$, $	ext{D}_2$, $	ext{K}_2$), edge consumption, connectivity phase transitions, and end-to-end capacity as a function of backbone node placement.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Diffraction length ($R_0$), atmospheric attenuation coefficient ($\alpha(h)$), pointing error variance ($\sigma_{pe}^2$), nodal density ($ho$), and the number/placement of backbone nodes ($n_b$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The optimal backbone configuration must correspond to a capacity-maximizing Voronoi tessellation, which is approximated by Lloyd's algorithm; analysis distinguishes between fixed (turbulence dominant) and mobile (pointing error dominant) user regimes.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare capacity bounds versus distance for fiber vs. free-space links, and illustrate the improvement in multi-path capacity when a fiber backbone is introduced across varying nodal densities.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes link loss mechanisms (diffraction, atmosphere) leading to capacity bounds; it then models network performance using random graph theory, culminating in the proof that Voronoi tessellation dictates optimal backbone placement for capacity maximization.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Free-space quantum communication provides a flexible complement to fiber-based quantum networks, but its point-to-point capacity is fundamentally limited by diffraction, atmospheric extinction and beam wandering induced by turbulence. In this work, we study the end-to-end performance of large-scale free-space quantum networks connecting randomly distributed fixed or mobile users, modelled as Waxman random graphs. We derive the mean network capacity, edge consumption and connectivity phase transitions for both single-path and multi-path (flooding) routing. We also study router-centered star networks, deriving the full distribution of end-to-end capacities as a function of the router's coverage radius. We then consider how performance may be improved by reinforcing free-space networks with a small number of optimally placed fiber-based backbone nodes. We prove that any optimal backbone configuration must correspond to a capacity-maximizing Voronoi tessellation of the network region, and show that this can be efficiently approximated by a centroidal Voronoi tessellation via Lloyd's algorithm, with backbone nodes connected according to a Delaunay triangulation. Numerical results show that even a modest number of backbone nodes substantially improves end-to-end capacity and reduces edge consumption for both mobile and fixed users.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper develops a novel method to test Dyson universality by reconstructing an effective Hamiltonian from known spectra. By analyzing the matrix elements in shells corresponding to energy transfer distances, the authors show that the GUE nature of Riemann zeros is revealed through this geometric structure rather than direct level spacing analysis. This suggests that global spectral geometry can encode deep physical symmetries.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, non-local diagnostic tool for classifying quantum systems based purely on spectral data, suggesting that global geometric properties (like shell moments) can encode fundamental symmetries (Dyson index) even when local statistics are insufficient or obscured.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates whether Dyson universality, typically seen in local eigenvalue statistics, can be recovered by analyzing the global geometry of an operator reconstructed from a given spectrum.

**Main result.** The GUE character of the Riemann zeta function zeros is recovered not from direct level spacing statistics, but from the distance-resolved matrix element geometry of the reconstructed Hamiltonian operator.

**Method.** The authors use an inverse-spectral reconstruction technique involving a dressing transformation to map input spectra (like those from Gaussian ensembles or zeta zeros) onto a deformed harmonic oscillator potential. The resulting operator is then analyzed by resolving its matrix elements into shells based on energy transfer distance.

**Model / system.** The analysis compares spectral data from three types of Gaussian $\beta$-ensembles (GOE, GUE, GSE) against the nontrivial zeros of the Riemann zeta function. A fixed reference harmonic oscillator serves as the basis for reconstructing the effective Hamiltonian $H(s)$.

**Key observables.** Distance-shell moments ($M^{(s)}_p$) and normalized ratios comparing these moments to the GUE expectation ($R(s/	ext{GUE})_p$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The Dyson index $\beta$, the moment order $p$ (up to 10), and the height $T$ of the Riemann zeros.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that resolving the matrix element weight into distance shells provides a robust diagnostic tool, and it notes that the residual deviation from GUE decreases with increasing height $T$.

**Figures summary.** Figures show the continuous variation of moment ratios with $\beta$ (Fig. 1a), compare these ratios for GOE/GUE/GSE vs Riemann zeros (Fig. 1b), and demonstrate that the residual deviation from GUE approaches unity as height $T$ increases (Fig. 2).

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the diagnostic using Gaussian $\beta$-ensembles, showing continuous resolution of $\beta$. It then applies this method to Riemann zeros, demonstrating recovery of the GUE sector via distance-resolved geometry. Finally, it performs consistency checks on parameter variations and fractal structure analysis.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Dyson universality typically manifests itself in local eigenvalue statistics. Here we show that its signature survives a nonlinear inverse-spectral reconstruction and reappears in the matrix geometry of the reconstructed operator. Using a dressing transformation, we map each unfolded spectrum to a deformation $f(x)$ of a fixed harmonic oscillator and represent it in the common oscillator basis by $F_{mn}=\bra m|f|n\ket$. We resolve the matrix-element weight into shells of fixed distance $d=|m-n|$, corresponding to the energy-transfer channels of the reference oscillator, and characterize the resulting distribution by distance-shell moments. Independently calibrated on Gaussian $β$-ensembles, these moments vary smoothly with $β$ and distinguish the GOE, GUE, and GSE. With this calibration fixed, applying the same diagnostic to the nontrivial zeros of the Riemann zeta function places the reconstructed operators in the GUE sector. Thus, the GUE character of the zeros is recovered not through direct statistics of the input levels, but from the distance-resolved geometry of the reconstructed operator.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper addresses the challenge of creating highly accurate approximations of truly random quantum unitaries. By introducing a new Pauli-mixing bound, the authors achieve strong unitary $k$-designs in optimal logarithmic depth ($\Theta(\log n)$). This result is critical for simulating complex physical phenomena like scrambling using limited quantum resources.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides fundamental tools (strong unitary designs) that are essential benchmarks for simulating complex quantum dynamics, such as scrambling rates measured via OTOCs, within realistic circuit constraints.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is constructing strong unitary designs that accurately approximate Haar-random unitaries while achieving optimal, low circuit depth.

**Main result.** The authors successfully construct approximate strong unitary $k$-designs in optimal $\Theta(\log n)$ all-to-all circuit depth using only the original system qubits.

**Method.** The construction relies on a novel logarithmic-depth Pauli-mixing bound for the perfect-matching ensemble, which is combined with existing design and gluing techniques to build high-order designs layer by layer.

**Model / system.** The context involves unitary evolution in quantum systems, where strong unitary designs are used as finite-moment approximations to Haar randomness. These designs are crucial for simulating physical processes like scrambling and black-hole dynamics.

**Key observables.** Measurable error ($	ext{Err}_{	ext{full}}^k(E)$), design order $k$, and the required circuit depth scaling $\Theta(\log n)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Design order $k$ and measurable-error tolerance $\epsilon$. The optimal depth scales logarithmically with system size $n$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The construction assumes that a logarithmic-depth Pauli-mixing bound can be established for the perfect-matching ensemble, which controls the mixed forward-reverse two-query case.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the quantum comb formalism used to model mixed-query experiments and show the circuit architecture for achieving approximate strong $k$-designs through gluing protocols involving local patches and global shells.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds upon previous work by first establishing a logarithmic-depth Pauli-mixing bound. It then uses this bound in conjunction with strong gluing lemmas to construct higher-order designs, culminating in the optimal $\Theta(\log n)$ depth result for strong $k$-designs.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Unitary designs provide finite-moment approximations to Haar-random unitaries, with wide-ranging applications across physics and quantum information, from scrambling and black-hole dynamics to foundational primitives in quantum algorithms. Strong unitary designs capture a more demanding operational notion of approximation, requiring indistinguishability from Haar randomness even for quantum algorithms that may access a unitary not only in the forward direction, but also through its inverse, transpose, and complex conjugate. Motivated by the physical requirement that scrambling arise within the system itself, Schuster, Ma, Lombardi, Brandão, and Huang (arXiv:2509.26310) left open whether strong unitary designs can be generated in logarithmic depth using only the system qubits. For every fixed design order $k$ and measurable-error tolerance, we construct strong approximate unitary $k$-designs in optimal $Θ(\log n)$ all-to-all circuit depth using only the $n$ original system qubits. Our new ingredient is a logarithmic-depth Pauli-mixing bound for the perfect-matching ensemble, whose layers pair the qubits uniformly at random and apply independent random two-qubit gates. This bound controls the mixed forward-reverse two-query case, which we combine with existing design and gluing results to obtain strong unitary designs of arbitrary fixed order.

</details>
