**Summary.** This paper theoretically investigates two coupled nonlinear oscillators modeling a synthetic antiferromagnet. It determines the system's stable steady states and analyzes small deviations to find characteristic oscillation modes. The findings predict distinct spectral behaviors, such as level-attraction versus level-repulsion, providing guidance for interpreting spectroscopic measurements in these complex quantum magnetic systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses open quantum systems and non-Hermitian physics, which are central topics in modern AMO physics, particularly concerning driven cavity QED or magnetic resonance experiments where dissipation is key.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study analyzes the dynamics, steady states, and oscillation modes of two coupled, driven, dissipative nonlinear oscillators exhibiting non-Hermitian coupling.

**Main result.** The system exhibits multistability, and the spectral response can display distinct level-attraction or level-repulsion patterns depending on which mode is driven and the driving strength.

**Method.** The analysis involves determining steady states by solving nonlinear equations and then linearizing the dynamics around these points to find normal modes via stability analysis.

**Model / system.** The model describes a synthetic antiferromagnet composed of two ferromagnetic layers, whose coupled oscillation modes (acoustic and optical) are governed by driven, dissipative, non-Hermitian differential equations.

**Key observables.** Steady states, normal mode frequencies, spectral patterns (level-attraction/repulsion), and multi-frequency normal modes.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Driving field amplitude and frequency ($\omega_d$), detuning parameters ($\omega_{ac}, \omega_{op}$), and coupling strengths ($g_3$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on approximations such as assuming purely exponential time dependence for the steady state, neglecting certain counter-rotating field components, and linearizing deviations around the found steady states.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the schematic of the synthetic antiferromagnet, parametric plots showing the existence and stability of multiple steady states (up to three), and spectra mapping normal mode frequencies versus system parameters for different driving regimes.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first setting up the coupled nonlinear equations, then analyzing the conditions for stable steady states through algebraic solutions, followed by linearizing these equations to derive the spectrum of small oscillations (normal modes) under various driving conditions.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We analyze the dynamics of two harmonic oscillators with nonlinear, non-Hermitian coupling between them. Specifically, we consider a synthetic antiferromagnet composed of two ferromagnetic layers with antiferromagnetic interactions between the two layers, leading to the emergence of two oscillation modes of the combined system. We analyze the response of this system to external driving fields. We determine the allowed steady states under various driving conditions. We then analyze the dynamics of small deviations away from the different steady states, which reveals the normal modes that can be probed using spectroscopic techniques in experiment. As expected, the nonlinear system exhibits multistability, in which multiple steady states can exist for the same driving conditions. We find that the dynamical response can be drastically different depending on which one of the two modes is driven and on the strength of the driving. Specifically, we can obtain level-repulsion or level-attraction patterns in the spectrum. In addition to the rich variety of spectra, we find that some steady states exhibit normal modes that contain multiple frequencies. Our results explain recent experiments on synthetic antiferromagnets and provide guidance for designing experiments that can explore previously unseen phenomena in these systems.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper presents a method to implement vector spin summation in a graphene spintronic platform, crucial for developing advanced probabilistic computers. By using dual ferromagnetic injectors, the authors achieve continuous electrical control over the resultant spin vector's direction and magnitude. This work combines novel experimental measurements with sophisticated circuit modeling to build a scalable pathway for non-Boolean computing architectures.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses hardware implementations for quantum/probabilistic computation by mapping abstract Hamiltonian models (Heisenberg) onto solid-state spintronic devices, which is highly relevant to physical realization of quantum information processing concepts.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The challenge is advancing probabilistic computing beyond simple binary bits by realizing and controlling vector spin information, which is necessary for modeling complex energy-based systems like the Heisenberg Hamiltonian.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrated a spintronic platform capable of performing real-space vector summation of two spin vectors using dual ferromagnetic injections into graphene, enabling continuous control over the resultant spin state's magnitude and direction.

**Method.** The work combines electrical measurements (Non-Local Spin Valve techniques) with advanced theoretical modeling, employing a generalized 'spin-circuit approach' to simulate spin transport in graphene channels.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a spintronic device built on monolayer graphene using ferromagnetic contacts. The model generalizes charge circuits by representing nodes with four components (charge + three spins) and uses $4 	imes 4$ matrices for conductance elements to describe spin-dependent transport.

**Key observables.** Non-local resistance ($\Delta R_{NL}$), resultant spin polarization angle, total electrical resistance ($R_{total}$), and spatial profiles of electrochemical potentials ($\mu$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Operating temperature (20 K), current injection ratios ($I_5/I_6$), and the ratio of spin-diffusion length to channel length ($\lambda_s/L \approx 1$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analytical derivation relies on approximations such as low interface conductance ($G_< \ll 1$) and specific forms for charge and spin transport matrices.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the NLSV setup, showing schematic diagrams of dual injection, non-local resistance measurements ($\Delta R_{NL}$ vs $R_{total}$), and simulated/measured rotations of the resultant spin vector angle based on current control.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from identifying the need for vector spin control in probabilistic computing to detailing the physical graphene spintronic platform, presenting experimental results showing continuous vector summation via dual injection, and finally validating these findings using a comprehensive $4 	imes 4$ matrix spin-circuit simulation model.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Probabilistic bits (p-bits) have emerged as a cornerstone of probabilistic computing, enabling energy-efficient hardware implementation for probabilistic inference and combinatorial optimization. A critical challenge in advancing this field beyond binary p-bits lies in realizing and manipulating vector spin information, essential for mapping complex energy-based models such as the Heisenberg Hamiltonian.Here, we demonstrate a spintronic platform capable of real-space vector summation by using dual ferromagnetic spin injections into a monolayer graphene channel. By electrically tuning the spin polarization through independently controlled injection currents, we achieve continuous control over the magnitude and direction of the resulting spin accumulation vector. Experimental observations, supported by theoretical vector summation models and spin-circuit simulations, reveal coherent vector interactions and angular tunability of the spin state. This approach enables direct implementation of vector-based spin logic and lays the groundwork for mapping classical Heisenberg models using stochastic low-barrier magnets. Our results establish a scalable pathway for realizing probabilistic spin circuits based on two-dimensional materials, offering new opportunities for low-power, non-Boolean computing architectures.

</details>

**Summary.** This review provides a comprehensive theoretical roadmap for understanding thermal transport in crystals by connecting disparate physical descriptions. It bridges quantum phonon theories, like the Dyson equation, down to mesoscopic hydrodynamic models that predict complex heat flow behaviors beyond simple Fourier's law. The work is crucial for guiding experimental detection and control of non-diffusive heat transfer phenomena.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on phonons, the methodology—bridging quantum field theory (Green's functions) to continuum hydrodynamics—is highly relevant to open quantum systems where dissipation and non-equilibrium dynamics are key.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses how thermal transport occurs in crystalline solids when driven out of equilibrium by a temperature gradient, requiring theoretical descriptions across multiple physical scales.

**Main result.** It demonstrates that advanced hydrodynamic models predict not only standard heat flow (Poiseuille flow) but also exotic non-diffusive phenomena like negative thermal resistance and vortices.

**Method.** The review rigorously connects several theoretical frameworks: fully quantum approaches (Dyson/Kadanoff-Baym equations), semiclassical methods (Boltzmann transport), and coarse-grained mesoscopic continuum models (viscous heat equations).

**Model / system.** The system is dielectric, non-magnetic crystals where heat is carried by quantized lattice vibrations (phonons). The theoretical modeling spans from the quantum Dyson equation down to macroscopic viscous heat equations.

**Key observables.** Heat flux, local temperature profile, thermal conductivity tensor, and signatures of non-diffusive flow like vortices or backflow.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature gradient ($
abla T$), length scales (mesoscopic/nanoscale), and the transition between quantum and classical transport regimes.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary approximations involve coarse-graining procedures to derive continuum equations, and treating phonon interactions within specific theoretical limits (e.g., linear response).

**Paper structure.** The paper follows a progression from fully quantum descriptions (Dyson/Kadanoff-Baym) through semiclassical approximations (Boltzmann equation) to the derivation and analysis of mesoscopic viscous heat equations, concluding with generalizations for other quasiparticles.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Thermal transport in dielectric, non-magnetic crystals is mediated by quantized lattice vibrations, which drift and interact when driven out of equilibrium by a temperature gradient. This phenomenon can be described at multiple theoretical levels, ranging from fully quantum descriptions to semiclassical and mesoscopic continuum approaches. This review rigorously discusses the theoretical steps and approximations connecting these levels, bridging quantum phonon Dyson and Kadanoff-Baym equations and semiclassical Boltzmann transport formalism, and discussing the coarse-graining procedures that yield mesoscopic viscous heat equations for non-diffusive, hydrodynamic heat transport in devices. We show how the Guyer-Krumhansl and dual-phase-lag equations emerge as special linear-isotropic-band and inviscid limits of the viscous heat equations, respectively; most importantly, we demonstrate that these equations predict not only Poiseuille flow and second sound, but also more exotic effects such as negative thermal resistance, steady-state thermal backflow and vortices. We highlight how combining these frameworks with first-principles simulations connects microscopic phonon physics to observable non-diffusive heat-transport phenomena and guides their detection, amplification, and control. We recast the viscous heat equations in terms of Helmholtz and biharmonic equations solved analytically, and use this to discuss similarities and differences between the macroscopic behavior of the phonon fluid and other hydrodynamic systems, such as classical and electron fluids, focusing on compressibility, vorticity, and their influence on phonon hydrodynamics. We conclude with a roadmap to generalize the tools used to describe phonon hydrodynamics to other quasiparticles, motivating future advances in collective quantum transport phenomena in solids.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces a differential Full Counting Statistics protocol to analyze charge transport in mesoscopic conductors. It moves beyond measuring bulk divergence by comparing terminal counting statistics against a model reference state. This methodology allows for the robust extraction of physical information, such as sub-Poissonian noise signatures, even when absolute measurements are difficult.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it develops advanced diagnostic tools for open quantum systems by focusing on relative changes (differential noise) rather than absolute quantities, which is crucial when dealing with complex boundary conditions in mesoscopic physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The authors aim to develop a robust measurement-domain language within Full Counting Statistics (FCS) to distinguish between bulk divergence measurements and open terminal quantities in mesoscopic charge transport.

**Main result.** They propose that the key diagnostic is comparing measured terminal counting statistics ($\chi_{\Gamma}$) against a model reference ($\chi_{ref}$), rather than relying on inferring the absolute bulk divergence from a single measurement. The framework successfully predicts sub-Poissonian Fano factors in topological systems.

**Method.** The study utilizes Levitov's FCS formalism, extending it to differential protocols that compare noise cumulants across different gate configurations at matched parameters.

**Model / system.** The theoretical analysis is applied to mesoscopic conductors, including quantum point contacts and topological systems modeled using the Dirac field. Microscopic calculations are performed using Non-Equilibrium Green's Functions (NEGF) on lattice structures.

**Key observables.** Fano factors ($F$), terminal counting statistics ($\chi_{\Gamma}$), bulk flux functionals ($\chi_{div}$), and the differential excess quantity ($\Delta\chi_{hidden}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Low temperature, specific gate configurations at matched $|\kappa_1|$, and operating bias voltages.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The identity $\chi_{\partial V} = \chi_{div}$ is treated as a consistency check under current conservation, not a universal theorem for all chiral edge systems.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare Fano factors derived from various theoretical models (e.g., 2D lattice NEGF vs. QPC calibration) and illustrate the comparison between open-terminal ($\chi_\Gamma$) and closed-boundary ($\chi_{div}$) excess quantities using different mathematical methods.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the need for a measurement language in FCS, details the differential protocol comparing terminal measurements to a reference state, provides theoretical derivations linking these observables, and validates the approach using NEGF calculations on lattice models.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We propose a differential full counting statistics protocol for mesoscopic transport. Additionally, we compare terminal Fano factors and noise cumulants between gate configurations at matched k1, instead of inferring a bulk divergence sensor from a single absolute F. it is illustrated analytically for a two channel factorization via a zero temperature geometry scan. Secondary benchmarks show that a two dimensional lattice non equilibrium Greens function calculation yields sub Poissonian Fano factors, whereas Kumars low temperature quantum point contact calibration validates the numerical implementation.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper analyzes the coupling between the Higgs mode and the Bardasis–Schrieffer exciton in superconductors with competing pairing symmetries. Using a quasiclassical approach, it finds that while symmetry forbids mixing at zero momentum, finite momentum allows coupling. Crucially, the ability to observe a resonance crossing depends critically on whether the system is clean or disordered.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant because it tackles collective excitations and symmetry breaking in unconventional superconductors, which are central topics in strongly correlated electron physics, often requiring advanced many-body techniques similar to those used in AMO or quantum optics for complex interacting systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the finite-momentum coupling between two distinct collective excitations—the Higgs mode (from $s$-wave pairing) and the Bardasis–Schrieffer (BS) exciton (from $d$-wave pairing)—in superconductors with competing pairing channels.

**Main result.** In the clean limit, kinematic differences cause the Higgs and BS branches to separate rather than cross. However, in the dirty limit, disorder can detach the resonance from the edge, potentially allowing for an avoided crossing set by the coupling strength $\Pi_{sd}(q, \Omega)$.

**Method.** The analysis employs a Nambu–Keldysh quasiclassical framework extended to leading $1/\epsilon_F$ corrections and incorporates self-consistently screened Coulomb potential effects.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled as a superconductor exhibiting competing pairing channels ($s$-wave and $d$-wave). The theoretical machinery involves analyzing coupled order-parameter fluctuations within the quasiclassical framework.

**Key observables.** Higgs mode dispersion, BS exciton dispersion, the coupling function $\Pi_{sd}(q, \Omega)$, and spectral weight distribution in amplitude/phase channels.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Momentum ($q$), frequency ($\Omega$), clean limit vs. dirty limit (disorder strength), and the ratio of pairing gaps to Fermi energy ($\Delta/\epsilon_F$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis distinguishes sharply between the clean limit (where kinematics forbid crossing) and the dirty limit (where disorder modifies dispersion relations).

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the separation of branches in the clean limit due to differing momentum dispersions ($\alpha_{SH}=1$ vs. $\alpha_{BS} < 1$). Figure 2 schematically shows how moderate disorder can induce an avoided crossing.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the physical context and symmetry constraints (zero momentum). It then develops the theoretical framework using quasiclassical methods to calculate the coupling term in closed form. Finally, it analyzes the resulting dispersion relations by comparing the clean limit results against those modified by disorder effects.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

In superconductors with competing pairing channels, two well defined excitations exist below the pair-breaking edge: the Higgs mode of the condensed $s$-wave channel and the Bardasis--Schrieffer (BS) exciton of the subdominant $d$-wave channel. Their mixing is doubly forbidden --- by point-group symmetry at zero momentum and because the two reside in the amplitude and phase sectors of the order parameter respectively, by particle--hole symmetry at every momentum. Working in a Nambu--Keldysh quasiclassical framework extended to leading $1/\varepsilon_F$ corrections and including the self-consistently screened Coulomb potential, we show that finite momentum combined with particle--hole asymmetry generates a direct coupling which we obtain in closed form. Whether this coupling produces an avoided crossing is decided, however, not by its magnitude but by kinematics. In the clean limit the Higgs is not a sub-gap pole but a resonance pinned to the pair-breaking edge, which disperses with coefficient unity in $(v_Fq)^2$, while the bound BS mode disperses more slowly: the two branches therefore separate rather than converge and never become degenerate. The obstruction is specific to the clean limit: exact dirty-limit results show that disorder detaches the amplitude resonance from the edge and reverses its dispersion, which can result in an avoided crossing with the BS mode at intermediate scattering. In that regime, the coupling computed here would set the splitting between the hybridized branches. We discuss the experimental implications of these results.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a comprehensive model to explain the tunability of g-factors in Germanium spin qubits. By integrating finite element simulations of strain with quantum calculations, the authors show that device geometry induces inhomogeneous strain fields. This strain modulates the effective g-tensor, providing a direct link between mechanical stress and qubit performance.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete physical mechanism (strain coupling) that directly controls a critical parameter (g-factor) in solid-state qubits, which is highly relevant for designing robust quantum computing hardware.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to quantitatively explain large, gate-voltage dependent variations in the g-factor of Germanium quantum dots by linking these changes directly to strain effects.

**Main result.** The research demonstrates that device-induced inhomogeneous strain causes spatially varying g-tensors, and shifting the dot within this strain landscape via gate voltages explains the observed tunability.

**Method.** It combines finite-element simulations of thermal elasticity (to map strain) with quantum calculations of hole wavefunctions to determine the resulting g-tensor modulation.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a Germanium quantum dot spin qubit integrated into a multi-layered semiconductor structure ($	ext{Ge}_{0.8}	ext{Si}_{0.2}$, Ge, Al gates). The theoretical framework involves solving thermal elasticity equations and calculating the g-tensor correction based on strain components.

**Key observables.** Effective g-factor, strain tensor components ($\epsilon_{ij}$), displacement vector components ($\delta$), and hole probability density distribution ($ho$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Cryogenic operating temperature ($T=20 	ext{ K}$) versus room temperature assembly ($T_0 = 300 	ext{ K}$); Lamé parameters ($\lambda, \mu$) at low T.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The weak coupling assumption is used in the mechanical modeling, decoupling temperature and strain fields; small strain approximation is employed for the geometry.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate SEM images of the device structure, cross-sections showing simulated displacement and strain distributions (Fig. 2, Fig. 3), band gap variations (Fig. 5), and the resulting g-tensor correction components (Fig. 6).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the need for an explanation of g-factor tunability to deriving the necessary strain/stress equations using FEM principles, calculating the resultant strain map, and finally applying this map via quantum calculations to predict the observable g-tensor modulation.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The g-factor is a key parameter governing the behavior of semiconductor spin qubits, as it directly determines the qubit frequency and its sensitivity to electrical and magnetic noise. Recent experiments in germanium quantum dots have revealed large g-factor variations under small gate voltage changes, indicating a strong coupling between electrostatics and spin properties. Here, we present a quantitative explanation based on strain-induced g-tensor modulation. By combining finite-element simulations of inhomogeneous strain with quantum calculations of hole wavefunctions, we show that device-induced strain produces spatially varying g-tensors. Gate voltages shift the quantum dot within this landscape, leading to substantial changes in the effective g-factor. Our results may account for the experimentally observed tunability and highlight the importance of in-plane g-tensor variations. This work establishes a direct link between strain, electrostatic control, and qubit performance in germanium spin qubits.

</details>

**Summary.** This work simulates how cyclic protein binding/unbinding drives dynamic, oscillating patterns called 'blinking domains' on lipid membranes. Using an active Potts model, the authors show that these non-equilibrium dynamics create stable patterns even when thermal equilibrium predicts different structures. This highlights the rich pattern formation possible in driven soft matter systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on soft matter physics, the use of active Potts models and non-equilibrium statistical mechanics principles for pattern formation shares conceptual overlap with driven quantum many-body systems or open quantum systems exhibiting persistent non-equilibrium steady states.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates non-equilibrium membrane pattern formation driven by cyclic protein binding and unbinding events.

**Main result.** Cyclic binding/unbinding induces 'blinking domains' (BD) with oscillating sizes, a standing-wave pattern robustly observed even in tensionless membranes where equilibrium patterns fail.

**Method.** The research employs meshless membrane simulations coupled with an off-lattice active Potts model to track state changes and resulting membrane deformations.

**Model / system.** The system models lipid membranes decorated with proteins that can occupy multiple states (s=0, 1, 2), each imparting a different spontaneous curvature. Dynamics are governed by Metropolis Monte Carlo updates driven by chemical potential differences ($\Delta H = \Delta U - \mu_{\alphaeta}$).

**Key observables.** Blinking domain patterns, mean cluster sizes ($\chi_s$), membrane vertical span ($\langle z_{mb}^2 angle$), and the time fraction characterizing dynamic modes.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Chemical potentials ($\mu_{01}, \mu_{02}$), surface tension ($\gamma$), and bending rigidity ($\kappa_u/k_B T$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The model assumes specific values for bending rigidity typical of lipid membranes, and the analysis distinguishes between equilibrium (HD) and non-equilibrium (BD/FD) regimes.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate schematic protein states, hexagonal alignment at equilibrium, and a dynamic phase diagram mapping regions for homogeneous phases (E0, E1, E2) versus pattern formations (BD, HD, FD).

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the model by defining the Hamiltonian and dynamics, analyzes the system across varying chemical potentials and surface tensions to map out distinct dynamical phases, and highlights the unique characteristics of blinking domains compared to equilibrium states.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Nonequilibrium membrane pattern formation is studied using meshless membrane simulation. Bound proteins are considered to have two states that generate different membrane spontaneous curvatures. Protein binding and unbinding occur cyclically owing to chemical potential differences, as an off-lattice active Potts model. It is found that this cyclic binding/unbinding can induce blinking domains, with oscillating size: convex domains of the proteins with a higher spontaneous curvature grow, and subsequently, the proteins change to the other state with a lower spontaneous curvature, resulting in domain shrinkage. These processes repeat. In thermal equilibrium, hexagonal convex domains are formed by the competition between bending and surface tension energies, so that they are stably formed only under positive surface tension. However, blinking domains can form even in tensionless membranes.

</details>

**Authors:** Georgii Koniukov, Dmitry Nerukh  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.13413v1>

**Summary.** This paper addresses the long-standing problem of formulating dissipative dynamics using variational principles. The authors propose a sophisticated method utilizing complex contour integration and fractional calculus to correctly derive the equation of motion for frictionally damped oscillators. This successfully recovers the expected energy dissipation rate, providing a mathematically rigorous framework for non-conservative systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** While not directly about quantum information, the rigorous mathematical treatment of non-conservative forces (dissipation) within an action principle is fundamental to open quantum systems theory, where deriving correct dynamics from generalized Lagrangians is crucial.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is deriving the equation of motion for dissipative systems with velocity-proportional friction from a variational principle, as standard Lagrangian mechanics fails due to fractional time derivatives.

**Main result.** The authors successfully derive the expected energy dissipation law (dE/dt = -gamma * dot(q)^2) by employing a novel approach using complex contour integration within the action functional.

**Method.** The method involves extending variational principles using specialized fractional calculus, specifically introducing and manipulating the Contour Caputo Fractional Derivative ($	ext{CD}^{1/2}_{\Gamma\epsilon}$) in the Lagrangian formulation.

**Model / system.** The model is a one-dimensional particle of mass m moving in a potential U(x) subject to linear viscous friction ($F_{fr} = -\gamma \dot{x}$). The dynamics are analyzed via an action functional incorporating complex fractional terms.

**Key observables.** Energy dissipation rate (dE/dt), Hamiltonian H, and the generalized equation of motion for position q(t).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The friction coefficient $\gamma$, mass $m$, and the order of the derivative ($\alpha=1/2$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivation relies on complex variable techniques (contour integration) to handle irreversible energy loss, and assumes specific boundary conditions for variational principles.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the complex integration contour $\Gamma_\epsilon$ used in defining the specialized Caputo fractional derivative.

**Paper structure.** The paper reviews existing failures of standard fractional calculus approaches before introducing a novel framework. It constructs a modified Lagrangian and Hamiltonian using complex fractional derivatives, derives the equations of motion via variation, and finally computes dH/dt to recover the known energy dissipation law.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

It has been known for a long time that the equation of motion for dissipative linear dynamical systems with constant coefficients cannot be derived from the classical principle of stationary action because the term proportional to velocity in the equation of motion leads to the time derivative of order one half in the Lagrangian. Thus, approaches utilising fractional calculus have been used; however, they suffer from deficiencies both from mathematical and physical points of view. We here present our version of such fractional calculus based approach that provides correct Euler-Lagrange and, ultimately, the Hamilton equations, energy change of the moving body, and an attempt for a geometric interpretation of how energy dissipates.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces an 'acoustic tweezer' technique to deterministically manipulate individual magnetic skyrmions by exploiting magnetoelastic coupling with acoustic waves. By showing that confined longitudinal acoustic beams carry phonon spin, they generate a polarity-selective radiation force. This allows for the precise, non-destructive routing of topological magnetic textures on an chip.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work bridges concepts from acoustics/optics (tweezers) with condensed matter magnetism (skyrmions), providing a clear physical analogy for controlling quantum-like excitations in solid-state systems using external fields. The rigorous derivation of the radiation force component is particularly valuable for understanding non-conservative driving forces.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge addressed is developing a method for deterministic, single-particle trapping and routing of magnetic skyrmions, overcoming limitations in current ensemble-based manipulation techniques.

**Main result.** The authors propose an 'acoustic tweezer' mechanism that uses the phonon spin carried by acoustic beams to generate polarity-selective radiation forces, enabling high-precision, non-destructive control over individual skyrmions.

**Method.** The analysis involves deriving the magnetoelastic force ($\mathbf{F}_{	ext{mec}}$) from first principles, decomposing it into gradient and radiation components, and modeling the resulting dynamics using a generalized Thiele equation.

**Model / system.** The system consists of magnetic skyrmions interacting with spatially extended longitudinal acoustic beams. The interaction is mediated by magnetoelastic coupling, which generates an effective field whose chirality is locked to the acoustic phonon spin texture.

**Key observables.** Polarity-selective radiation force; time-averaged force ($\langle \mathbf{F}_{	ext{mec}} angle_T$); Rayleigh dissipation rate ($\langle\dot{\mathcal{U}}angle_T$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Acoustic wavelength ($\lambda$) comparable to skyrmion size ($R_{sk}$); the topological charge ($Q$) of the skyrmion.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on a one-way coupling scheme, assuming the skyrmion drift velocity is much lower than the speed of sound; it also uses approximations like linear response theory and boundary condition assumptions for force integration.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate that positive phonon spin creates an attractive trapping line (red) while negative spin creates a repulsive line (blue), demonstrating polarity-selective attraction toward regions of maximum energy dissipation.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from identifying the need for single-particle control, deriving the magnetoelastic force based on phonon spin angular momentum, establishing the radiation force mechanism via susceptibility analysis, and finally simulating deterministic trapping using intersecting beams to create reconfigurable points.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Current methods for driving magnetic skyrmions predominantly translate ensembles as a whole, lacking single-particle selectivity. Here, we propose an "acoustic tweezer" that deterministically traps and routes individual skyrmions using spatially extended acoustic beams. We reveal that spatially confined longitudinal waves carry nontrivial phonon spin, inducing a magnetoelastic field whose chirality is locked to the acoustic spin texture. This generates polarity-selective radiation forces, distinct from conservative gradient forces, that attract skyrmions to local phonon spin maxima. Intersecting orthogonal beams create reconfigurable attractive points for adiabatic, deterministic manipulation. Our global-field-local-interaction paradigm establishes a non-destructive, on-chip route for high-precision topological spintronics.

</details>

**Authors:** Nicola Maggiore

**Summary.** This paper analyzes the Casimir interaction and edge physics in Maxwell--Chern-Simons theory on a finite strip. Using advanced field theory techniques, it derives that the vacuum energy exhibits distinct asymptotic behaviors depending on whether the topological mass term (from the Chern-Simons part) is zero or non-zero. This work provides a rigorous framework for understanding how boundary topology modifies bulk quantum interactions.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on gauge theory, the explicit calculation of vacuum energy (Casimir effect) in a confined geometry provides a concrete example of how topological terms modify standard QFT results, which is relevant for understanding boundary effects in quantum systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates edge physics and the resulting Casimir interaction within Maxwell--Chern-Simons (MCS) theory defined on a strip geometry.

**Main result.** The analysis derives a closed determinant representation for the finite vacuum interaction energy, showing that the force transitions from a power-law decay in the pure Maxwell limit to an exponentially screened Yukawa potential when topological mass is present.

**Method.** The authors employ the Symanzik framework for quantum field theory with boundaries, deriving boundary conditions via variational principles and analyzing the resulting scattering determinant structure.

**Model / system.** The system is MCS theory on a strip $\mathbb{R}_{1,1} 	imes [0, h]$, characterized by both Maxwell and Chern-Simons terms. The bulk supports one massive physical helicity, while boundary physics involves two coupled $U(1)$ current algebras.

**Key observables.** The Casimir interaction energy ($E_{	ext{int}}$), reflection coefficients, and the structure of the secular determinant governing vacuum fluctuations.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Strip width ($h$), Chern-Simons coupling ($\kappa$), and the resulting topological mass ($m = \kappa g^2$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on retaining terms up to one tangential derivative in the boundary functional and assumes a specific 'edge-active' choice for boundary conditions.

**Paper structure.** The paper follows a structured derivation: establishing boundary equations from local functionals, deriving conserved currents via Ward identities, reducing the problem to physical modes, and finally calculating the interaction energy using scattering determinants.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study Maxwell--Chern--Simons theories on a strip $\mathcal M=\mathbb R^{1,1}\times[0,h]$ within the Symanzik framework for quantum field theory with boundaries. We add the most general local boundary functional within the quadratic one-tangential-derivative truncation adopted throughout the paper and derive the admissible boundary conditions from locality and the variational principle, with gauge symmetry implemented through boundary Ward identities. The strip supports two boundary $U(1)$ current algebras, with opposite levels fixed by the Chern--Simons coupling $κ$ and by boundary orientation, while edge velocities depend on local boundary couplings. They are equal and opposite on the flip-symmetric branch, which is the natural choice for a spatially symmetric strip. The same boundary data determine the reflection coefficients for bulk modes and yield a closed determinant (scattering) representation of the finite interaction energy. In the Maxwell limit ($κ=0$) the inter-edge interaction is long-ranged and produces a power-law Casimir force, whereas in Maxwell--Chern--Simons theory the topological mass $m=κg^2$ generates Yukawa suppression at large separations $mh\gg1$. The determinant form also gives analytic control of the asymptotic regimes and separates topological boundary data (levels and anomaly inflow) from dynamical bulk effects (dispersion, boundary mixing and Casimir interaction).

</details>

**Summary.** This work unifies the seemingly separate phenomena of ion migration and electron-phonon coupling in metal halide perovskites using an orbital hybridization descriptor, $R$. By showing that both processes are governed by the same underlying electronic structure parameter, the authors propose a generalized mechanism for designing soft semiconductors with optimized charge and ionic transport simultaneously.

**Why it may be interesting.** While not directly focused on quantum information, the rigorous theoretical coupling of lattice vibrations (phonons) to electronic structure parameters ($R$) provides a powerful model for understanding strongly coupled open quantum systems where environment interaction dictates transport properties.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research aims to develop a unified microscopic description linking lattice dynamics, electron-phonon coupling (EPC), and ion migration in metal halide perovskites.

**Main result.** A single orbital hybridization descriptor ($R$) was introduced to show that both ionic transport barriers and EPC strengths arise from the same underlying electronic structure framework. This suggests a cooperative mechanism for optimizing charge and ionic transport simultaneously.

**Method.** The study employs Density Functional Theory (DFT) calculations, utilizing an orbital hybridization descriptor derived from a dual-atom model to quantify how lattice displacements modulate electronic coupling.

**Model / system.** The physical system is Metal Halide Perovskites ($	ext{CsPbI}_3$, $	ext{CsSnI}_3$, and double perovskites). The theoretical framework uses an orbital hybridization descriptor $R = 2V / |\Delta E|$ to model the coupling between metal and halide orbitals.

**Key observables.** Electron-phonon coupling strength ($\Gamma_{e-	ext{ph}}$), ion migration barrier energies ($E_b$), and the orbital hybridization descriptor ($R$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The relative values of $R$ across different perovskite compositions (e.g., $	ext{Cs}_2	ext{AgBiBr}_6 > 	ext{Sn-I} > 	ext{Pb-I}$), and the dependence of $R$ on specific phonon modes (stretching vs. shearing).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the descriptor $R$ acts as a universal electronic parameter governing both lattice restoring forces (migration) and dielectric screening (EPC). Calculations are often performed on archetypal cubic phases.

**Figures summary.** Figures conceptually link the hybridization descriptor $R$ to phonon modes: low-frequency shearing modes govern ion migration, while high-frequency stretching modes govern carrier scattering via the Fröhlich interaction. Supplementary figures detail how $R$ changes under specific atomic displacements (shortening/elongating bonds).

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes a conceptual framework linking bonding to dynamics using $R$. It then systematically calculates and analyzes how perturbing bond lengths or angles modulates $R$, which in turn dictates both the ion migration barrier and the EPC strength across multiple perovskite types.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The remarkable optoelectronic properties of metal halide perovskites are closely linked to their unusually soft and polar chemical bonds that enable both strong electron-phonon interactions and ion migration. Yet these two defining characteristics have largely been treated as independent consequences of the same underlying chemical bonding. Here we show that they originate from a common electronic-structure framework by developing a general description linking lattice dynamics, electron-phonon coupling, and halide ion migration across representative Pb-based, Sn-based, and double perovskites. Spectrally resolved phonon-mode contributions demonstrate that the low-frequency shearing modes dominate halide migration, whereas high-frequency stretching modes govern carrier scattering through the Fröhlich interaction in all three compositions. We introduce an orbital hybridization descriptor to unify these findings, which connects metal-halide bonding characteristics with the migration barrier energies and Fröhlich coupling strengths, indicating a cooperative evolution of these two properties. These findings provide a generalized microscopic mechanism for simultaneously optimizing charge and ionic transport in soft semiconductors.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper explores inducing passive non-reciprocal scattering in textured image sensors by introducing structural asymmetry at sub-wavelength scales. Using antenna theory and FDTD simulations, the authors find that while theory predicts significant enhancement, actual measurements show this effect is suppressed by field concentration at sharp apices. Future designs must focus on asymmetric modifications directly at these high-field regions.

**Why it may be interesting.** Although focused on classical optics/photonics, the investigation into symmetry breaking to induce non-reciprocity at nanoscale boundaries touches upon concepts relevant to topological photonics or engineered quantum light-matter interactions where boundary conditions are paramount.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The work investigates whether structural asymmetry at sub-wavelength scales can induce passive non-reciprocal scattering in linear, time-invariant systems, moving beyond traditional macroscopic continuum assumptions.

**Main result.** While theory predicts a potential 5%-15% efficiency enhancement via symmetry breaking, numerical simulations reveal that intense local field concentration at the apex shields this effect, suggesting asymmetry must be engineered directly at the field-concentrating point to observe non-reciprocity.

**Method.** The study combines theoretical development using perturbation theory with detailed numerical simulations performed on the MEEP finite-difference time-domain (FDTD) platform.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a CMOS image sensor modified with an asymmetrically textured surface, inspired by bacterial photosynthetic light harvesters. The model analyzes wave scattering properties at the sub-wavelength scale using antenna theory principles.

**Key observables.** Non-reciprocal susceptibility ($\chi^{(2)}$), DC current ($\langle J(t) angle$), and relative change in average optical absorption (measured via FDTD flux data).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Sub-wavelength scales, eccentricity parameter ($\delta \approx 13.6\%$), and incidence angles ($	heta = 0^\circ$ to $30^\circ$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that macroscopic continuum models are invalid at the sub-wavelength scale, and that geometric configuration dictates the electromagnetic response.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare absorption performance for circular versus elliptical cone shapes across different lattice spacings and show optical absorption curves under oblique illumination angles ($	heta = 0^\circ$ to $30^\circ$).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the theoretical framework based on bio-inspiration and perturbation theory, through numerical validation using MEEP/FDTD simulations comparing various geometries (circular vs. elliptical), culminating in a discussion of the discrepancy between predicted enhancement and measured results.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

This work numerically validates the application of bio-inspired concepts on CMOS derived from bacterial photosynthetic light harvesters. We investigate a modification of symmetric inverted pyramid array CMOS image sensors into an asymmetrically shaped texture to explore the structural boundary of passive non-reciprocity. Diverging from traditional macroscopic continuum assumptions, we analyze whether structural asymmetry at sub-wavelength scales can induce non-reciprocal scattering under passive, linear, and time-invariant conditions. A theoretical framework based on perturbation theory is developed, estimating a potential efficiency enhancement of 5\% to 15\%. Numerical simulations performed via the MEEP finite-difference time-domain (FDTD) platform reveal that the linear response is highly localized, showing a subtle 0.02\% change. This suggests that macroscopic Lorentz reciprocity remains robust at the investigated scale due to apex field concentration, defining a clear geometric threshold for microscopic non-reciprocity.

</details>

**Summary.** This work investigates 'moiré magnetic chaos' in twisted bilayer CrI3, demonstrating that the inherent interlayer exchange frustration generates chaotic dynamics without external driving. Using micromagnetic simulations, the authors quantify this chaos via Lyapunov exponents and analyze the resulting domain patterns, showing they are statistically random yet governed by underlying topological constraints.

**Why it may be interesting.** The study bridges concepts from nonlinear dynamics (chaos theory) into condensed matter magnetism, providing a concrete physical realization of autonomous chaos in a solid-state system, which is highly relevant for understanding complex magnetic switching and memory effects.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To demonstrate a new type of magnetic chaos, termed 'moiré magnetic chaos,' arising from mesoscopic domain variables in twisted bilayer CrI3 without external driving.

**Main result.** The system exhibits intrinsic, undriven chaos characterized by extreme sensitivity to initial conditions and stochastic domain configurations due to interlayer exchange frustration. This identifies a microscopic mechanism for nonlinear magnetism beyond conventional driven regimes.

**Method.** Micromagnetic simulations are used extensively, employing techniques like calculating finite-time Lyapunov exponents, analyzing damage spreading from perturbations, and performing statistical ensemble characterization.

**Model / system.** The system is twisted bilayer CrI3, modeled by a Heisenberg spin Hamiltonian incorporating interlayer exchange coupling ($J_{	ext{inter}}$). The moiré superlattice structure creates regions of frustration (AFM patches) that provide multiple dynamical degrees of freedom necessary for autonomous chaos.

**Key observables.** Finite-time Lyapunov exponents ($\lambda$), Hamming Fraction ($H/N_d$), Shannon entropy ($\langle H_n angle$), and topological charge ($Q$) of reversed domains.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Twist angle ($	heta = 1.61^\circ$); characteristic energy gap $\Delta E/|E| \approx 1.8 	imes 10^{-4}$; Lyapunov exponent scales (e.g., $9 	ext{ ns}^{-1}$ vs. $290.8 	ext{ ns}^{-1}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes weak coupling between neighboring AFM patches, allowing for quasi-independent polarization selection in each patch; the chaotic behavior is understood as transient sensitivity rather than sustained attractor dynamics.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate interlayer exchange maps showing FM/AFM patterns (Fig. 1a), representative metastable domain configurations (Fig. 1b), damage spreading evolution ($\delta m(t)$) confirming chaos, and topological charge distributions classifying domains as skyrmion-like or bubble-like.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the physical platform (twisted CrI3) and the mechanism for frustration to quantifying chaos using Lyapunov exponents and perturbation analysis. It then characterizes the resulting domain states statistically (entropy, correlations) and topologically (skyrmions vs. bubbles), concluding with a refined understanding of transient versus sustained chaotic dynamics.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The study of magnetic chaos has traditionally focused on macroscopic variables under external driving. Here we demonstrate a new type of magnetic chaos, termed moiré magnetic chaos, associated with mesoscopic magnetic domain variables in twisted bilayer CrI3 without external driving. The domains are stabilized by a characteristic interlayer exchange frustration, which supplies the multiple dynamical degrees of freedom required for autonomous chaos. Through micromagnetic simulations, we show that relaxation toward moiré magnetic textures is extremely sensitive to minute local perturbations of the initial state, characterized by substantial finite-time Lyapunov exponents and a final-state sensitivity that persists over five decades of perturbation amplitude. Statistical analysis further reveals that the resulting domain configurations are stochastic and pairwise uncorrelated. Our results identify a form of microscopic, undriven chaos in twisted magnets that extends nonlinear magnetism beyond the conventional driven regime.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces Adiabatic Perturbation Theory (APT) to accurately model energy and charge transfer in electron-ion systems. It systematically derives corrections beyond the standard Born-Oppenheimer approximation by treating the small parameter $\epsilon$ (electron-to-nucleus mass ratio) perturbatively. The resulting framework allows for calculating advanced transport properties like nonadiabatic conductivity with controlled accuracy.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides rigorous, high-order theoretical tools for treating nonadiabatic coupling effects in condensed matter systems, which is crucial for understanding energy and charge flow where electronic and nuclear motions are strongly coupled.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is quantifying energy transfer and charge transport in condensed matter systems with controlled accuracy concerning the adiabatic small parameter ($\epsilon$, the electron-to-nucleus mass ratio). This requires going beyond standard approximations like the harmonic approximation.

**Main result.** The authors develop Adiabatic Perturbation Theory (APT) to systematically derive effective Hamiltonians and calculate nonadiabatic corrections to transport properties, such as frequency-dependent conductivity and Drude weight, to any desired order in $\epsilon$.

**Method.** The primary method is Adiabatic Perturbation Theory (APT), which treats the electron-ion Schrödinger equation as a singularly perturbed differential equation in $\epsilon$ by employing near-identity unitary transformations to decouple electronic and nuclear degrees of freedom.

**Model / system.** The model is an electron-ion system described by a full nonrelativistic Hamiltonian. The theory analyzes how coupling between fast electronic motion and slow, heavy ionic motion (nuclei) affects transport properties.

**Key observables.** Rate of change of the nuclear kinetic energy (energy transfer formula), nonadiabatic frequency-dependent conductivity, and Drude weight.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The adiabatic small parameter $\epsilon$ (electron-to-nucleus mass ratio); the bare nuclear mass tensor $M_0$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The theory systematically improves upon approximations by controlling errors in powers of $\epsilon$, avoiding assumptions inherent to the harmonic approximation or standard Born-Oppenheimer treatments.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from identifying the failure points of simpler models (like the Harmonic Approximation) to introducing APT as a systematic, order-by-order expansion in $\epsilon$ via unitary transformations to derive an effective Hamiltonian for transport calculations.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Energy and charge transfer in condensed matter systems are often described in terms of quasiparticles. The order of accuracy of the results in terms of the electron-to-nucleus mass ratio $ε$, an adiabatic small parameter, is usually not known. To quantify energy transfer with controlled accuracy in $ε$, we use adiabatic perturbation theory to derive a formula for the rate of change of the nuclear kinetic energy. Applying a sequence of near-identity unitary transformations decouples the electronic and nuclear degrees of freedom to higher and higher order and produces an effective Hamiltonian. In the special case that the nuclei are treated classically, the energy transfer formula reduces to the usual formula for the rate of work done on a classical particle but the mass of the particle is enhanced by the factor $M_0^{-1}(M_0+εM_1)$, where $M_1$ is a nonadiabatic correction to the bare nuclear mass tensor $M_0$. Additional quantum geometric corrections appear at higher orders. Adiabatic perturbation theory is further used to incorporate, order-by-order in $ε$, nonadiabatic transitions into linear response calculations. The nonadiabatic frequency-dependent conductivity and Drude weight of an electron-ion system are calculated.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper reports the discovery of 'trans-moiré orbitals' in rhombohedral graphene/hBN moiré superlattices. These orbitals emerge on the side distant from the primary moiré interface and are responsible for stabilizing topological features like the FQAHE. The findings resolve a long-standing paradox by showing that electron interactions force the system into these specific, topologically active orbital states.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses emergent topological phases in moiré systems using spectroscopic measurements, which is highly relevant for understanding correlated electron behavior in engineered quantum materials.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to resolve the microscopic mechanism behind the Fractional Quantum Anomalous Hall Effect (FQAHE) in rhombohedral graphene moiré superlattices, specifically addressing the paradox of observing a small-twist-angle interface only when electrons are kept distant from it.

**Main result.** The authors discovered emergent 'trans-moiré orbitals' on the moiré-distant surface, which enforce moiré periodicity and vanish at critical twist angles ($	heta \sim 1^\circ$), providing a microscopic explanation for the topological effects observed.

**Method.** The research combines Scanning Tunneling Microscopy/Spectroscopy (STM/STS) measurements with self-consistent mean-field calculations to map out the electronic structure reshaping.

**Model / system.** The physical system is rhombohedral hexalayer graphene ($	ext{R6G}$) stacked on hexagonal boron nitride ($	ext{hBN}$), forming a moiré superlattice. The electronic properties are tuned by gate voltage and controlled by the moiré twist angle ($	heta$).

**Key observables.** Trans-moiré orbitals (e.g., hollow-cage shape), flat-band renormalization strength, Chern minibands, and quantized Hall plateaus.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Moiré twist angle ($	heta$), filling factor ($v$), and gate voltage ($V_{	ext{bg}}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The mechanism is attributed to an interaction-driven charge-redistribution across layers, forcing electrons into these topological orbitals.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate STM topographies and dI/dV spectra showing moiré renormalization on the distant surface for small $	heta$, contrasting with null results at large $	heta$. Simulations map these emergent orbitals to theoretical Chern minibands.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the problem (FQAHE mechanism), presents experimental evidence via STM mapping of trans-moiré orbitals under specific twist angles, explains this emergence through interaction-driven charge redistribution, and concludes by linking this finding to a general platform for synthetic FQAHE realization.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The fractional quantum anomalous Hall effect (FQAHE) exhibited in fractional Chern insulators has recently been demonstrated in twisted MoTe2 and rhombohedral graphene/hBN moiré superlattices, promising new routes toward topological quantum computation. Central to realizing this promise is the understanding of the underlying microscopic mechanism. This, however, remains elusive in the case of rhombohedral graphene, with the crux being its two seemingly paradoxical conditions: a pronounced small-twist-angle (θ) moiré interface, yet only when electrons are kept distant from it. Here, by scanning tunnelling microscopic imaging with both conditions fulfilled, we capture dramatic electronic structure reshaping in rhombohedral hexalayer graphene by unforeseen 'trans-moiré orbitals', which emerge on the other, distant side of the moiré interface but nevertheless enforce the moiré periodicity at all measured fillings. We visualize a hierarchy of spatially and energetically distinct trans-moiré orbitals which doped electrons must sequentially occupy--the lowest-energy orbital, expectedly responsible for the FQAHE at small fillings, carries a hollow-cage-like shape. Remarkably, these trans-moiré orbitals vanish at θ {\gtrsim} 1°, and so do QAHE plateaus in similar devices. Simulations reveal an interaction-driven charge-redistribution mechanism which shapes the trans-moiré orbitals and corresponding Chern minibands. With our findings providing the missing microscopic link, the paradoxical conditions find a natural explanation: electrons are not simply kept distant from a small-θ moiré interface; they are forced into topological trans-moiré orbitals, forged precisely under such conditions. Our microscopic diagnostics unlocks a wide range of possible 'synthetic' FQAHE platforms.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes pulse propagation in nonparaxial optical media by reducing the governing equation to a Hamiltonian system. It demonstrates that incorporating non-Kerr nonlinearities (Self Steepening and Self Frequency Shift) fundamentally alters the phase space structure of the traveling waves. Furthermore, it characterizes the transition from regular to chaotic wave dynamics when the system is subjected to external periodic forcing.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work applies advanced dynamical systems tools (Hamiltonian mechanics, bifurcation theory) to model complex wave phenomena in nonlinear optics, providing a rigorous mathematical framework for understanding how higher-order physical effects alter stable wave solutions.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how non-Kerr nonlinear effects, specifically self steepening (SS) and self frequency shift (SFS), fundamentally reorganize the phase space geometry and traveling wave structure in nonparaxial optical media.

**Main result.** These non-Kerr effects reshape the underlying Hamiltonian dynamics; SS directly renormalizes the effective nonlinearity, while SFS constrains the admissible real travelling wave manifold. The system can also exhibit complex quasiperiodic or chaotic responses under external forcing.

**Method.** The analysis involves reducing the nonlinear Helmholtz equation via a traveling wave ansatz to a planar Hamiltonian dynamical system. This is followed by phase space analysis, bifurcation theory for forced systems, and chaos diagnostics like Lyapunov exponents.

**Model / system.** The model is based on the Nonlinear Helmholtz Equation (NLH) describing pulse propagation in nonparaxial optical media. The dynamics are reduced to a conservative Hamiltonian $H(V, Q)$ governing the envelope amplitude $V$ and its derivative $Q$.

**Key observables.** Localized/periodic wave existence, phase space geometry (separatrices, equilibria), Largest Lyapunov Exponent ($\Lambda_{\max}$), and Poincaré section structure.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The effective nonlinear coefficients $\alpha$ and $eta$, which depend on physical parameters like dispersion, nonparaxiality, Kerr nonlinearity, SS, and SFS terms.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on applying a symmetry-guided traveling wave ansatz to reduce the NLH equation to a planar Hamiltonian system. The study also examines periodically forced extensions of this reduced system.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate parameter space diagrams showing how varying physical coefficients determine the signs of the effective nonlinear coefficients $\alpha$ and $eta$, which govern the phase plane topology.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the reduction to a Hamiltonian system using traveling wave transformations. It then analyzes the resulting planar dynamics via equilibrium point classification and phase space analysis, detailing how SS and SFS modify the conserved Hamiltonian structure. Finally, it extends this analysis to periodically forced systems, employing Lyapunov exponents and Poincaré sections to map out transitions to chaos.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

In this article, the nonlinear Helmholtz equation with non-Kerr nonlinearity, such as self steepening and self frequency shift, is considered. A travelling wave transformation is applied, and the extended nonlinear Helmholtz equation is reduced to a Hamiltonian dynamical system. Then, the reduced Hamiltonian system is analyzed by classification of equilibrium points, phase space analysis, and the construction of exact wave solutions. The relationship between the reduced dynamical coefficients and the original physical parameters is further established through a parameter space analysis. It is shown that self steepening directly modifies the reduced dynamics, whereas self frequency shift acts through the compatibility condition for the real travelling wave reduction. Together, these non-Kerr effects reshape the phase space geometry and travelling wave structure. Localized and periodic travelling waves are obtained, with their existence determined by the balance among dispersion, nonparaxiality, Kerr nonlinearity, and non-Kerr effects. Furthermore, a periodically forced version of the reduced system is examined to study the transition from regular to irregular dynamics. It has been observed that external forcing can induce complex oscillatory behavior. Bifurcation analysis, time series evolution, phase space analysis, largest Lyapunov exponent, and Poincaré section demonstrate the emergence of quasiperiodic and chaotic responses under sufficiently strong forcing. All analytical branches are verified through full-equation residual evaluation, while a few selected branches are additionally examined through direct numerical propagation and robustness tests under complex Gaussian perturbations. The results show that self steepening directly renormalizes the effective nonlinear dynamics, whereas self frequency shift restricts the admissible real-envelope travelling wave manifold.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper theoretically explores engineering topological superconductors in doubly-coupled nanowires that combine proximity superconductivity and altermagnetism. By tuning parameters like the superconducting phase difference ($\varphi$) and magnetic couplings ($J_{1,2}$), the authors demonstrate the realization of multiple robust TSC phases, including those hosting protected Majorana zero modes. This work provides a versatile platform for designing topological quantum materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete theoretical blueprint for realizing exotic superconducting states by combining magnetic order (altermagnetism) with superconductivity in low-dimensional structures, which is highly relevant to condensed matter theory involving symmetry protection.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study theoretically investigates how topological superconductivity (TSC) can be engineered in a novel platform: doubly-coupled nanowires that integrate proximity-induced superconductivity and altermagnetism.

**Main result.** The system was shown to host four distinct TSC phases, including classes D and BDI, with the ability to realize robust Majorana zero modes protected by specific spin-group or magnetic point-group symmetries.

**Method.** The analysis uses a tight-binding model Hamiltonian solved via Bogoliubov–de Gennes formalism in momentum space, characterizing topological states using symmetry classes and winding numbers.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a doubly-coupled semiconducting nanowire platform incorporating superconducting (SC) regions and altermagnetic (AM) regions. The model Hamiltonian $H = H_{w1} + H_{w2} + H_{int}$ describes the coupled two-band superconductor.

**Key observables.** Topological phase transitions marked by bulk gap closing, the presence of Majorana zero modes (MZMs) at boundaries, and the topological invariant Z.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Tunable parameters include the superconducting phase difference ($\varphi$), inter-wire coupling ($t_0$), chemical potential ($\mu$), and altermagnetism strengths ($J_{1,2}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes proximity coupling to superconductors and altermagnets, and relies on controlling multiple symmetry parameters.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate schematics of the coupled nanowire platform, energy eigenvalues showing zero-energy states (MZMs), and phase diagrams mapping out topological regimes based on $\varphi$ and $J_{1,2}$.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from identifying the physical challenge to deriving the Hamiltonian, analyzing how symmetry dictates the topological class (D vs BDI), and finally detailing the tunability of these phases using specific parameter controls.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We theoretically investigate the possibility of engineering topological superconductivity in doubly coupled nanowires integrating proximity-induced superconductivity and altermagnetism. By tuning experimentally accessible parameters, we find four distinct topological superconducting phases: class D, class BDI, and two phases hosting two Majorana zero modes per end, protected respectively by spin-group and magnetic point-group symmetries. Beyond inheriting the advantages of alternating?magnetism induced topological superconductivity, our system provides multiple tuning knobs (e.g., superconducting phase difference and inter-wire coupling) to control topological properties.

</details>

**Summary.** The research simulates how oxygen diffusing into Niobium's surface layer during vacuum annealing affects its superconducting properties. By solving reaction-diffusion equations, the authors predict that this doping profile alters the magnetic screening current distribution. This establishes a quantitative framework linking controlled surface chemistry to the operational performance of critical SRF cavity components.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a crucial quantitative bridge between solid-state chemistry (defect diffusion) and applied superconductivity physics relevant for accelerator technology, which is highly valuable for understanding material limitations in quantum hardware components.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to quantitatively link oxygen doping, resulting from surface oxide dissolution and diffusion in Niobium (Nb), to the superconducting performance of Nb Superconducting Radio Frequency (SRF) cavities.

**Main result.** Oxygen doping significantly modifies the near-surface current distribution by reducing the surface screening current and shifting its maximum several nanometers into the bulk material.

**Method.** The study uses numerical simulation of a 1D reaction-diffusion process, solving for oxygen concentration profiles over time and temperature ranges. These profiles are then used to calculate resulting superconducting properties like the Meissner screening current and critical supercurrent density.

**Model / system.** The physical system is Nb metal undergoing chemical doping by interstitial oxygen during vacuum annealing treatments. The modeling involves a reaction-diffusion equation coupled with electrodynamics (London/Ginzburg-Landau theory) to predict macroscopic superconducting response from microscopic defect profiles.

**Key observables.** Oxygen concentration profile [O](x, t), Meissner screening current distribution J(x), magnetic penetration depth $\lambda(T, x, B_0)$, and critical supercurrent density $J_c$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Treatment temperatures ($50^\circ	ext{C}$ to $200^\circ	ext{C}$) and times ($0.5 	ext{h}$ to $120 	ext{h}$). The diffusion coefficient $D$ and rate constants follow Arrhenius dependencies.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The dissolution process is treated as irreversible, step-wise reactions, and the oxygen source term for diffusion is approximated as a localized surface plane source.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate simulated profiles of $[	ext{O}](x)$, $\ell$, $B(x)$, and $J(x)$ across various annealing times and temperatures, comparing results to idealized 'clean' and 'dirty' limits.

**Paper structure.** The paper follows a logical progression: first modeling the chemical process (dissolution via rate equations), then simulating the resulting diffusion profile using reaction-diffusion equations, and finally calculating the macroscopic electromagnetic response (Meissner screening/supercurrent) based on that profile.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We report simulations of the dissolution and diffusion of Nb's surface oxide layer in vacuum. While this chemical doping process is important for the surface preparation of Nb superconducting radio frequency (SRF) cavities - common components of particle accelerators - quantitatively linking the resulting oxygen distributions to superconducting performance remains challenging. In this work, we simulate the reaction-diffusion process numerically for treatment temperatures $T = 50^{\circ}$C to $200^{\circ}$C and times $t = 0.5$ h to $120$ h, and calculate the effect of the spatially inhomogeneous oxygen doping on Nb's superconducting properties. We find that oxygen doping redistributes the Meissner screening current, reducing its value at the surface and shifting its maximum several nanometres into the material. These results provide a microscopic link between oxygen diffusion profiles and the electromagnetic response of Nb relevant for SRF cavity operation. This work provides a quantitative framework linking oxygen diffusion profiles to superconducting performance and establishes a foundation for future studies involving time-dependent and multi-step heat treatment protocols.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper investigates dromion solutions for a generalized nonlinear Schrödinger equation in two spatial dimensions, utilizing the Painlevé expansion method. It uncovers several novel, universal attributes of these localized structures, such as boundary reflections and non-uniform energy sharing among multiple interacting solitons. These findings suggest deep underlying physics applicable to fields like nonlinear optics and plasma dynamics.

**Why it may be interesting.** The study's focus on robust, universal physical signatures (like firewalls or non-uniform energy sharing) in integrable PDEs is highly relevant to theoretical physics exploring complex wave phenomena in optics and plasma physics. The connection to soliton interactions provides a rich mathematical framework for modeling many-body quantum dynamics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to explore novel properties of dromion solutions by investigating a generalized (2+1) dimensional Nonlinear Schrödinger Equation (GNLS). It seeks to uncover attributes beyond those previously documented for these localized wave structures.

**Main result.** The study reveals universal properties of dromions, including the existence of 'firewall' and reflection at boundaries, uneven energy distribution among bound states, and amplitude dependence on neighboring dromions. These effects are confirmed across multiple nonlinear systems.

**Method.** The primary mathematical tool employed is the Truncated Painlevé Expansion method to derive closed-form solutions for the complex partial differential equation. Numerical simulations were used extensively to corroborate analytical findings.

**Model / system.** The core model is a generalized (2+1) dimensional Nonlinear Schrödinger Equation, which integrates aspects of Davey-Stewartson equations. The analysis focuses on finding and characterizing dromion solutions within this framework.

**Key observables.** Dromion density profile ($|q|^2$), energy distribution among bound states, phase shifts during collision, and amplitude changes upon removal of neighboring components.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The coupling constant $\lambda$, and parameters controlling the functional forms derived from the Painlevé analysis ($\phi_1, \phi_2$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method relies on the assumption that the system can be analyzed via the Truncated Painlevé approach, which is limited to integrable (2+1) dimensional PDEs.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate snapshots and density profiles confirming inelastic collisions, firewall effects, and reflection for two dromions. Other figures demonstrate how removing specific dromion components causes a measurable decrease in the overall cluster amplitude, suggesting nonlinear coupling.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the generalized GNLS equation and applies the Painlevé approach to derive general solution forms. It then analyzes multi-dromion solutions (two-dromion, seven-dromion) by setting specific functional forms for the components. Finally, it systematically demonstrates the universal nature of observed properties—such as energy distribution control and amplitude dependence—by comparing results across various nonlinear systems.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

In this paper, we travel through an unknown territory of dromions, to unearth and show the new properties/attributes of dromions which have never been brought to the fore since they were first discovered by Boiti etal [1]. These new attributes are brought out by investigating a generalized (2+1) dimensional Nonlinear Schrodinger (NLS) equation by exploiting Truncated Painleve approach. The signatures that can be attributed to dromions include existence of firewall and reflection at the boundary, uneven distribution of energy among different bound states, amplitude dependence on adjacent dromions, etc. We have corroborated the main analytical results with numerical simulations also. We categorically state that these properties are universal and can be extracted in any (2+1) dimensional nonlinear partial differential equation. We do believe that these properties which shed more light on the behaviour of dromions may have wider repercussions in nonlinear optics, Bose-Einstein condensates and plasma physics.

</details>
